\langle u\hskip 0.56917pt,v\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle x}=\mathrm{B}(\xi\hskip 0.56917pt,\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v})
whenever
u=\xi\cdot x
.

Proposition 1.10
.

In the notation of the previous proposition,

\mathrm{Exp}_{x}(v)=\exp(\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v})\cdot x

(1.122)

for
x\in M
and
v\in T_{x}M
.

Propositions
1.9
and
1.10
offer a straightforward computational route to the Riemannian exponential map
\mathrm{Exp}
. To compute
\mathrm{Exp}_{x}(v)
, one begins by “lifting”
v
from
T_{x}M
to
\mathfrak{g}
, under the form of
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v\,}
. Then, it is enough to compute the action of
\exp(\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v})
, which is just a matrix exponential, in practice.

Example 1 :
consider an example of the non-compact case,
M=\mathrm{H}(N)
, the space of
N\times N
Hermitian positive-definite matrices. Here,
G=\mathrm{GL}(N,\mathbb{C})
and
\pi(g)=gg^{\dagger}
for
g\in G
. Then,

d\pi(g)\cdot h=h\hskip 0.56917ptg^{\dagger}+g\hskip 0.56917pth^{\dagger}

for
h\in T_{g}G
. For
x=\pi(g)
and
v\in T_{x}M
, it follows that
v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(g)=\left.v\hskip 0.85355pt\theta(g)\middle/2\right.
, where
\theta(g)=(g^{\dagger})^{-1}
. By definition,
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}=dR_{\scriptscriptstyle g^{-1}}(v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(g))
. Since
gg^{\dagger}=x
, this gives
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}=(v/2)\hskip 0.85355ptx^{-1}
. Therefore, using the fact that
g\cdot x=g\hskip 0.56917ptx\hskip 0.56917ptg^{\dagger}
, it follows

\mathrm{Exp}_{x}(v)=\exp\left(v\hskip 0.85355ptx^{-1}\!\middle/2\right)\,x\,\exp\left(x^{-1}\hskip 0.85355ptv\middle/2\right)

Accordingly, by an elementary property of the matrix exponential
9
9

9

Matrix functions (powers, logarithms,
etc.
) of Hermitian arguments should be understood as Hermitian matrix functions, obtained using the spectral decomposition — see
[
20
]
.
,

\mathrm{Exp}_{x}(v)=x^{\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left(x^{-\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 1.13791ptv\hskip 1.13791ptx^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)x^{\frac{1}{2}}

(1.123)

which is the formula made popular by
[
21
]
.

Example 2 :
let
M=G/K
be a Riemannian symmetric space of the compact case. That is, the scalar product
Q
on
\mathfrak{g}
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant. Write
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}+\mathfrak{p}
the Cartan decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}
. For
x\in M
, denote
K_{x}
the stabiliser of
x
in
G
. If
x=\pi(g)
, this has Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}_{x}=\mathrm{Ad}(g)(\mathfrak{k})
(that is, the image under
\mathrm{Ad}(g)
of
\mathfrak{k}
). For
v\in T_{x}M
, by Proposition
1.9
, its “lift”
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}
should verify (note that, for the present example,
\mathrm{B}=Q
)

\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}\cdot x=v\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359ptQ(\xi\hskip 0.56917pt,\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v})=0\text{ for }\xi\in\mathfrak{k}_{x}

where the second identity is because
\xi\cdot x=0
for
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}_{x}\,
. Because
Q
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant, this second identity is equivalent to

Q(\kappa\hskip 0.56917pt,\mathrm{Ad}(g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})(\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}))=0
for
\kappa\in\mathfrak{k}
. That is,
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}=\mathrm{Ad}(g)(\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}(o))
for some
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}(o)\in\mathfrak{p}
. This
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}(o)
is determined from
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}\cdot x=v
, which yields
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}(o)\cdot o=g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\cdot v
. However, the map
\omega\mapsto\omega\cdot o
is an isomorphism from
\mathfrak{p}
onto
T_{o}M
. Denoting its inverse by
\pi_{o}:T_{o}M\rightarrow\mathfrak{p}
, it follows that
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}(o)=\pi_{o}(g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\cdot v)
. Finally,

\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\left(\pi_{o}(g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\cdot v)\right)

(1.124)

A special case of this formula was used in (
1.32
) of
1.4
.

Proof of Proposition
1.9
:
to begin, one must prove

\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}\cdot x=v

(1.125)

From the definition of
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}
and
v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(e)
, it is clear
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}=dR_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}}\hskip 0.56917pt(v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(g))
. Replacing this into (
1.121
), the left-hand side of (
1.125
) becomes,

\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}\cdot x=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\exp(t\,dR_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}}\hskip 0.85355ptv^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(g))\cdot x=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\left(\gamma(t)\hskip 1.13791ptg^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\right)\cdot x

where
\gamma
is any curve in
G
, through
g
with
\dot{\gamma}(0)=v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(g)
. Therefore,

\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v}\cdot x=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\gamma(t)\cdot o=d\pi\left(v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(g)\right)=v

from the definition of
v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(g)
. This proves (
1.125
). It remains to show,

\langle u\hskip 0.56917pt,v\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle x}=Q(\xi\hskip 0.56917pt,\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{for }u=\xi\cdot x

(1.126)

The proof is separated into two cases.

non-compact case :
in this case,
Q(\xi\hskip 0.56917pt,\omega)=-B(\xi,d\hskip 0.56917pt\theta(\omega))
, where
B
is an
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant, non-degenerate, symmetric bilinear form. To prove (
1.126
), note that

d\pi(g)\left(dR_{\scriptscriptstyle g}(\xi)\right)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\left(\exp(t\xi)\,g\right)\cdot o=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\exp(t\xi)\cdot x=u

Therefore,
dR_{\scriptscriptstyle g}(\xi)=u^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(g)+w
where
w\in V_{g}
. From (
1.120
), using left-invariance of
(\cdot,\cdot)
,

\langle u\hskip 0.56917pt,v\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle x}\,=\,\left(dR_{\scriptscriptstyle g}(\xi)\hskip 0.56917pt,v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(g)\right)_{\scriptscriptstyle g}\,=\,Q\left(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})(\xi)\hskip 0.56917pt,v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(e)\right)

(1.127)

Thus, using the definition of
Q
, and the fact that
v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(e)\in\mathfrak{p}
,

\langle u\hskip 0.56917pt,v\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle x}=-B(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})(\xi),d\hskip 0.56917pt\theta(v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(e)))=B(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})(\xi),v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(e))

Finally, since
B
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant,

\langle u\hskip 0.56917pt,v\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle x}=B(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})(\xi),v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(e))=B(\xi,\mathrm{Ad}(g)(v^{\scriptscriptstyle H}(e)))

which is the same as (
1.126
), by the definition of
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle v\,}
. Indeed, in the present case,
\mathrm{B}=B
.

compact case :
this follows from (
1.127
), using the fact that
Q
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant. Indeed, in the present case,
\mathrm{B}=Q
.

Proof of Proposition
1.10
:
for
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, introduce the corresponding vector fields
X_{\xi}
on
M
, given by
X_{\xi}(x)=\xi\cdot x
. Since this is a Killing vector field
[
19
]
, if
c:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow M
is a geodesic curve in
M
, then
\ell(\xi)=\langle X_{\xi}\hskip 0.85355pt,\dot{c}\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle c(t)}
is a constant, (a law of conservation, really due to Noether’s theorem!). Now, in the notation of Proposition
1.9
, let
\omega(t)=\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle\dot{c}(t)\,}
. By Proposition
1.9
,

\mathrm{B}(\omega(t),\xi)=\ell(\xi)

Since this is a constant, and since
\mathrm{B}
is non-degenerate, it follows that
\omega(t)=\omega
is a constant. Proposition
1.9
also implies that
c
satisfies the ordinary differential equation

\dot{c}=\omega\cdot c

But this differential equation is also satisfied by
c(t)=\exp(t\hskip 0.85355pt\omega)\cdot\hskip 0.56917ptc(0)
, as one may see from (
1.121
). By uniqueness of the solution, for given initial conditions,

c(t)=\exp(t\hskip 0.85355pt\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle\dot{c}(0)})\cdot c(0)

This immediately implies (
1.122
), by setting
t=1
,
c(0)=x
and
\dot{c}(0)=v
.

Chapter 2

The barycentre problem

State-of-the art results establish the existence and uniqueness of the Riemannian barycentre of a probability distribution which is supported inside a compact convex geodesic ball. What happens for a probability distribution which is not supported, but concentrated, inside a convex geodesic ball ? This question raises new difficulties that cannot be resolved by using the tools applicable to distributions which have compact convex support. The present chapter develops new tools, able to deal with these difficulties (at least in part), following the approach in
[
22
]
.

•

2.1
and
2.2
review some of the major contributions to the study of Riemannian barycentres, due to Fréchet, Emery, Kendall, Afsari, and Arnaudon.

•

2.3
introduces the main problem treated in the following, which is to study the existence and uniqueness of Riemannian barycentres of Gibbs distributions on compact Riemannian symmetric spaces.

•

2.4
–
2.6
lead up to the following conclusion : let
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\propto\exp(-U/T)
be a Gibbs distribution on a simply connected compact Riemannian symmetric space
M
, such that the potential function
U
has a unique global minimum at
x^{*}\in M
. If
M
is simply connected, then for each
\delta<r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}/2
(where
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}
is the convexity radius of
M
), there exists a critical temperature
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
such that
T<T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
implies that
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
has a unique Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and this
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
belongs to the geodesic ball
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. The assumption that
M
is simply connected cannot be removed (see Lemma
2.1
and the following remark).

•

2.7
provides expressions which can be used to analytically compute the critical temperature
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta\hskip 0.85355pt}
.

•

2.8
introduces additional background material, on the geometry of compact symmetric spaces, which is required for the proofs of the results in
2.4
–
2.6
.

•

2.9
details the proofs of these results, concerning the concentration, differentiability, convexity, existence and uniqueness of Riemannian barycentres of Gibbs distributions on compact symmetric spaces.

2.1

Fréchet’s fruitful idea

In 1948, Maurice Fréchet proposed a generalisation of the concept of mean value, from Euclidean spaces to general metric spaces
[
23
]
.
Today, this generalisation is known as the Fréchet mean. Precisely, a Fréchet mean, of a probability distribution
\pi
on a metric space
M
, is any global minimum of the so-called variance function

\mathcal{E}_{\pi}(y)\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi(dx)

(2.1)

where
d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)
denotes the distance between
x
and
y
in
M
. In the following, the focus will be on the case where
M
is a Riemannian manifold. Then, a Fréchet mean of
\pi
will be called a Riemannian barycentre, or just a barycentre, of
\pi
.

If
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}(y)
takes on finite values (in fact, if it is finite for just one
y=y_{o}
), then
\pi
has at least one Fréchet mean. In particular, if
M
is a Euclidean space, then this Fréchet mean is always unique, and equal to the mean value (expectation) of
\pi
. In general, the Fréchet mean of a probability distribution
\pi
is not unique, and one may think of the Fréchet mean of
\pi
as the set
F(\pi)
, of all global minima of its variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\pi\hskip 0.85355pt}
.

Example 1 :
if
M=S^{1}
, the unit circle, and
\pi
is the uniform distribution (
i.e.
Haar measure), on
S^{1}
, then
F(\pi)=S^{1}
. Any point on the circle is a barycentre of the uniform distribution.

If
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,x_{\scriptscriptstyle N}\in M
, then an empirical Fréchet mean of
(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,x_{\scriptscriptstyle N})
is any Fréchet mean of the empirical distribution
(\delta_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}}+\ldots+\delta_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle N}})/N
(
\delta_{x}
denotes the Dirac distribution concentrated at
x
). In other words, an empirical Fréchet mean of
(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,x_{\scriptscriptstyle N})
is any global minimum of the empirical variance function

\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}(y)\,=\,\frac{1}{2N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x_{n})

(2.2)

When
M
is a Riemannian manifold, the term “empirical Fréchet mean” will be replaced by the term “empirical barycentre”.

Example 2 :
if
M=S^{1}
, and
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1\hskip 0.56917pt},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
are two opposite points on
S^{1}
, then the empirical barycentre of
(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1\hskip 0.56917pt},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
is a two-point set. For example, if
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=1
and
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=-1
, then the empirical barycentre is the set
\{i,-i\}
(
i
being the square root of
-1
).

Two important problems, in relation to the concept of Fréchet mean, are establishing the uniqueness of the Fréchet mean of some probability distribution, and effectively computing this Fréchet mean, (or the set of Fréchet means, in case uniqueness does not hold).

Another type of problem is related to the large-sample theory of the Fréchet mean, and was treated in
[
24
]
[
25
]
. Assume
(x_{n}\,;n\geq 1)
are independent samples from the distribution
\pi
. If
F_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
is the set of empirical Fréchet means of
(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,x_{\scriptscriptstyle N})
, then one is interested in using
F_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
to somehow approximate
F(\pi)
.

In
[
24
]
, it was shown that, if the metric space
M
is such that any closed and bounded subset of
M
is compact
1
1

1

That is, for any
x\in M
, the function
y\mapsto d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)
is a proper function, meaning it has compact sublevel sets.
, then for any
\epsilon>0
, the set
F_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
almost-surely belongs to the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of the set
F(\pi)
, when
N
is sufficiently large.

Moreover, if
\pi
has a unique Fréchet mean, say
F(\pi)=\{\hat{x}_{\pi}\}
, then any sequence of empirical Fréchet means,
\bar{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}\in F_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
converges almost-surely to
\hat{x}_{\pi}
(an extension of this last result, from independent to Markovian samples, is obtained in
4.3.2
).

In
[
25
]
, a central limit theorem was added to this last convergence result. Specifically, if
M
is a Riemannian manifold, the distribution of
N^{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{Exp}_{\hat{x}_{\pi}}(\bar{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N})
converges to a multivariate normal distribution (in the tangent space at
\hat{x}_{\pi}
). This “central limit theorem” requires several technical conditions, in order to hold true, and should therefore only be applied after due verification.

2.2

Existence and uniqueness

The problem of the existence and uniqueness of Riemannian barycentres has generated a rich literature, with ramifications in stochastic analysis on manifolds, Riemannian geometry, and probability theory. The present section attempts a quick, non-exhaustive summary of some famous results from this literature.

2.2.1

Emery and Kendall

The works of Emery and Kendall
[
26
]
, later expanded upon by Afsari
[
27
]
, are related to the existence and uniqueness of the Riemannian barycentre of a probability distribution
\pi
, supported inside some geodesic ball
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, in a Riemannian manifold
M
.

Emery and Kendall, among others, considered the so-called Karcher mean of
\pi
. This is a local minimum of the variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
in (
2.1
). In
[
26
]
,
\pi
is assumed to have compact support, inside a so-called regular geodesic ball
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. Here, “regular geodesic ball” means

\bullet

\delta<\frac{\pi}{2}c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, where all sectional curvatures of
M
are less than
\kappa_{\max}=c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
.

\bullet
the cut locus of
x^{*}
does not intersect
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
(that is
\delta<\mathrm{inj}(x^{*})
).

These two conditions guarantee that the closed ball
\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
is weakly convex, and that it has convex geometry.

Weakly convex means for any
x\hskip 0.56917pt,y\in\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
there exists a unique geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M
, such that
\gamma(0)=x
,
\gamma(1)=y
and
\gamma(t)\in\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
for all
t\in[0,1]
(this is equivalent to the terminology of
[
11
]
2
2

2

This geodesic
\gamma
is the unique length-minimising curve, among all curves which connect
x
to
y
and lie in
\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. See the proof of Theorem IX.6.2, Page 405 in
[
11
]
.
).

Convex geometry means there exists a positive, bounded, continuous, and convex function
\Psi
, defined on
\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)\times\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, such that
\Psi(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)=0
if and only if
x=y
.

When
\pi
is supported inside
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, the function
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
takes on finite values, and therefore has a global minimum
\hat{x}_{\pi}\hskip 0.85355pt
. However, it is not immediately clear this
\hat{x}_{\pi}
should lie within
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. In
[
26
]
, the existence of a local minimum,
i.e.
Karcher mean, within
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
is guaranteed, subject to interpreting the distance in (
2.1
) as geodesic distance within
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
.

If
\hat{x}_{\pi}
is a local minimum of
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
in
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, then the convex geometry property of the closed ball
\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
guarantees this local minimum is unique. This follows by using a general form of Jensen’s inequality, due to Emery. Specifically, if
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
are Karcher means in
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, then
(\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1\hskip 0.56917pt},\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
is a Karcher mean of the image distribution
\delta^{*}\pi
of
\pi
, under the map
\delta(x)=(x,x)
. Then, applying Jensen’s inequality to the convex function
\Psi
, it follows

\Psi(\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1\hskip 0.56917pt},\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\leq\int_{\scriptscriptstyle\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)}\Psi(x,x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi(dx)

so
\Psi(\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1\hskip 0.56917pt},\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})=0
, and therefore
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2\hskip 0.85355pt}
.

Remark :
it was conjectured by Emery that any weakly convex geodesic ball should also have convex geometry. A counterexample to this conjecture was provided by Kendall, in the form of his “propeller”
[
28
]
.

2.2.2

Afsari’s contribution

Afsari’s seminal work on Riemannian barycentres was published ten years ago
[
27
]
. It provided the following statement : if
\pi
is supported inside a geodesic ball
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, then
\pi
has a unique Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\pi}
and
\hat{x}_{\pi}\in B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, as soon as

\delta<\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\min\left\{\pi c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1},\mathrm{inj}(M)\right\}

(2.3)

Here,
c
is such that all sectional curvatures of
M
are less than
\kappa_{\max}=c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
(if
M
has negative sectional curvatures,
c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
is understood to be
+\infty
), and
\mathrm{inj}(M)
is the injectivity radius of
M
.

Condition (
2.3
) ensures the geodesic ball
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
is convex, in the sense of
1.7.4
(strongly convex, in the terminology of
[
11
]
), rather than just weakly convex as in
2.2.1
. This stronger condition is required, because the Riemannian barycentre (Fréchet mean) is considered, rather than just the Karcher mean. In fact, Afsari extended his results beyond Riemannian barycentres to
L^{\scriptscriptstyle p}
Riemannian barycentres, which are obtained by replacing the squared distance in (
2.1
) with a distance elevated to the power
p
, where
p\geq 1
.

In
[
27
]
, the following approach is used, for the proof of existence and uniqueness. First, it is shown that any global minimum of
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
must lie inside
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. This is done using the Alexandrov-Toponogov comparison theorem, under its form stated in
[
11
]
(Page 420). Then, the Poincaré-Hopf theorem is employed, in order to prove uniqueness of local minima, inside the geodesic ball
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
.

Specifically,
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
is differentiable at any point
y
which belongs to the closed ball
\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, and

\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}_{\pi}(y)\,=\,-\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi(dx)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for }y\in\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)

Then, it is shown that, if
y\in B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
and
\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}_{\pi}(y)=0
, then
\mathrm{Hess}\,\mathcal{E}_{\pi}(y)
is positive-definite. In other words, the singular point
y
of the gradient vector field
\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
has its index equal to
1
. Since this vector field is outward pointing on the boundary of
\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, the Poincaré-Hopf theorem implies the sum of the indices of all its singular points in
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
is equal to the Euler-Poincaré characteristic of
\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, which is equal to
1
(since
\bar{B}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
is homeomorphic to a closed ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
).

Remark :
the argument just summarised not only shows that
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
has a unique local minimum in
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, but that it has a unique stationary point in
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. Moreover, the advantage of this argument, over the “convex geometry” uniqueness argument, (summarised in
2.2.1
), is that it can be used to show the uniqueness of
L^{\scriptscriptstyle p}
Riemannian barycentres, for general
p>1
.

2.2.3

Hadamard manifolds

Existence and uniqueness of Riemannian barycentres hold under quite general conditions, when the underlying Riemannian manifold
M
is a Hadamard manifold (recall definition from
1.7.4
). Mostly, these existence and uniqueness properties are just special cases of the properties of Fréchet means in metric spaces of non-positive curvature, which were developed by Sturm
[
29
]
.

Let
\pi
be a probability distribution on a Hadamard manifold
M
. As already mentioned in
2.1
, if the variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
in (
2.1
) takes on finite values, then
\pi
has at least one Riemannian barycentre, say
\hat{x}_{\pi\hskip 0.85355pt}
. For this, it is enough that

\mathcal{E}_{\pi}(y_{o})<\infty
, for just one
y_{o}\in M
. In other words, it is enough that
\pi
should have a finite second-order moment

\int_{M}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(y_{o}\hskip 0.85355pt,x)\,\pi(dx)\,<\,\infty

(2.4)

Indeed, if (
2.4
) is verified, then a straightforward application of the triangle inequality implies that
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}(y)<\infty
for all
y\in M
.

When
M
is a Hadamard manifold, existence of a Riemannian barycentre automatically implies its uniqueness. This can be shown using the “convex geometry” uniqueness argument, discussed in
2.2.1
. Indeed, if
M
is a Hadamard manifold, then
\Psi:M\times M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
\Psi(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)=d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)
is convex, and
\Psi(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)=0
if and only if
x=y
. Alternatively, uniqueness of the Riemannian barycentre follows from the strong convexity of the variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\pi\hskip 0.85355pt}
. Recall from
1.7.4
that
f_{x}(y)=d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)/2
is a
1/2
-strongly convex function, for each
x\in M
. Then, (
2.1
) says that
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
is an expectation of
1/2
-strongly convex functions, and is therefore
1/2
-strongly convex. In turn, this implies that
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
has a unique global minimum,
\hat{x}_{\pi}\in M
.

When
M
is a Hadamard manifold, it should also be noted that
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
is smooth throughout
M
, and that its gradient is given by

\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}_{\pi}(y)=-\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi(dx)

(2.5)

as can be found by applying (
1.75
) under the integral in (
2.1
). Strong convexity of
\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
implies its global minimum
\hat{x}_{\pi}
is also its unique stationary point in
M
(
i.e.
the unique point where
\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}_{\pi}
is equal to zero).

2.2.4

Generic uniqueness

The empirical barycentre of the points
(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,x_{\scriptscriptstyle N})
, in any complete Riemannian manifold
M
, is generically unique. This means that this empirical barycentre is unique, for almost all
(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,x_{\scriptscriptstyle N})
in the product Riemannian manifold
M^{\scriptscriptstyle N}=M\times\ldots\times M
, equipped with its Riemannian volume measure. This interesting result was obtained by Arnaudon and Miclo
[
30
]
. In particular, it implies that when
(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,x_{\scriptscriptstyle N})
are independent samples, from a distribution
\pi
, which has a probability density with respect to the Riemannian volume of
M
, then their empirical barycentre
\bar{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
is almost-surely unique.

2.3

Gibbs distributions : an open problem

Throughout the following,
M
will be a compact, orientable Riemannian manifold, with positive sectional curvatures, all less than
\kappa_{\max}=c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
. Afsari’s statement, recalled in
2.2.2
, says that if
\pi
is a probability distribution on
M
, supported inside a convex geodesic ball
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, then
\pi
has a unique Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\pi}\hskip 0.85355pt
, as soon as

\delta<\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\min\left\{\pi c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1},\mathrm{inj}(M)\right\}

(2.6)

where
\mathrm{inj}(M)
denotes the injectivty radius of
M
.

Inequality (
2.6
) is optimal. Indeed, it is easy to think of examples which show that, if it is replaced by an equality, then
\hat{x}_{\pi}
will immediately fail to be unique. On the other hand, this inequality does not tell us what happens in the important case where
\pi=\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is a Gibbs distribution,

\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)=\left(Z(T)\right)^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\exp\left[-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right]\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)\\

(2.7)

for some temperature
T
, and potential function
U:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
Z(T)
is a normalising constant (
\mathrm{vol}
denotes the Riemannian volume form).

The present chapter will introduce several results, which deal with this case. These are concerned with the concentration, differentiability, convexity, and uniqueness properties, of the Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
of the Gibbs distribution
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
.

The starting assumption for these results is that the potential function
U
has a unique global minimum at
x^{*}\in M
. Under this assumption, while
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is not supported inside any convex geodesic ball
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, it is still
concentrated
on any such ball, provided the temperature
T
is sufficiently small. Then, the aim is to know exactly how small
T
should be made, in order to ensure the required properties of
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T\hskip 0.56917pt}
. This aim can be fully achieved, under the further assumption that
M
is a simply connected compact Riemannian symmetric space.

Given these two assumptions, the following conclusion will be obtained : for each
\delta<\frac{1}{2}r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}
(
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}
denotes the convexity radius of
M
), there exists a critical temperature
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
such that
T<T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
implies
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
has a unique Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and this
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
belongs to the geodesic ball
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. Moreover, if
U
is invariant by geodesic symmetry about
x^{*}
, then
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=x^{*}
.

Remark :
if
M
is a Riemannian manifold, the convexity radius
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}(x)
of
x\in M
is the supremum of
R>0
such that the geodesic ball
B(x\hskip 0.56917pt,R)
is convex (this is strictly positive, for any
x\in M
). The convexity radius
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}(M)
of
M
is the infimum of
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}(x)
, over all
x\in M
(if
M
is compact, this is strictly positive). Here,
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}(M)
is just denoted
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx\hskip 0.85355pt}
.

2.4

Concentration of barycentres

Denote the variance function of the Gibbs distribution
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
in (
2.7
) by
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T\hskip 0.85355pt}
. According to (
2.1
),

\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.8)

Throughout the following, it will be assumed that the potential function
U
, which appears in (
2.7
), has a unique global minimum at
x^{*}\in M
. While
U
is not required to be smooth, it is required to be well-behaved near
x^{*}
, in the sense that there exist
\mu_{\min}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu_{\max}>0
and
\rho>0
such that

\mu_{\min}\hskip 0.85355ptd^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\,\leq\,2(U(x)-U(x^{*}))\,\leq\mu_{\max}\hskip 0.85355ptd^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})

(2.9)

whenever
d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\leq\rho
. This is always verified if
U
is twice differentiable at
x^{*}
, and the spectrum of
\mathrm{Hess}\,U(x^{*})
is contained in the open interval
(\mu_{\min}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu_{\max})
.

The following Proposition
2.1
establishes the concentration property of the Riemannian barycentres of
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
as the temperature
T
is made small. In this proposition,
W
denotes the Kantorovich (
L^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
-Wasserstein) distance, and
\delta_{x^{*}}
the Dirac distribution concentrated at
x^{*}
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
M
be a compact, orientable Riemannian manifold, with positive sectional curvatures, and dimension equal to
n
.

(i) Let
\eta>0
. For any Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
of
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}

W(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})<\frac{\eta^{2}}{4\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{diam}\,M}\;\Longrightarrow\;d(\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})<\eta

(2.10)

where
\mathrm{diam}\,M
is the diameter of
M
.

(ii) There exists a temperature
T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
such that
T\leq T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
implies

W(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})\leq(8\pi)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptB^{-1}_{n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{\!n-1}\left(\frac{\mu_{\max}}{\mu_{\min}}\right)^{\!\!\frac{n}{2}}\left(\frac{T}{\mu_{\min}}\right)^{\!\!\frac{1}{2}}

(2.11)

where
B_{n}=B(1/2\hskip 0.56917pt,n/2)
in terms of the Euler Beta function.

Proposition
2.1
shows exactly how small
T
should be made, in order to ensure that all the Riemannian barycentres
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
concentrate within an open ball
B(x^{*}\!,\eta)
. Roughly, (i) states that, if
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is close to
\delta_{x^{*}}\hskip 0.85355pt
, then all
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
will be close to
x^{*}
. On the other hand, (ii) bounds the distance between
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and
\delta_{x^{*}}\hskip 0.85355pt
, as a function of
T
. The temperature
T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
mentioned in (ii) will be expressed explicitly in
2.7
, below.

Here, two things should be noted, concerning (
2.11
). First, this inequality is both optimal and explicit. It is optimal because the dependence on
T^{\frac{1}{2}}
in its right-hand side cannot be improved. Indeed, the multi-dimensional Laplace approximation (for example, see
[
31
]
), shows the left-hand side is equivalent to
\mathrm{L}\cdot T^{\frac{1}{2}}
when
T\rightarrow 0
. While this constant
\mathrm{L}
is not tractable, the constants appearing in (
2.11
) depend explicitly on the manifold
M
and the function
U
. In fact, (
2.11
) does not follow from the multi-dimensional Laplace approximation, but rather from the volume comparison theorems, in
1.9.1
.

Second, in spite of these nice properties, (
2.11
) does not escape the curse of dimensionality. Indeed, for fixed
T
, its right-hand side increases exponentially with the dimension
n
of
M
(note that
B_{n}
decreases like
n^{\scriptscriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}}
). In fact, the temperature
T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
also depends on
n
, but it is typically much less affected by it, and decreases slower than
n^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
as
n
increases.

2.5

Differentiability of the variance function

Assume that
M
is a simply connected compact Riemannian symmetric space. Under this assumption, it turns out that the variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
is
C^{2}
throughout
M
, for any value
T>0
of the temperature
T
. This surprising result is contained in Proposition
2.2
.

To state Proposition
2.2
, consider for
x\in M
the function
f_{x}(y)=d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,y)/2
. Recall from
1.7.4
that this function is
C^{2}
on the open set
\mathrm{D}(x)=M-\mathrm{Cut}(x)
. When
y\in\mathrm{D}(x)
, denote
G_{y}(x)
and
H_{y}(x)
the gradient and Hessian of
f_{x}(y)
.

With this notation, for any
x\in M
, the gradient
G_{y}(x)
belongs to
T_{y}M
, and the Hessian
H_{y}(x)
defines a symmetric bilinear form on
T_{y}M
. However (recall the remarks in
1.7.3
), both
G_{y}(x)
and
H_{y}(x)
are singular on
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, where
H_{y}(x)
will even blow up, as it has an eigenvalue equal to
-\infty
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
M
be a simply connected compact Riemannian symmetric space.

(i) The following integrals converge for any temperature
T>0

G_{y}=\int_{\mathrm{D}(y)}G_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)\hskip 5.69046pt;\hskip 5.69046ptH_{y}=\int_{\mathrm{D}(y)}H_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.12)

and both depend continuously on
y
.

(ii) The gradient and Hessian of the variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
are given by

\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)=G_{y}\hskip 5.69046pt;\hskip 5.69046pt\mathrm{Hess}\,\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)=H_{y}

(2.13)

so that
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
is
C^{2}
throughout
M
.

The proof of Proposition
2.2
relies on the following lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume
M
is a simply connected compact Riemannian symmetric space. Let
\gamma:I\rightarrow M
be a geodesic defined on a compact interval
I
. Denote by
\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)
the union of all cut loci
\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma(t))
for
t\in I
. Then, the Hausdorff dimension of
\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)
is strictly less than the dimension of
M
. In particular,
\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)
is a set with Riemannian volume equal to zero.

Remark :
the assumption that
M
is simply connected cannot be removed. For example, the conclusion of Lemma
2.1
does not hold if
M
is a real projective space.

The proof of Lemma
2.1
uses the structure of Riemannian symmetric spaces, as well as some results from dimension theory, found in
[
32
]
. The notion of Hausdorff dimension is needed, because
\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)
may fail to be a manifold.

Lemma
2.1
is crucial to Proposition
2.2
, because it leads to the following expression,

\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(\gamma(t))=\int_{M}\,f_{x}(\gamma(t))\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)=\int_{\mathrm{D}(\gamma)}f_{x}(\gamma(t))\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for all $t\in I$}

where
\mathrm{D}(\gamma)=M-\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)
, and the second inequality follows since
\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)
has Riemannian volume equal to zero. Then, recalling that
x\in\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma(t))
if and only if
\gamma(t)\in\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, it becomes possible to differentiate
f_{x}(\gamma(t))
under the integral. This leads to the proof of (ii).

2.6

Uniqueness of the barycentre

The following Proposition
2.3
establishes the uniqueness of
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
as the temperature
T
is made small. As in the previous Proposition
2.2
,
M
is a simply connected compact Riemannian symmetric space. The convexity radius of
M
is denoted
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx\hskip 0.85355pt}
. This is given by
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}=\frac{\pi}{2}\hskip 0.85355ptc^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
(see
2.8
, below).

Recall the definition (
2.7
) of the Gibbs distribution
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T\hskip 0.85355pt}
, where the potential function
U
has a unique global minimum at
x^{*}\in M
. Let
s_{x^{*}}
denote the geodesic symmetry at
x^{*}
(recall definition from
1.9.2
). The potential function
U
is said to be invariant by geodesic symmetry about
x^{*}
, if
U\circ s_{x^{*}}=U
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
M
be a simply connected compact Riemannian symmetric space, with convexity radius
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx\hskip 0.85355pt}
. For
\delta<\frac{1}{2}r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx\hskip 0.85355pt}
, there exists a critical temperature
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
such that

(i) When
T<T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta\hskip 0.85355pt}
, the Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
of
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is unique and
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\in B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
.

(ii) If, in addition,
U
is invariant by geodesic symmetry about
x^{*}
, then
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=x^{*}
.

Proposition
2.3
shows exactly how small
T
should be made, in order to ensure that the Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is unique. In turn, this uniqueness of
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
follows from the convexity of the variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
, obtained in the following Lemma
2.2
.

To state this lemma, consider the function
f(T)
of the temperature
T

f(T)=\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\right)\left(\frac{\pi}{8}\right)^{\!n-1}\left(\frac{\mu_{\max}}{T}\right)^{\!\!\frac{n}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{U_{\delta}}{T}\right)

(2.14)

for any given
\delta
, where
U_{\delta}=\inf\{U(x)-U(x^{*})\,;x\notin B(x^{*}\!,\delta)\}
. Note that
f(T)
decreases to zero as
T
is made arbitrarily small.

Lemma 2.2
.

Under the same assumptions as Proposition
2.3
, let
\delta<\frac{1}{2}r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx\hskip 0.85355pt}
.

(i) For all
y\in B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
,

\mathrm{Hess}\,\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)\,\geq\,\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)\hskip 0.85355pt[1-\mathrm{vol}(M)f(T)]-\pi A_{\scriptscriptstyle M}\hskip 0.85355ptf(T)

(2.15)

where
\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)=2c\delta\hskip 0.56917pt\cot(2c\delta)
and
A_{\scriptscriptstyle M}>0
is a constant which depends only on the symmetric space
M
.

(ii) There exists a critical temperature
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
such that
T<T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
implies the variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
is strongly convex on
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
.

The inequality in (
2.15
) should be understood as saying all the eigenvalues of
\mathrm{Hess}\,\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
are greater than the right-hand side (of course, this is an abuse of notation). The critical temperature
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
will be expressed in the following section.

2.7

Finding
T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
and
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}

The present paragraph provides expressions of the temperatures
T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
and
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta\hskip 0.85355pt}
, which appear in Propositions
2.1
and
2.3
. These are expressions (
2.17
) and (
2.18
) below, which should be considered as part of Propositions
2.1
and
2.3
, and will accordingly be proved in
2.9
.

Expressions (
2.17
) and (
2.18
) allow
T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
and
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
to be computed as solutions of scalar non-linear equations, which depend on Condition (
2.9
) and on the Riemannian symmetric space
M
. In order to state them, write

f(T,m,\rho)=\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\right)^{\!\!\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{\mu_{\max}}{T}\right)^{\!\!\frac{m}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{U_{\rho}}{T}\right)

(2.16)

in terms of the temperature
T
and positive
m
and
\rho
, where
U_{\rho}
is defined as in (
2.14
). It should be noted that
f(T,m,\rho)
decreases to
0
as
T
is made arbitrarily small, for fixed
m
and
\rho
. The following expression holds for
T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\hskip 0.85355pt
,

T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\,=\,\min\hskip 0.56917pt\{T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\hskip 0.56917pt,T^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\}

(2.17)

with
T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
and
T^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
given by

\begin{array}[]{l}T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}=\inf\hskip 0.56917pt\{T>0:f(T,n-2,\rho)>\rho^{2-n}\hskip 0.56917ptA_{n-1}\}\\[11.38092pt]
T^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}=\inf\hskip 0.56917pt\left\{T>0:f(T,n+1,\rho)>\left(\mu_{\max}\middle/\mu_{\min}\right)^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\frac{n}{2}}\hskip 0.28436ptC_{n}\right\}\end{array}

where
A_{n}
is the
n
-th absolute moment of a standard normal random variable (
A_{n}=\mathbb{E}|X|^{n}
where
X\sim N(0,1)
), and
C_{n}=\left.(\omega_{n-1}\hskip 0.56917ptA_{n})\middle/(\mathrm{diam}\,M\times\mathrm{vol}\,M)\right.
, where
\omega_{n-1}
is the area of the unit sphere
S^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover, for
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}\hskip 0.85355pt
,

T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}\,=\,\min\hskip 0.56917pt\{T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}\hskip 0.56917pt,T^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}\}

(2.18)

where, in the notation of (
2.11
) and (
2.15
),

\begin{array}[]{l}T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}=\inf\hskip 0.56917pt\left\{T\leq T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}:\left(2\pi T\middle/\mu_{\min}\right)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}>\delta^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\hskip 0.85355pt\left(\mu_{\min}\middle/\mu_{\max}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}\hskip 0.85355ptD_{n}\right\}\\[11.38092pt]
T^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}=\inf\hskip 0.56917pt\left\{T\leq T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}:f(T)>\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)[\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)\mathrm{vol}(M)+\pi A_{\scriptscriptstyle M}]^{-1}\right\}\end{array}

with
D_{n}=(2/\pi)^{n-1}\!\left.B_{n}\middle/(4\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{diam}\,M)\right.
.

Remark :
the following formulae for
A_{n}
and
\omega_{n-1}
will be useful in
2.9
,

A_{n}=\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}}2^{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{n}{2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\Gamma((n+1)/2)\hskip 5.69046pt;\hskip 5.69046pt\omega_{n-1}=\frac{2\hskip 0.56917pt\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{n}{2}}}{\Gamma(n/2)}

(2.19)

These are well-known, and follow easily from the definition of the Euler Gamma function
[
33
]
.

2.8

Compact symmetric spaces

Compact Riemannian symmetric spaces belong to the “compact case”, already treated in
1.9.2
. Some additional material, on these spaces, is needed for the proofs of Propositions
2.2
and
2.3
.

2.8.1

Roots and the Jacobi equation

As of now, let
M=G/K
be a symmetric space, where
G
is semisimple and compact, and
K=K_{y}
the stabiliser in
G
of some point
y\in M
. Recall the Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}+\mathfrak{p}
, where
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{k}
are the Lie algebras of
G
and
K
, respectively. Moreover, let
\mathfrak{a}
be a maximal Abelian subspace of
\mathfrak{p}
, and denote
\Delta_{+}
the corresponding set of positive roots
\lambda:\mathfrak{a}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Then,
\mathfrak{p}
may be identified with
T_{y}M
, and any
v\in\mathfrak{p}
can be written
v=\mathrm{Ad}(k)\,a
for some
k\in K
and
a\in\mathfrak{a}
. Accordinly, the self-adjoint curvature operator,
R_{v}
(given by
R_{v}(u)=[v\hskip 0.85355pt,[v\hskip 0.56917pt,u]]
for
u\in T_{y}M
), can be diagonalised (the reader may wish to note (
2.20
) differs from (
1.104
) by a minus sign, since the space here denoted
\mathfrak{p}
would have been
\mathfrak{p}_{*}=i\hskip 0.56917pt\mathfrak{p}
, in Chapter
1
)

\mathrm{Ad}(k^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})\circ R_{v}\circ\mathrm{Ad}(k)=R_{a}\hskip 11.38092pt\text{where }R_{a}=-\sum_{\lambda\in\Delta_{+}}(\lambda(a))^{2}\;\Pi_{\lambda}

(2.20)

and where
\Pi_{\lambda}
is the orthogonal projector onto the eigenspace of
R_{a}
which corresponds to the eigenvalue
-(\lambda(a))^{2}
. The rank of
\Pi_{\lambda}
is denoted
m_{\lambda}
and called the multiplicity of
\lambda
.

Recall that the curvature tensor of a symmetric space is parallel :
\hskip 0.85355pt\nabla\,R=0
. This property, when combined with the diagonalisation (
2.20
), yields the solutions of the operator Jacobi equation (
1.69
), and of the Ricatti equation (
1.71
).

Alternatively, if
A(t)
solves (
1.69
) and
\mathcal{A}(t)=\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}_{t}\circ A(t)
, where
\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}_{t}
denotes parallel transport, along the geodesic
c_{\scriptscriptstyle v}
with
c_{\scriptscriptstyle v}(0)=y
and
\dot{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle v}(0)=v
, then
\mathcal{A}(t)
solves the differential equation

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}-R_{v}\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{A}=0\hskip 28.45274pt\mathcal{A}(0)=0\,,\,\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(0)=\mathrm{Id}_{y}

(2.21)

where the prime denotes differentiation with respect to
t
. Using (
2.20
), it follows that

\mathcal{A}(t)\,=\,\Pi^{k}_{\mathfrak{a}}\,+\,\sum_{\lambda\in\Delta_{+}}\left(\sin(\lambda(a)t)\middle/\lambda(a)\right)\hskip 0.56917pt\Pi^{k}_{\mathfrak{\lambda}}

(2.22)

where
\Pi^{k}_{\mathfrak{a}}=\mathrm{Ad}(k)\circ\Pi_{\mathfrak{a}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}(k^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})
and

\Pi^{k}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{Ad}(k)\circ\Pi_{\lambda}\circ\mathrm{Ad}(k^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})
, with
\Pi_{\mathfrak{a}}
the orthogonal projector onto
\mathfrak{a}
.

2.8.2

The cut locus

Let
M
be a compact Riemannian symmetric space, as above. Assume, as in Propositions
2.2
and
2.3
, that
M
is simply connected. In ths case, the following important property holds
[
9
]
: the cut locus of any point
y\in M
is identical to the first conjugate locus of this point.

Accordingly, if
v
is a unit vector in
\mathfrak{p}\simeq T_{y}M
, the geodesic
c_{\scriptscriptstyle v}
will meet the cut locus of
y
for the first time, when
\det(\mathcal{A}(t))=0
for the first time after
t=0
. But, as seen from (
2.22
), if
v=\mathrm{Ad}(k)\,a
, then this happens when
t=\mathrm{t}(v)
given by

\mathrm{t}(v)=\min_{\lambda\in\Delta_{+}}\,\frac{\pi}{|\lambda(a)|}=\min_{\lambda\in\Delta_{+}}\,\frac{\pi}{\lambda(a)}

(2.23)

where the absolute value can be dropped because it is always possible to assume
a
belongs to
\bar{C}_{+}\hskip 0.56917pt
, the closure of the Weyl chamber
C_{+}
(the set of
a\in\mathfrak{a}
such that
\lambda(a)>0
for each
\lambda\in\Delta_{+}
). If
M
is an irreducible symmetric space, then there exists a maximal root
c\in\Delta_{+}\hskip 0.56917pt
, so that
c(a)\geq\lambda(a)
for all
\lambda\in\Delta_{+}
and
a\in\bar{C}_{+}

[
10
]
. In this case,
\mathrm{t}(v)=\pi/c(a)
. On the other hand, if
M
is not irreducible, it is a product of irreducible compact Riemannian symmetric spaces, say
M=M_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\times\ldots\times M_{s\hskip 0.85355pt}
. If
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,c_{s}
are the corresponding maximal roots,

\mathrm{t}(v)=\min_{\ell=1,\ldots,\hskip 0.56917pts}\,\frac{\pi}{c_{\ell}(a)}

(2.24)

The cut locus of
y
is the set of all points
c_{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\mathrm{t}(v))
where
v
is a unit vector in
T_{y}M
. Then, the injectivity radius
\mathrm{inj}(y)
of
y
is equal to the minimum of
\mathrm{t}(v)
, taken over all unit vectors
v
. From (
2.24
), this is equal to
\pi\hskip 0.85355ptc^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
where
c=\max_{\ell=1,\ldots,\hskip 0.56917pts}\|c_{\ell}\|
and
\|c_{\ell}\|
denotes the norm of
c_{\ell}\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}
(the dual space of
\mathfrak{a}
). Since
M
is a homogeneous space, the injectivity radius of
M
is also equal to
\pi\hskip 0.85355ptc^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, since it is equal to the injectivity radius of any point
y
in
M
. Incidentally,
c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is the maximum sectional curvature of
M
.

With a bit of additional work, the above description of the cut locus of
y
can be strengthened, to yield the following statements. Let
S=K/K_{\mathfrak{a}}
where
K_{\mathfrak{a}}
is the centraliser of
\mathfrak{a}
in
K
. Moreover, denote
Q_{+}
the set of
a\in\mathfrak{a}
such that
\lambda(a)\in(0,\pi)
for each
\lambda\in\Delta_{+}\hskip 0.56917pt
. Then, consider the mapping

\varphi(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)=\mathrm{Exp}_{y}(\beta(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a))\hskip 28.45274pt(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)\in S\times\bar{Q}_{+}

(2.25)

where
\beta(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)=\mathrm{Ad}(s)\,a
and
\bar{Q}_{+}
is the closure of
Q_{+}\hskip 0.56917pt
. This mapping
\varphi
is onto
M
, and is a diffeomorphism of
S\times Q_{+}
onto its image
M_{r}\hskip 0.85355pt
, which is also the set of regular values of
\varphi
. Finally,

\mathrm{Cut}(y)=\varphi(S\times\bar{Q}_{\pi})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }\bar{Q}_{\pi}=\bar{Q}_{+}\,\cap\,(\cup_{\ell}\,\{a:c_{\ell}(a)=\pi\})

(2.26)

2.8.3

The squared distance function

For
x\in M
, consider the squared distance function
f_{x}(y)=d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,y)/2
. If
x\notin\mathrm{Cut}(y)
, then
f_{x}
is
C^{2}
near
y
(this is because
y\in\mathrm{Cut}(x)
if and only if
x\in\mathrm{Cut}(y)
).

In this case, write
x=\varphi(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)
, where the map
\varphi
was defined in (
2.25
). Let
G_{y}(x)
and
H_{y}(x)
denote the gradient and Hessian of
f_{x}
at
y
. These are given by

G_{y}(x)=-\beta(s,a)\hskip 93.89418pt

(2.27)

H_{y}(x)=\Pi^{s}_{\mathfrak{a}}\,+\,\sum_{\lambda\in\Delta_{+}}\lambda(a)\cot\lambda(a)\;\Pi^{s}_{\lambda}

(2.28)

in the notation of (
2.22
). Here, (
2.27
) follows from (
1.75
), since
x=\mathrm{Exp}_{y}\hskip 0.85355pt(\beta(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a))
, and (
2.28
) follows from the solution of the Ricatti equation (
1.71
), discussed in
2.8.1
.

If
M
is simply connected, then
\mathrm{Cut}(y)
is given by (
2.26
). Now, if
x\in\mathrm{Cut}(y)
is written
x=\varphi(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)
, then
\lambda(a)=\pi
for some
\lambda\in\Delta_{+}\hskip 0.56917pt
(
\lambda=c_{\ell}
which achieves the minimum in (
2.24
)). By (
2.28
),
H_{y}(x)
then has an eigenvalue equal to
-\infty
. In other words,
H_{y}(x)
blows up when
x
approaches
\mathrm{Cut}(y)
.

The convexity radius of a simply connected compact Riemannian symmetric space
M
is equal to half its injectivity radius. Accordingly, the convexity radius of
M
is
r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}=(\pi/2)\hskip 0.85355ptc^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
. The proof of this statement may be summarised in the following way :

If
\delta<r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx\hskip 0.56917pt}
, then any
y_{\scriptscriptstyle 1\hskip 0.56917pt},y_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
in
B(x\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta)
must have
d(y_{\scriptscriptstyle 1\hskip 0.56917pt},y_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})<\pi\hskip 0.85355ptc^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, the injectivity radius of
M
, and are therefore connected by a unique length-minimising geodesic curve
\gamma
. But, by (
2.28
), the squared distance function
f_{x}
is convex on
B(x\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta)
, where all eigenvalues of its Hessian are greater than
c\delta\hskip 0.56917pt\cot(c\delta)>0
. This can be used to show that the geodesic
\gamma
lies entirely in
B(x\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta)

[
34
]
(Page 177). In other words, the geodesic ball
B(x\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta)
is convex. On the other hand
[
9
]
, if
\delta=r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}
then there exists a closed (i.e. periodic) geodesic, of length
2\pi\hskip 0.85355ptc^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, contained in
B(x\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta)
, so that this geodesic ball cannot be convex.

2.8.4

A different integral formula

Consider again the map
\varphi
, defined in (
2.25
). Let
M_{r}
denote the set of regular values of
\varphi
. By Sard’s lemma
[
16
]
, the complement of
M_{r}
in
M
has zero Riemannian volume. Therefore, if
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a measurable function,

\int_{M}\,f(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)=\int_{M_{r}}\,f(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(2.29)

However, it was seen in
2.8.2
that
\varphi
is a diffeomorphism of
S\times Q_{+}
onto
M_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt
. Then, performing a “change of variables”, it follows that

\int_{M}\,f(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)=\int_{Q_{+}}\!\int_{S}\,f(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)\hskip 0.56917ptD(a)\hskip 0.85355ptda\hskip 0.56917pt\omega(ds)

(2.30)

where
f(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)=f(\varphi(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a))
and
\varphi^{*}(\mathrm{vol})=D(a)\hskip 0.85355ptda\hskip 0.56917pt\omega(ds)
. In particular, the “volume density”
D(a)
can be read from (
1.113
),

D(a)=\prod_{\lambda\in\Delta_{+}}\left|\sin\hskip 0.56917pt\lambda(a)\right|^{m_{\lambda}}\hskip 68.28644pt

(2.31)

where the absolute value may be dropped, whenever
a\in Q_{+}
is understood from the context.

Remark :
the integral formula (
2.30
) is somewhat similar to (
1.115
). Roughly, both formulae involve the same change of variables, but (
2.30
) takes advantage of the the description of the cut locus of
y
in (
2.26
). Of course, (
2.30
) only works when the compact symmetric space
M
is simply connected.

2.9

All the proofs

Throughout the following proofs, it will be assumed that
U(x^{*})=0
. There is no loss of generality in making this assumption. Indeed, looking back at the definition (
2.7
) of the Gibbs distribution
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.85355pt
, it is clear that a factor
\exp(-U(x^{*})/T)
may always be absorbed into
Z(T)
.

2.9.1

Proof of Proposition
2.1

Proof of (i)

For each
y\in M
, let
f_{y}(x)=d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x)/2
. It follows from (
2.8
) that

\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)=\int_{M}\,f_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.32)

On the other hand, consider the function
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(y)
,

\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(y)=\int_{M}\,f_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\delta_{x^{*}}(dx)=d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})/2

(2.33)

For any
y\in M
, it is elementary that
f_{y}(x)
is a Lipschitz function of
x
, with Lipschitz constant
\mathrm{diam}\,M
. Then, from the Kantorovich-Rubinshtein formula
[
35
]
(see VIII.4)

\left|\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)-\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(y)\right|\leq(\mathrm{diam}\,M)\hskip 0.56917ptW(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})

(2.34)

a uniform bound in
y\in M
. It now follows that, for any
\eta>0
,

\inf_{y\in B(x^{*}\!,\eta)}\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)-\inf_{y\in B(x^{*}\!,\eta)}\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(y)\leq(\mathrm{diam}\,M)\hskip 0.56917ptW(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})

(2.35)

\inf_{y\notin B(x^{*}\!,\eta)}\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(y)-\inf_{y\notin B(x^{*}\!,\eta)}\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)\leq(\mathrm{diam}\,M)\hskip 0.56917ptW(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})

(2.36)

However, from (
2.33
), it is clear that

\inf_{y\in B(x^{*}\!,\eta)}\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(y)=0\hskip 7.11317pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046pt\inf_{y\notin B(x^{*}\!,\eta)}\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(y)=\frac{\eta^{2}}{2}

To complete the proof, replace these into (
2.35
) and (
2.36
), and assume the condition in (
2.10
) is verified. It then follows that

\inf_{y\in B(x^{*}\!,\eta)}\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)<\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}<\inf_{y\notin B(x^{*}\!,\eta)}\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)

(2.37)

However, this means any global minimum of
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
must belong to
B(x^{*}\!,\eta)
. Equivalently, any Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
of
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
must verify
d(\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})<\eta
. Thus, the conclusion in (
2.10
) holds.

Proof of (ii)

Recall the condition in (
2.9
), which holds for
d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\leq\rho
. By choosing
\rho<\min\{\mathrm{inj}(x^{*}),\frac{\pi}{2}c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\}
, it will be possible to apply (
1.94
) from
1.9.1
, in the remainder of the proof. Consider the truncated distribution

\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)\,=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B_{\rho}}(x)}{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho})}\hskip 0.85355pt\pi^{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.38)

where
\mathbf{1}
denotes the indicator function, and
B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}
denotes the open ball
B(x^{*}\!,\rho)
.
Of course, by the triangle inequality

W(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})\leq W(\pi^{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T})+W(\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})

(2.39)

Now, the proof relies on the following estimates, which use the notation of
2.7
.

– first estimate :
if
T\leq T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle W\hskip 0.85355pt}
, then

W(\pi^{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T})\leq(\mathrm{diam}\,M\times\mathrm{vol}\,M)\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\right)\left(\frac{\pi}{8}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}\left(\frac{\mu_{\max}}{T}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{U_{\rho}}{T}\right)

(2.40)

– second estimate :
if
T\leq T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle W\hskip 0.85355pt}
, then

W(\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})\leq(2\pi)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptB^{-1}_{n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{\!n-1}\left(\frac{\mu_{\max}}{\mu_{\min}}\right)^{\!\!\frac{n}{2}}\left(\frac{T}{\mu_{\min}}\right)^{\!\!\frac{1}{2}}

(2.41)

These two estimates will be proved below. To obtain (
2.11
), assume that they hold, and that
T\leq T_{\scriptscriptstyle W\hskip 0.85355pt}
. Then,
T\leq T^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
and the definition of
T^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
implies

f(T,n+1,\rho)\leq\left(\mu_{\max}\middle/\mu_{\min}\right)^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\frac{n}{2}}\hskip 0.28436ptC_{n}

Using the definition of
C_{n}
and formulae (
2.19
), this inequality reads

(\mathrm{diam}\,M\times\mathrm{vol}\,M)\hskip 0.56917ptf(T,n+1,\rho)\leq 2\hskip 0.85355pt(2\pi)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptB^{-1}_{n}\left(\mu_{\max}\middle/\mu_{\min}\right)^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\frac{n}{2}}

This is the same as

(\mathrm{diam}\,M\times\mathrm{vol}\,M)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\left(\frac{\pi}{8}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}f(T,n+1,\rho)\leq\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{\!n-1}B^{-1}_{n}\left(\mu_{\max}\middle/\mu_{\min}\right)^{\!\scriptscriptstyle\frac{n}{2}}

From the definition of
f(T,n+1,\rho)
, it then follows that the right-hand side of (
2.40
) is less than half the right-hand side of (
2.41
). Since this is the case, (
2.11
) follows from the triangle inequality (
2.39
).

– proof of first estimate :
consider the probability distribution
K
on
M\times M
,

K(dx_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\times dx_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})=\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})\left[\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho})\hskip 0.56917pt\delta_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}}(dx_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})+\mathbf{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B^{\scriptscriptstyle c}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\right]

(2.42)

where
B^{\scriptscriptstyle c}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}
denotes the complement of
B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}
in
M
. This distribution
K
provides a coupling between
\pi^{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and
\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T\hskip 0.85355pt}
. Therefore, replacing (
2.42
) into the definition of the Kantorovich distance, it follows

W(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T})\leq(\mathrm{diam}\,M)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(B^{\scriptscriptstyle c}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho})

(2.43)

However, the definition (
2.7
) of
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
implies

\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(B^{\scriptscriptstyle c}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho})\leq\left(Z(T)\right)^{-1}(\mathrm{vol}\,M)\exp\left(-\frac{U_{\rho}}{T}\right)

(2.44)

Now, (
2.40
) follows directly from (
2.43
) and (
2.44
), if the following lower bound on
Z(T)
can be proved

Z(T)\geq\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\left(\frac{8}{\pi}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}\left(\frac{T}{\mu_{\max}}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{for }T\leq T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}

(2.45)

To prove this lower bound, note that

Z(T)=\int_{M}\,\exp\left(-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)\,\geq\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}\,\exp\left(-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)

Replacing (
2.9
) into this last inequality, it is possible to write

Z(T)\geq\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}\,\exp\left(-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)\geq\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}\,\exp\left(-\frac{\mu_{\max}}{2T}d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,x^{*})\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(2.46)

Since
\rho<\min\{\mathrm{inj}(x^{*}),\frac{\pi}{2}c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\}
, it is possible to apply (
1.94
) from
1.9.1
, to (
2.46
). Specifically, the lower bound in (
1.94
) yields,

Z(T)\geq\omega_{n-1}\,\int^{\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\,e^{-\frac{\mu_{\max}}{2T}r^{2}}\left(c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\!\sin(c\hskip 0.56917ptr)\right)^{n-1}\hskip 0.56917ptdr\geq\omega_{n-1}(2/\pi)^{n-1}\,\int^{\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\,e^{-\frac{\mu_{\max}}{2T}r^{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptr^{n-1}\hskip 0.56917ptdr

(2.47)

where the second inequality follows since
\sin(t)
is a concave function of
t\in[0,\pi/2]
, so that
\sin(c\hskip 0.56917ptr)\geq(2/\pi)c\hskip 0.56917ptr
for
r\in[0,\rho]
. Now, the required bound (
2.45
) follows from (
2.47
) by noting

\int^{\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\,e^{-\frac{\mu_{\max}}{2T}r^{2}}r^{n-1}\hskip 0.56917ptdr=(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{T}{\mu_{\max}}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}A_{n-1}-\int^{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}_{\rho}\,e^{-\frac{\mu_{\max}}{2T}r^{2}}r^{n-1}\hskip 0.56917ptdr

where
A_{n}=\mathbb{E}|X|^{n}
for
X\sim N(0,1)
, and that

\int^{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}_{\rho}\,e^{-\frac{\mu_{\max}}{2T}r^{2}}r^{n-1}\hskip 0.56917ptdr\leq\frac{\rho^{n-2\hskip 0.56917pt}T}{\mu_{\max}}\,e^{-\frac{\mu_{\max}}{2T}\rho^{2}}\leq\frac{\rho^{n-2\hskip 0.56917pt}T}{\mu_{\max}}\,e^{-\frac{U_{\rho}}{T}}

Indeed, taken together, these give

Z(T)\geq\omega_{n-1}(2/\pi)^{n-1}\,\left[(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{T}{\mu_{\max}}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}A_{n-1}-\frac{\rho^{n-2\hskip 0.56917pt}T}{\mu_{\max}}\,e^{-\frac{U_{\rho}}{T}}\right]

(2.48)

Then, (
2.45
) can be obtained by noting that the second term in square brackets is negligible in comparison to the first as
T\rightarrow 0
, and using formulae (
2.19
) for
A_{n-1}
and
\omega_{n-1\hskip 0.85355pt}
.

– proof of second estimate :
the Kantorovich distance
W(\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})
between
\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and
\delta_{x^{*}}
is equal to the first-order moment

\int_{M}\,d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\hskip 0.56917pt\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

According to (
2.7
) and (
2.38
), this means

W(\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})=\left(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho})Z(T)\right)^{-1}\,\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left(-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)

Using (
2.7
) to express the probability in parentheses, this becomes

W(\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\hskip 0.56917pt,\delta_{x^{*}})=\frac{\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left(-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)}{\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}\exp\left(-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)}

(2.49)

A lower bound on the denominator can be found from (
2.46
) and the subsequent inequalities, which were used to prove (
2.45
). These inequalities provide

\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}\exp\left(-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)\geq\omega_{n-1}\hskip 5.69046pt(2/\pi)^{n-1}(2\pi)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptA_{n-1}\left(T\middle/\mu_{\max}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}

(2.50)

whenever
T\leq T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
. For the numerator in (
2.49
), it will be shown that

\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left(-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)\leq\omega_{n-1}(2\pi)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptA_{n}\left(T\middle/\mu_{\min}\right)^{\!\frac{n+1}{2}}

(2.51)

Then, (
2.41
) follows by dividing (
2.51
) by (
2.50
), and replacing in (
2.49
), using the fact that
A_{n}/A_{n-1}=(2\pi)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}B^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}_{n}\hskip 0.85355pt
, which can be found from formulae (
2.19
). Now, it only remails to prove (
2.51
). This is done by noting, from (
2.9
),

\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left(-\frac{U(x)}{T}\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)\leq\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left(-\frac{\mu_{\min}}{2T}d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,x^{*})\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)

Applying the upper bound in (
1.94
) (with
\kappa_{\min}=0
), to the last integral, it follows that

\int_{B_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}}d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left(-\frac{\mu_{\min}}{2T}d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,x^{*})\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)\leq\omega_{n-1}\int^{\rho}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}e^{-\frac{\mu_{\min}}{2T}r^{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptr^{n}\hskip 0.56917ptdr\leq\omega_{n-1}\int^{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}e^{-\frac{\mu_{\min}}{2T}r^{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptr^{n}\hskip 0.56917ptdr

The integral on the right-hand side is half the
n
-th absolute moment of a normal distribution. By expressing it in terms of
A_{n}\hskip 0.85355pt
, it is possible to directly recover (
2.51
).

2.9.2

Proof of Proposition
2.2

Proof of (i)

Under the integrals in (
2.12
),
G_{y}(x)
and
H_{y}(x)
are given by (
2.27
) and (
2.28
), for any
x\in\mathrm{D}(y)
. Furthermore, by (
2.27
) and (
2.28
), both integrands
G_{y}(x)
and
H_{y}(x)
are continuous on the domaine of integration
\mathrm{D}(y)
.

The integral
G_{y}
converges, because
G_{y}(x)
is uniformly bounded on
\mathrm{D}(y)
. Indeed, from (
2.27
),

\|G_{y}(x)\|_{y}=\|\beta(s,a)\|_{y}=d(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x)

where the second equality follows from the fact that
x=\mathrm{Exp}_{y}(\beta(s,a))
. Of course,
d(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x)
is always less than
\mathrm{diam}\,M
.

The integral
H_{y}
is an improper integral, since
H_{y}(x)
blows up when
x
approaches
\mathrm{Cut}(y)
, as explained in
2.8.3
. Nonetheless, this integral converges absolutely, as shall be seen from the material in
2.8.4
.

Precisely, recall the mapping
\varphi
defined in (
2.25
). Because
M
is simply connected,
\mathrm{Cut}(y)
is identical to the first conjugate locus of
y
. This means that
\mathrm{Cut}(y)
is contained in the set of critical values of
\mathrm{Exp}_{y}\hskip 0.85355pt
, and therefore also in the set of critical values of
\varphi
. Equivalently,
\mathrm{D}(y)
contains the set of regular values of
\varphi
, denoted
M_{r}
in (
2.29
). It then follows, as in (
2.30
),

H_{y}=\int_{Q_{+}}\!\int_{S}\,H_{y}(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)\hskip 0.56917ptp_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)\hskip 0.56917ptD(a)\hskip 0.85355ptda\hskip 0.56917pt\omega(ds)

(2.52)

where
p_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
denotes the density of
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
with respect to the Riemannian volume.

To prove that
H_{y}
converges absolutely, it is enough to prove the integrand in (
2.52
) is uniformly bounded. However, the density
p_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is bounded, since it is continuous and
M
is compact. Moreover, it is clear from (
2.28
) and (
2.31
) that

H_{y}(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)=\Pi^{s}_{\mathfrak{a}}\,+\,\sum_{\lambda\in\Delta_{+}}\lambda(a)\cot\lambda(a)\;\Pi^{s}_{\lambda}

(2.53)

D(a)=\prod_{\lambda\in\Delta_{+}}\left|\sin\hskip 0.56917pt\lambda(a)\right|^{m_{\lambda}}\hskip 68.28644pt

(2.54)

The product of these two expressions is uniformly bounded, because
\lambda(a)\in(0,\pi)
on
Q_{+}
.

Thus, the integrals
G_{y}
and
H_{y}
converge, and it is clear from the above that this is true for any temperature
T>0
. The fact that both
G_{y}
and
H_{y}
depend continuously on
y
will be clear from the arguments in the proof of (ii).

Proof of (ii)

The proof relies in a crucial way on Lemma
2.1
, which is proved in
2.9.3
, below. To compute the gradient and Hessian of
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
at
y\in M
, consider any geodesic
\gamma:I\rightarrow M
, defined on a compact interval
I=[-\tau,\tau]
, with
\gamma(0)=y
. For each
t\in I
, it is immediate from (
2.8
) that

\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(\gamma(t))=\int_{M}\,f_{x}(\gamma(t))\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.55)

However, Lemma
2.1
states that the set

\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)=\bigcup_{t\in I}\,\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma(t))

has Riemannian volume equal to zero. Thus, since
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is uniformly continuous with respect to Riemannian volume,
\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)
can be removed from the domain of integration in (
2.55
), to obtain

\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(\gamma(t))=\int_{\mathrm{D}(\gamma)}f_{x}(\gamma(t))\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for all $t\in I$}

(2.56)

where
\mathrm{D}(\gamma)=M-\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)
. Now, if
x\in\mathrm{D}(\gamma)
, then
x\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma(t))
for any
t\in I
. According to
2.8.3
, this implies that
f_{x}(\gamma(t))
is
C^{2}
near each
t\in I
. In other words,
f_{x}(t)=f_{x}(\gamma(t))
is
C^{2}
inside the interval
I
. Then, the first and second derivatives of this function are given by

f^{\prime}_{x}(t)=\left\langle G_{\gamma(t)}(x)\hskip 0.56917pt,\dot{\gamma}\right\rangle_{\gamma(t)}\hskip 8.5359pt;\hskip 8.5359ptf^{\prime\prime}_{x}(t)=\left\langle H_{\gamma(t)}(x)\cdot\dot{\gamma}\hskip 0.56917pt,\dot{\gamma}\right\rangle_{\gamma(t)}

(2.57)

as in Proposition
1.1
. Formally, (
2.13
) follows by differentiating under the integral sign in (
2.56
), replacing from (
2.57
), and then putting
t=0
. This differentiation under the integral sign is justified, as soon as it is shown that the families of functions,

\left\{x\mapsto G_{\gamma(t)}(x)\,;t\in I\right\}\hskip 8.5359pt;\hskip 8.5359pt\left\{x\mapsto H_{\gamma(t)}(x)\,;t\in I\right\}

which all have common domain of definition
\mathrm{D}(\gamma)
, are uniformly integrable with respect to the probability distribution
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
(precisely, with respect to the restriction of
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
to
\mathrm{D}(\gamma)
).

Roughly (for the exact definition, see
[
16
]
), uniform integrability means that the rate of absolute convergence of the following integrals

G_{\gamma(t)}=\int_{\mathrm{D}(\gamma)}G_{\gamma(t)}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)\hskip 5.69046pt;\hskip 5.69046ptH_{\gamma(t)}=\int_{\mathrm{D}(\gamma)}H_{\gamma(t)}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.58)

does not depend on
t\in I
. This is clear for the integrals
G_{\gamma(t)}
because, as in the proof of (i),

\|G_{\gamma(t)}(x)\|_{\gamma(t)}=d(\gamma(t)\hskip 0.56917pt,x)

and this is bounded by
\mathrm{diam}\,M
, independently of
x
and
t
.

Then, consider the integral
H_{\gamma(0)}=H_{y}\hskip 0.85355pt
, and recall Formulae (
2.52
)–(
2.53
). For simplicity, assume that
M
is an irreducible symmetric space (see Chapter VIII of
[
10
]
, Page 307). In this case, according to
2.8.2
, there exists a maximal root
c\in\Delta_{+}\hskip 0.85355pt
, so that
c(a)\geq\lambda(a)
for all
\lambda\in\Delta_{+}
and
a\in Q_{+}\hskip 0.56917pt
. Therefore, it follows from (
2.53
) that

\|H_{y}(x)\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}\leq(\dim\,M)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\max\{1,|c(a)\cot c(a)|\}

(2.59)

where
\|_{\cdot}\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}
denotes the Frobenius norm given by the Riemannian metric of
M
. Now, the required uniform integrability is equivalent to the statement that

\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\,\int_{\mathrm{D}(\gamma)}\,\|H_{y}(x)\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathbf{1}\{\|H_{y}(x)\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}>K\}\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)=0\;\;\text{uniformly in $y$}

(2.60)

But from the inequality in (
2.59
), if
K>1
there exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\{\|H_{y}(x)\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}>K\}\subset\{c(a)>\pi-\epsilon\}

and
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
as
K\rightarrow\infty
. In this case, the integral in (
2.60
) is less than

(\dim\,M)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sup\hskip 0.56917ptp_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(x)\right)\int_{\mathrm{D}(\gamma)}\,|c(a)\cot c(a)|\hskip 0.85355pt\mathbf{1}\{c(a)>\pi-\epsilon\}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(2.61)

By expressing this integral as in (
2.52
), it is seen to be equal to

\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{Q_{+}}\!\int_{S}\,|c(a)\cot c(a)|\hskip 0.85355pt\mathbf{1}\{c(a)>\pi-\epsilon\}\hskip 0.56917ptD(a)\hskip 0.85355ptda\hskip 0.56917pt\omega(ds)\,=\\[5.69046pt]
\omega(S)\,\int_{Q_{+}}\left[|c(a)\cot c(a)|\hskip 0.56917ptD(a)\right]\mathbf{1}\{c(a)>\pi-\epsilon\}\hskip 0.56917ptda\end{array}

In view, of (
2.54
), since
c\in\Delta_{+}\hskip 0.56917pt
, the function in square brackets is bounded on the closure of
Q_{+}
by
c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}=\|c\|^{2}
(incidentally, this is the maximum sectional curvature of
M
, as explained in
2.8.2
). Finally, by (
2.61
), the integral in (
2.60
) is less than

(\dim\,M)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sup\hskip 0.56917ptp_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(x)\right)\omega(S)\hskip 0.56917ptc^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}\,\int_{Q_{+}}\mathbf{1}\{c(a)>\pi-\epsilon\}\hskip 0.56917ptda

Recall that
c(a)\in(0,\pi)
for
a\in Q_{+}\hskip 0.85355pt
. It is then clear this last integral converges to
0
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, at a rate which does not depend on
y
. This proves the required uniform integrability, so the proof is now complete, at least in the case where
M
is an irreducible symmetric space.

In the general case, where
M
is not irreducible, it is enough to note that, according to
2.8.2
,
M
is a product of irreducible Riemannian symmetric spaces,
M=M_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\times\ldots\times M_{s\hskip 0.85355pt}
. Then, the proof boils down to the special case where
M
is irreducible, as treated above.

2.9.3

Proof of Lemma
2.1

The proof uses the following general remark.

Remark :
let
M
be a Riemannian manifold, and
g:M\rightarrow M
be an isometry. Recall that
g\cdot y
is used to denote
g(y)
, for
y\in M
. Similarly, if
A\subset M
, let
g\cdot A
denote the image of
A
under
g
. Then, for any
y\in M
,
\mathrm{Cut}(g\cdot y)=g\cdot\mathrm{Cut}(y)
.
This is because a point
x\in M
belongs to
\mathrm{Cut}(y)
, if and only if
x
is a first conjugate point to
y
along some geodesic, or there exist two different length-minimising geodesics connecting
y
to
x
, and because both of these properties are preserved by any isometry
g
.

Assume
M
is a simply connected compact Riemannian symmetric space. In the notation of
2.8
,
M\simeq G/K
. Recall (by Proposition
1.10
of
1.10
) any geodesic
\gamma:I\rightarrow M
is given by

\gamma(t)=\exp(t\hskip 0.56917pt\omega)\cdot y

(2.62)

for some
y\in M
and
\omega\in\mathfrak{p}
, where
\exp
denotes the Lie group exponential. From the above remark, for each
t\in I
, the cut locus
\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma(t))
of
\gamma(t)
is given by

\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma(t))=\exp(t\hskip 0.56917pt\omega)\cdot\mathrm{Cut}(y)

(2.63)

However,
\mathrm{Cut}(y)
is described by (
2.26
) in
2.8.2
, which reads

\mathrm{Cut}(y)=\varphi(S\times\bar{Q}_{\pi})

(2.64)

in terms of the mapping
\varphi
defined in (
2.25
). It follows from (
2.63
) and (
2.64
) that

\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)=\Phi(I\times S\times\bar{Q}_{\pi})\hskip 14.22636pt\Phi(t,s,a)=\exp(t\hskip 0.56917pt\omega)\cdot\varphi(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)

(2.65)

The aim is to show that this set has Hausdorff dimension strictly less than
\dim\,M
. This is done using results from dimension theory
[
32
]
. Precisely, note from (
2.26
) that

\bar{Q}_{\pi}=\cup_{\ell}\,\bar{Q}_{\ell}\hskip 11.38092pt\text{where }\bar{Q}_{\ell}=\bar{Q}_{+}\,\cap\,\{a:c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\}

Therefore, it is clear that

\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)=\bigcup_{\ell}\Phi(I\times S\times\bar{Q}_{\ell})

(2.66)

Then, it follows from
[
32
]
(Item (2) of Theorem 2) that

\mathrm{dim}_{\scriptscriptstyle H}\,\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)\leq\max_{\ell}\,\mathrm{dim}_{\scriptscriptstyle H}\,\Phi(I\times S\times\bar{Q}_{\ell})

(2.67)

where
\mathrm{dim}_{\scriptscriptstyle H}
is used to denote the Hausdorff dimension. Now, for each
\ell
,

\Phi(I\times S\times\bar{Q}_{\ell})=\Phi(I\times S_{\ell}\times\bar{Q}_{\ell})\subset\Phi(\mathbb{R}\times S_{\ell}\times\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\})

where
S_{\ell}=K/K_{\ell}
with
K_{\ell}
the centraliser of
\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\}
in
K
. This last inclusion implies
[
32
]
(Item (1) of Theorem 2)

\mathrm{dim}_{\scriptscriptstyle H}\,\Phi(I\times S\times\bar{Q}_{\ell})\leq\mathrm{dim}_{\scriptscriptstyle H}\,\Phi(\mathbb{R}\times S_{\ell}\times\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\})

(2.68)

To conclude, note that the set
\mathbb{R}\times S_{\ell}\times\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\}
is a differentiable manifold. Let this differentiable manifold be equipped with a product Riemannian metric (arising from flat metrics on
\mathbb{R}
and
\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\}
, and from the invariant metric induced onto
S_{\ell}
from
K
). It is clear from (
2.65
) that
\Phi
is smooth, and therefore locally Lipschitz. Then
[
32
]
(Item (5) of Theorem 2),

\mathrm{dim}_{\scriptscriptstyle H}\,\Phi(\mathbb{R}\times S_{\ell}\times\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\})\leq\mathrm{dim}_{\scriptscriptstyle H}\,(\mathbb{R}\times S_{\ell}\times\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\})

(2.69)

But the Hausdorff dimension of a Riemannian manifold is the same as its (usual) dimension. Accordingly,

\mathrm{dim}_{\scriptscriptstyle H}\,(\mathbb{R}\times S_{\ell}\times\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\})=1+\dim\,S_{\ell}+(\dim\,\mathfrak{a}-1)

since the dimension of a hyperplane in
\mathfrak{a}
is
\dim\,\mathfrak{a}-1
. In addition, from
[
10
]
(Page 253),
\dim\,S_{\ell}<\dim\,S
. Therefore,

\mathrm{dim}_{\scriptscriptstyle H}\,(\mathbb{R}\times S_{\ell}\times\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)=\pi\})=\dim\,S_{\ell}+\dim\,\mathfrak{a}<\dim\,M

since
\dim\,M=\dim\,S+\dim\,\mathfrak{a}
, as can be seen from (
2.25
). Replacing this into (
2.69
), it follows from (
2.67
) and (
2.68
) that
\dim\,\mathrm{Cut}(\gamma)<\dim\,M
. The lemma has therefore been proved.

2.9.4

Proof of Proposition
2.3

Assume that Lemma
2.2
is true. This lemma is proved in
2.9.5
, below.

Proof of (i)

For
\delta<\frac{1}{2}r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx\hskip 0.85355pt}
, let
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
be given by (
2.18
). By (ii) of Lemma
2.2
,
T<T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
implies the variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
is strongly convex on
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. It will be proved that any Riemannian barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
of
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
belongs to
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. Then, since
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is a minimum of
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
in

, it follows that
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is unique (thanks to the strong convexity of
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
).

By (i) of Proposition
2.1
, to prove that any

belongs to

, it is enough to prove

W(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T\hskip 0.56917pt},\delta_{x^{*}})<\frac{\delta^{2}}{4\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{diam}\,M}

(2.70)

However, if
T<T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
then
T<T_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
and, by (ii) of Proposition
2.1
,

W(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T\hskip 0.56917pt},\delta_{x^{*}})
satisfies inequality (
2.11
). In addition (from the definition of

and
