) one has
T<T^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
and

(2\pi)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.56917pt(T/\mu_{\min})^{\!\frac{1}{2}}<\delta^{2}\hskip 0.85355pt(\mu_{\min}/\mu_{\max})^{\!\frac{n}{2}}\hskip 0.85355ptD_{n}

By replacing the expression of
D_{n}
and simplifying, this is the same as

(2\pi)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptB^{-1}_{n}\hskip 0.56917pt(\pi/2)^{n-1}\hskip 0.85355pt(\mu_{\max}/\mu_{\min})^{\!\frac{n}{2}}(T/\mu_{\min})^{\!\frac{1}{2}}<\frac{\delta^{2}}{4\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{diam}\,M}

(2.71)

Now, (
2.70
) follows from (
2.11
) and (
2.71
).

Proof of (ii)

From the proof of (i),
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
is strongly convex on
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
, and
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is the minimum of

\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
in
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. To prove that
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=x^{*}
, it is then enough to prove that
x^{*}
is a stationary point of
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
. However, the fact that
U
is invariant by the geodesic symmetry
s_{x^{*}}
will be seen to imply

d\hskip 0.56917pts_{x^{*}}\cdot G_{x^{*}}=G_{x^{*}}

(2.72)

which is equivalent to
G_{x^{*}}=0
, since
d\hskip 0.56917pts_{x^{*}}
is equal to minus the identity on
T_{x^{*}}M
(see
1.9.2
). Then, by (
2.13
) in Proposition
2.2
,
\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(x^{*})=0
, so
x^{*}
is indeed a stationary point of
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
.

To obtain (
2.72
), it is possible to write from (
2.12
),

d\hskip 0.56917pts_{x^{*}}\cdot G_{x^{*}}=d\hskip 0.56917pts_{x^{*}}\cdot\int_{\mathrm{D}(x^{*})}\,G_{x^{*}}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.73)

But, it follows from (
2.25
) and (
2.27
) that
x=\mathrm{Exp}_{x^{*}}(-G_{x^{*}}(x))
. Then, since
s_{x^{*}}
reverses geodesics through
x^{*}
,

d\hskip 0.56917pts_{x^{*}}\cdot G_{x^{*}}(x)=G_{x^{*}}(s_{x^{*}}\cdot x)

Replacing this into (
2.74
), and introducing the new variable of integration
z=s_{x^{*}}\cdot x
3
3

3

\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\circ s_{x^{*}}
is the image of the distribution
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
under the map
s_{x^{*}}:M\rightarrow M
. In other places of this thesis, this would be noted
(s_{x^{*}})^{*}\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T\hskip 0.85355pt}
, but this notation seems kind of clumsy, in the present case.
,

d\hskip 0.56917pts_{x^{*}}\cdot G_{x^{*}}=\int_{\mathrm{D}(x^{*})}\,G_{x^{*}}(z)\hskip 0.56917pt(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\circ s_{x^{*}})(dz)

(2.74)

since
s^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}_{x^{*}}=s_{x^{*}}
and
s_{x^{*}}
maps
\mathrm{D}(x^{*})
onto itself. Now, note that
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\circ s_{x^{*}}=\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T\hskip 0.85355pt}
. This is clear, since from (
2.7
),

(\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}\circ s_{x^{*}})(dz)=\left(Z(T)\right)^{-1}\exp\left[-\frac{(U\circ s_{x^{*}})(z)}{T}\right](\mathrm{vol}\circ s_{x^{*}})(dz)

However, by assumption,
(U\circ s_{x^{*}})(z)=U(z)
, since
U
is invariant by geodesic symmetry about
x^{*}
. Moreover, because
s_{x^{*}}
is an isometry, it must preserve Riemannian volume, so

(\mathrm{vol}\circ s_{x^{*}})(dz)=\mathrm{vol}(dz)
. Thus, (
2.74
) reads

d\hskip 0.56917pts_{x^{*}}\cdot G_{x^{*}}=\int_{\mathrm{D}(x^{*})}\,G_{x^{*}}(z)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dz)

From (
2.12
), the right-hand side is
G_{x^{*}\hskip 0.85355pt}
, so (
2.72
) is obtained. From (
2.72
), since
d\hskip 0.56917pts_{x^{*}}=-\mathrm{Id}_{x^{*}}\hskip 0.85355pt
, and
G_{x^{*}}
belongs to

,

G_{x^{*}}=-\,G_{x^{*}}

Of course, this means

, as required.

2.9.5

Proof of Lemma
2.2

Proof of (i)

Let
y\in B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
where
\delta<\frac{1}{2}r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx\hskip 0.85355pt}
. Now, recall that
\mathrm{Hess}\,\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)=H_{y}
for all
y\in B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. Then, from (
2.12
), it is possible to write

H_{y}\,=\int_{B(y\hskip 0.56917pt,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx})}\,H_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)+\int_{\mathrm{D}(y)-B(y\hskip 0.56917pt,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx})}\,H_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.75)

Indeed,
B(y\hskip 0.56917pt,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx})\subset\mathrm{D}(y)
, since the injectivity radius of
M
is
2r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}
as given in
2.8.2
. The first integral in (
2.75
) will be denoted
I_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and the second integral
I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2\hskip 0.85355pt}
.

With regard to
I_{\scriptscriptstyle 1\hskip 0.85355pt}
, note the inclusions
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)\subset B(y\hskip 0.56917pt,2\delta)\subset B(y\hskip 0.56917pt,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx})
, which follow from the triangle inequality. In addition, note that
H_{y}(x)\geq 0
for
x\in B(y\hskip 0.56917pt,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx})
. Therefore,

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\geq\int_{B(x^{*}\!,\delta)}\,H_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.76)

where
H_{y}(x)
is given by (
2.28
). But, from (
2.28
); the eigenvalues of
H_{y}(x)
are

\lambda(a)\cot\lambda(a)\geq\min_{\ell}\,c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\cot c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)

(2.77)

where the maximal roots
c_{\ell}
were introduced before (
2.24
). By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\leq\|c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}\|\hskip 0.56917pt\|a\|\leq c\|a\|
, where
c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
denotes the maximum sectional curvature of
M
, whose expression was recalled in
2.8.2
. Now, if
x\in B(y\hskip 0.56917pt,2\delta)
, then
\|a\|=d(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x)<2\delta
, and it follows from (
2.77
) that

H_{y}(x)\geq\min_{\ell}\,c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\cot c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\geq 2c\delta\hskip 0.56917pt\cot(2c\delta)=\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)>0

(2.78)

where the last inequality is because
2c\delta<\frac{\pi}{2}
. Replacing in (
2.76
) gives

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\geq\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(B(x^{*}\!,\delta))=\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)[1-\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(B^{\scriptscriptstyle c}(x^{*}\!,\delta))]

Finally, (
2.44
) and (
2.45
) imply that
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(B^{\scriptscriptstyle c}(x^{*}\!,\delta))\leq\mathrm{vol}(M)\hskip 0.56917ptf(T)
, where
f(T)
was defined in (
2.14
) (precisely, this follows after replacing
\rho
by
\delta
in (
2.44
)).
Thus,

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\geq\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)[1-\mathrm{vol}(M)\hskip 0.56917ptf(T)]

(2.79)

The proof of (
2.15
) will be completed by showing

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\geq-\pi A_{\scriptscriptstyle M}\hskip 0.85355ptf(T)

(2.80)

To do so, introduce the function

k(a)=\min_{\ell}\,c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\cot c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for $a\in Q_{+}$}

(2.81)

and note using (
2.77
) that

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\geq\int_{\mathrm{D}(y)-B(y\hskip 0.56917pt,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx})}\,k(a)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)\geq\int_{\mathrm{D}(y)}\,\mathbf{1}\{k(a)\leq 0\}k(a)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(dx)

(2.82)

Indeed, the set of
a
such that
k(a)\leq 0
is contained in
\mathrm{D}(y)-B(y\hskip 0.56917pt,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx})
, because

\{k(a)\leq 0\}=\cup_{\ell}\hskip 0.85355pt\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\cot c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\leq 0\}=\cup_{\ell}\hskip 0.85355pt\{c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\geq\pi/2\}

(2.83)

and
c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\geq\pi/2
implies
d(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x)=\|a\|\geq\frac{\pi}{2}\hskip 0.85355ptc^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}=r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx}
(by Cauchy-Schwarz). By expressing the last integral in (
2.82
) as in (
2.52
), it is seen to be equal to

\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{Q_{+}}\!\int_{S}\,\mathbf{1}\{k(a)\leq 0\}k(a)\hskip 0.56917ptp_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)\hskip 0.56917ptD(a)\hskip 0.85355ptda\hskip 0.56917pt\omega(ds)\geq\\[8.5359pt]
-\pi\,\int_{Q_{+}}\!\int_{S}\,\mathbf{1}\{k(a)\leq 0\}\hskip 0.56917ptp_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)\hskip 0.85355ptda\hskip 0.56917pt\omega(ds)\end{array}

Indeed, it follows from (
2.54
) and (
2.81
) that
k(a)\hskip 0.56917ptD(a)\geq\min_{\ell}-c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)
, and this is greater than
-\pi
because
c_{\scriptscriptstyle\ell}(a)\in(0,\pi)
for
a\in Q_{+}\hskip 0.56917pt
. Now, (
2.82
) implies

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\geq-\pi\,\int_{Q_{+}}\!\int_{S}\,\mathbf{1}\{k(a)\leq 0\}\hskip 0.56917ptp_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)\hskip 0.85355ptda\hskip 0.56917pt\omega(ds)

(2.84)

As seen from (
2.83
), the set
\{k(a)\leq 0\}\subset B^{\scriptscriptstyle c}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx})\subset B^{\scriptscriptstyle c}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. On the other hand,
p_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(x)\leq f(T)
for
x\in B^{\scriptscriptstyle c}(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. Replacing in (
2.84
),

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\geq-\pi f(T)\,\int_{Q_{+}}\!\int_{S}\,da\hskip 0.56917pt\omega(ds)

(2.85)

The double integral on the right-hand side is a positive constant which depends only on the symmetric space
M
. Denoting this by
A_{\scriptscriptstyle M}
yields (
2.80
).

Proof of (ii)

Let
\delta<\frac{1}{2}r_{\scriptscriptstyle cx\hskip 0.85355pt}
. According to (
2.15
), which has just been proved

\mathrm{Hess}\,\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)\,\geq\,\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)\hskip 0.85355pt[1-\mathrm{vol}(M)f(T)]-\pi A_{\scriptscriptstyle M}\hskip 0.85355ptf(T)

(2.86)

for all
y\in B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
. Now, let
T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
be given by (
2.18
). It follows from the definition of
T^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
that
T<T_{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}
implies

f(T)<\frac{\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)}{\mathrm{Ct}(2\delta)\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{vol}(M)+\pi\hskip 0.56917ptA_{\scriptscriptstyle M}}

(2.87)

This amounts to saying the right-hand side of (
2.86
) is strictly positive. Since this is independent of
y
, it is clear that the variance function
\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle T}(y)
is indeed strongly convex on
B(x^{*}\!,\delta)
.

Chapter 3

Gaussian distributions and RMT

Gaussian distributions on Riemannian symmetric spaces were introduced in
[
36
]
. The present chapter expands on this work in several ways. In particular, it uncovers and exploits the connection between Gaussian distributions on Riemannian symmetric spaces and random matrix theory (RMT).

•

3.1
attempts to answer the seemingly easy question
what is a Gaussian distribution ?
, by adopting a historical perspective (the source material used is from
[
37
]
[
38
]
[
39
]
).

•

3.2
defines Gaussian distributions as a family of distributions on a Riemannian manifold, for which
maximum-likelihood estimation is equivalent to the Riemannian barycentre problem
.

•

3.3
expresses the normalising factor of a Gaussian distribution on a Riemannian symmetric space, which belongs to the non-compact case, under the form of a multiple integral. It also discusses analytic and numerical evaluation of this multiple integral.

•

3.4
proves existence and uniqueness of maximum-likelihood estimates for Gaussian distributions, defined on a Hadamard manifold which is also a homogeneous space. It also shows that these distributions maximise Shannon entropy, for given barycentre and dispersion.

•

3.5
describes the Riemannian barycentre and the covariance tensor of a Gaussian distribution.

•

3.6
and draws on random matrix theory to obtain an analytic formula for the normalising factor of a Gaussian distribution, on the space
\mathrm{H}(N)
of
N\times N
Hermitian positive-definite matrices.

•

3.7
and
3.8
study large
N
asymptotics of Gaussian distributions on
\mathrm{H}(N)
. In particular,
3.7
provides an asymptotic expression of the normalising factor.

•

3.9
introduces
\Theta
distributions, a family of distributions, on the unitary group
U(N)
(which is the dual symmetric space of
\mathrm{H}(N))
, with a remarkable connection to Gaussian distributions on
\mathrm{H}(N)
.

3.1

From Gauss to Shannon

The story of Gaussian distributions is a story of discovery and re-discovery. Different scientists, at different times, were repeatedly lead to these distributions, through different routes.

In 1801, on New Year’s day, Giuseppe Piazzi sighted a heavenly body (in fact, the asteroid Ceres), which he thought to be a new planet. Less than six weeks later, this “new planet” disappeared behind the sun. Using a method of least squares, Gauss predicted the area in the sky, where it re-appeared one year later. His justification of this method of least squares (cast in modern language) is that measurement errors follow a family of distributions, which satisfies

property 1 :
maximum-likelihood estimation is equivalent to the least-squares problem.

In an 1809 paper, he used this property to show that the distribution of measurement errors is (again, in modern language) a Gaussian distribution.

In 1810, Laplace studied the distribution of a quantity, which is the aggregate of a great number of elementary observations. He was lead in this (completely different) way, to the same distribution discovered by Gauss. Laplace was among the first scientists to show

property 2 :
the distribution of the sum of a large number of elementary observations is (asymptotically) a Gaussian distribution.

Around 1860, Maxwell rediscovered Gaussian distributions, through his investigation of the velocity distribution of particles in an ideal gas (which he viewed as freely colliding perfect elastic spheres). Essentially, he showed that

property 3 :
the distribution of a rotationally-invariant random vector, which has independent components, is a Gaussian distribution.
1
1

1

A deeper version of Maxwell’s idea was obtained by Poincaré and Borel, around 1912, who showed that : if
v=(v_{n}\,;n=1,\ldots,N)
is uniformly distributed, on an
(N-1)
-dimensional sphere of radius
N^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then the distribution of
v_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
is (asymptotically) a Gaussian distribution. This is Poincaré’s model of the one-dimensional ideal gas, with
N
particles.

Kinetic theory lead to another fascinating development, related to Gaussian distributions. Around 1905, Einstein (and, independently, Smoluchowsky) showed that

property 4 :
the distribution of the position of a particle, which is undergoing a Brownian motion, is a Gaussian distribution.

In addition to kinetic theory, alternative routes to Gaussian distributions have been found in quantum mechanics, information theory, and other fields.
In quantum mechanics, a Gaussian distribution is a position distribution with minimum uncertainty. That is, it achieves equality in Heisenberg’s inequality (this is because a Gaussian function is proportional to its own Fourier transform). In information theory, one may attribute to Shannon the following maximum entropy characterisation

property 5 :
a probability distribution with maximum entropy, among all distributions with a given mean and variance, is a Gaussian distribution.

The above list of re-discoveries of Gaussian distributions, by means of different definitions, may be extended much longer. However, the main point is the following. In Euclidean space, any one of the above five properties leads to the same famous expression of a Gaussian distribution,

P(dx|\bar{x},\sigma)=\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!-\frac{n}{2}}\exp\left[-\frac{(x-\bar{x})^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]dx

In non-Euclidean space, each one of these properties may lead to a different kind of distribution, which may then be called a Gaussian distribution, but only from a more restricted point of view. People interested in Brownian motion may call the heat kernel of a Riemannian manifold a Gaussian distribution on that manifold. However, statisticians will not like this definition, since it will (in general) fail to have a straightforward connection to maximum-likelihood estimation.

Going from Euclidean to non-Euclidean spaces, the concept of Gaussian distribution breaks down into several different concepts. At best, one may define Gaussian distributions based on a practical motivation, which makes one (or more) of their classical properties seem more advantageous than the others.

3.2

The “right” Gaussian

As of now, the following definition of Gaussian distributions is chosen. Gaussian distributions, on a Riemannian manifold
M
, are a family of distributions
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
, parameterised by
\bar{x}\in M
and
\sigma>0
, such that : a maximum-likelihood estimate
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
of
\bar{x}
, based on independent samples
(x_{n}\,;n=1,\ldots,N)
from
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
, is a solution of the least-squares problem

\text{minimise over $x\in M$}\hskip 8.5359pt\mathcal{E}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}(x)=\sum^{N}_{n=1}d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x_{n}\hskip 0.56917pt,x)

Of course, this is the same least-squares problem as (
2.2
), so
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
is an empirical barycentre of the samples
(x_{n})
. Therefore (as discussed in
2.2.4
),
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
is almost-surely unique, if
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
has a probability density with respect to the Riemannian volume of
M
(this will indeed be the case).
Now, consider the density profile

f(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,=\,\exp\left[-\frac{d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,\bar{x})}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]

(3.1)

and the normalising factor,

Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)=\int_{M}\,f(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(3.2)

If this is finite, then

P(dx|\bar{x},\sigma)\,=\,\left(Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\right)^{-1}\hskip 0.85355ptf(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 1.13791pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(3.3)

is a well-defined probability distribution on
M
. In
3.4
, below, it will be shown that
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
, as defined by (
3.3
), is indeed a Gaussian distribution, if
M
is a Hadamard manifold, and also a homogeneous space. The following propositions will then be helpful.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
M
be a Hadamard manifold, whose sectional curvatures lie in
[\kappa\hskip 0.85355pt,0]
, where
\kappa=-c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
. Then, for any
\bar{x}\in M
and
\sigma>0
, if
Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
is given by (
3.2
),

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\sigma)\leq\,Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,\leq Z_{\scriptscriptstyle c}(\sigma)

(3.4)

where
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\sigma)=\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!\frac{n}{2}}
and
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle c}(\sigma)
is positive and given by (recall
n
is the dimension of
M
)

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle c}(\sigma)=\omega_{n-1}\hskip 0.56917pt\frac{\sigma}{(2c)^{n-1}}\hskip 0.85355pt\sum^{n-1}_{k=0}(-1)^{k}\hskip 0.85355pt\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n-1\\
k\end{array}\!\!\right)\frac{\Phi\left((n-1-2k)\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma c\right)}{\mathstrut\Phi^{\prime}\left((n-1-2k)\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma c\right)}

(3.5)

with
\omega_{n-1}
the area of the unit sphere
S^{\hskip 0.56917ptn-1}
, and
\Phi
the standard normal distribution function.

Proposition 3.2
.

If
M
is a Riemannian homogeneous space, and
Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
is given by (
3.2
), then
Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
does not depend on
\bar{x}
. In other words,
Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)=Z(\sigma)
.

If
M
is a Hadamard manifold, and also a homogeneous space, then both Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
apply to
M
. Indeed, if
M
is a Riemannian homogeneous space, then its sectional curvatures lie within a bounded subset of the real line. Therefore, Proposition
3.1
implies
Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
is finite for all
\bar{x}\in M
and
\sigma>0
. On the other hand, Proposition
3.2
implies that
Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)=Z(\sigma)
.

Thus, if
M
is a Hadamard manifold, and also a homogeneous space, then (
3.3
), reduces to

P(dx|\bar{x},\sigma)\,=\,\left(Z(\sigma)\right)^{-1}\hskip 0.85355pt\exp\left[-\frac{d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,\bar{x})}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]\hskip 1.13791pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(3.6)

and yields a well-defined probability distribution
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
on
M
. This will be the main focus, throughout the following.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
:
(
3.4
) is a direct application of (
1.94
). Let
f(y)=f(y|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
, and
\kappa_{\max}=0
,
\kappa_{\min}=\kappa
. Also, since
M
is a Hadamard manifold, note that
\min\{\mathrm{inj}(\bar{x})\hskip 0.85355pt,\pi\hskip 0.85355ptc^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\}=\infty
. Therefore, (
1.94
) (applied with
x=\bar{x}
), yields

\omega_{n-1}\,\int^{\infty}_{0}\,\exp\left[-\frac{r^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{\mathstrut 2\sigma^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\right]\mathrm{sn}^{n-1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(r)\hskip 0.56917ptdr\leq\,Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,\leq\omega_{n-1}\,\int^{\infty}_{0}\,\exp\left[-\frac{r^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{\mathstrut 2\sigma^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\right]\mathrm{sn}^{n-1}_{\kappa}(r)\hskip 0.56917ptdr

However,
\mathrm{sn}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(r)=r
and
\mathrm{sn}_{\kappa}(r)=c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\hskip 0.56917pt\sinh(c\hskip 0.85355ptr)
. Therefore, the expression for
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\sigma)
follows easily. For
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle c}(\sigma)
, on the other hand, note that

\int^{\infty}_{0}\,\exp\left[-\frac{r^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{\mathstrut 2\sigma^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\right]\mathrm{sn}^{n-1}_{\kappa}(r)\hskip 0.56917ptdr=\frac{1}{(2c)^{n-1}}\hskip 0.85355pt\int^{\infty}_{0}\,\exp\left[-\frac{r^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{\mathstrut 2\sigma^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\right]\left(e^{c\hskip 0.85355ptr}-e^{-c\hskip 0.85355ptr\hskip 0.85355pt}\right)^{n-1}\hskip 0.85355ptdr

Then, (
3.5
) follows by performing a binomial expansion, and using

\int^{\infty}_{0}\,\exp\left[-\frac{r^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{\mathstrut 2\sigma^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}+(n-1-2k)\hskip 0.85355ptc\hskip 0.85355ptr\right]dr=\sigma\,\frac{\Phi\left((n-1-2k)\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma c\right)}{\mathstrut\Phi^{\prime}\left((n-1-2k)\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma c\right)}

Remark :
clearly,
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\sigma)
is the normalising factor of a Gaussian distribution, when
M
is a Euclidean space,
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}\,
. On the other hand,
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle c}(\sigma)
is the normalising factor of a Gaussian distribution, when
M
is a hyperbolic space of dimension
n
, and constant negative curvature
\kappa=-c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
. This will become clear in
3.3
, below.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
:
assume
M
is a homogeneous space, and fix some point
o\in M
. There exists an isometry
g
of
M
such that
g\cdot\bar{x}=o
. In the integral (
3.2
), introduce the new variable of integration
z=g\cdot x
. Since
g
(being an isometry) preserves Riemannian volume,

Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)=\int_{M}\,f(g^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\cdot z|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,\mathrm{vol}(dz)=\int_{M}\,f(z|o\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,\mathrm{vol}(dz)=Z(o\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)

where the second equality follows from (
3.1
). Thus,
Z(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)=Z(o\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
does not depend on
\bar{x}
.

3.3

The normalising factor
Z(\sigma)

Assume now
M=G/K
is a Riemannian symmetric space which belongs to the non-compact case, described in
1.9.2
. In particular,
M
is a Hadamard manifold, and also a homogeneous space. Thus, for each
\bar{x}\in M
and
\sigma>0
, there is a well-defined probability distribution
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
on
M
, given by (
3.6
). Here, the normalising factor
Z(\sigma)
can be expressed as a multiple integral, using the integral formula (
1.109
), of Proposition
1.7
. Applying this proposition (with
o=\bar{x}
), it is enough to note

f(\varphi(s\hskip 0.56917pt,a)|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)=\exp\left[-\frac{\|a\|^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]

where
\|a\|^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}=B(a,a)
, in terms of the
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant symmetric bilinear form
B
. Since this expression only depends on
a
, it is possible to integrate
s
out of (
1.109
), to obtain

Z(\sigma)\,=\,\frac{\omega(S)}{|W|}\,\int_{\mathfrak{a}}\exp\left[-\frac{\|a\|^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]\prod_{\lambda\in\Delta_{+}}\left|\sinh\hskip 0.56917pt\lambda(a)\right|^{m_{\lambda}}\hskip 0.85355ptda

(3.7)

This formula expresses the normalising factor
Z(\sigma)
as a multiple integral on the vector space
\mathfrak{a}
.

Example 1 :
the easiet instance of (
3.7
) arises when
M
is a hyperbolic space of dimension
n
, and constant sectional curvature equal to
-1
. Then,
M
has rank equal to
1
, so that
\mathfrak{a}=\mathbb{R}\hskip 0.85355pt\hat{a}
for some unit vector
\hat{a}\in\mathfrak{a}
. Since the sectional curvature is equal to
-1
, there is only one positive root
\lambda
, say
\lambda(\hat{a})=1
, with multiplicity
m_{\lambda}=n-1
. In addition, there are two Weyl chambers,
C_{+}=\{t\hskip 0.56917pt\hat{a}\,;t>0\}
and
C_{-}=\{t\hskip 0.56917pt\hat{a}\,;t<0\}
. In other words,
|W|=2
. Now, (
3.7
) reads

Z(\sigma)\,=\,\frac{\omega_{n-1}}{2}\,\int^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\exp\left[-\frac{r^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{\mathstrut 2\sigma^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\right]\left|\sinh(r)\right|^{n-1}\hskip 0.85355ptdr=\omega_{n-1}\,\int^{+\infty}_{0}\exp\left[-\frac{r^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{\mathstrut 2\sigma^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\right]\sinh^{n-1}(r)\hskip 0.85355ptdr

In general, if all distances are divided by
c>0
, the sectional curvature
-1
is replaced by
-c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
. Thus, when
M
is a hyperbolic space of dimension
n
, and sectional curvature
-c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, one has

Z(\sigma)=\omega_{n-1}\,\int^{+\infty}_{0}\exp\left[-\frac{r^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{\mathstrut 2\sigma^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\right](c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\hskip 0.56917pt\sinh(c\hskip 0.85355ptr))^{n-1}\hskip 0.85355ptdr

This is exactly
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle c}(\sigma)
, expressed analytically in (
3.5
).

Example 2 :
another example, also susceptible of analytic expression, is when
M
is a cone of positive-definite matrices (covariance matrices), with real, complex, or quaternion coefficients. Then,
M=G/K
with
G=\mathrm{GL}(N,\mathbb{K})
, where
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{H}
(real numbers, complex numbers, or quaternions), and
K
is a maximal compact subgroup of
G
, say
K=U(N),O(N)
or
Sp(n)
.
In each of these three cases,
\mathfrak{a}
is the space of
N\times N
real diagonal matrices, and the positive roots are the linear maps
\lambda(a)=a_{ii}-a_{jj}
where
i<j
, each one having its multiplicity
m_{\lambda}=\beta
, (
\beta=1,2
or
4
, according to
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{H}
). In addition,
\|a\|^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}=4\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{tr}(a^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})=4\hskip 0.56917pta^{{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 11}+\ldots+4\hskip 0.56917pta^{{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}_{\scriptscriptstyle NN\,}
.

The Weyl group
W
is the groupe of permutation matrices in
K
, so
|W|=N!
. Finally,
S=K/T_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
where
T_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
is the subgroup of all matrices
t
which are diagonal and belong to
K
. Replacing all of this into (
3.7
), it follows that

2
2

2

Curious readers will want to compute
\omega_{\beta}(N)
. But, how ? For example,
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)
can be found using the Weyl integral formula on U(N)
[
40
]
. This yields
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)=\mathrm{vol}(U\!(N))/(2\pi)^{N}
, where the volume of the unitary group
U(N)
is
\mathrm{vol}(U\!(N))=(2\pi)^{(N^{2}+N)/2}/G(N)
, in terms of
G(N)=1!\times 2!\times\ldots\times(N-1)!
, which can be found, just by looking at the normalising factor of a Gaussian unitary ensemble.

Z(\sigma)=\frac{\omega_{\beta}(N)}{N!}\,\int_{\mathfrak{a}}\,\prod^{N}_{i=1}\,\exp\left[-\frac{2\hskip 0.56917pta^{{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}_{\scriptscriptstyle ii}}{\sigma^{2}}\right]\prod_{i<j}\left|\sinh(a_{ii}-a_{jj})\right|^{\beta}\hskip 0.85355ptda

(3.8)

where
\omega_{\beta}(N)
stands for
\omega(S)
, and
da=da_{\scriptscriptstyle 11}\ldots da_{\scriptscriptstyle NN\,}
. Passing to the variables
x_{i}=\exp(2a_{\scriptscriptstyle ii})
,

Z(\sigma)=\frac{\omega_{\beta}(N)}{\mathstrut 2^{\scriptscriptstyle NN_{\beta}}N!}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}_{+}}\,\prod^{N}_{i=1}\left[\rho(x_{i}\hskip 0.56917pt,2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\hskip 0.85355ptx^{\!-N_{\beta}\,}_{i}\right]\hskip 0.85355pt|V(x)|^{\beta}\hskip 0.85355pt\prod^{N}_{i=1}\hskip 0.56917ptdx_{i}

where
N_{\beta}=(\beta/2)(N-1)+1
,
\rho(x,k)=\exp(-\log^{2}(x)/k)
and
V(x)=\prod_{i<j}(x_{j}-x_{i})
is the Vandermonde determinant. Finally, using the elementary identity

\rho(x,k)\hskip 0.85355ptx^{\alpha}=\exp\left[\frac{k}{4}\hskip 0.85355pt\alpha^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\right]\omega\left(e^{-\frac{k}{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\alpha}\hskip 0.56917ptx\hskip 0.56917pt,k\right)

it is immediately found that

Z(\sigma)=\frac{\omega_{\beta}(N)}{\mathstrut 2^{\scriptscriptstyle NN_{\beta}}N!}\times\exp\left[-N\hskip 0.56917ptN^{2}_{\beta}\hskip 1.13791pt(\sigma^{2}\!/2)\right]\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}_{+}}\,\prod^{N}_{i=1}\rho(u_{i}\hskip 0.56917pt,2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\,|V(u)|^{\beta}\,\prod^{N}_{i=1}\hskip 0.56917ptdu_{i}

(3.9)

For the case
\beta=2
, the integral in (
3.9
) will be expressed analytically in
3.6
, below. The cases
\beta=1,4
should be pursued using the techniques in
[
17
]
(Chapter 5).

Example 3 :
for this last example, I am still unaware of any valid means of analytic expression. Let
M=\mathrm{D}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
be the Siegel domain
[
41
]
. This is the set of
N\times N
symmetric complex matrices
z
, such that
\mathrm{I}_{N}-z^{\dagger}z\geq 0
(where the inequality is understood in the sense of the Loewner order). Here,
M=G/K
, where
G\simeq\mathrm{Sp}(N,\mathbb{R})
(real symplectic group) and
K\simeq U(N)
(unitary group). Precisely,
G
is the group of all
2N\times 2N
complex matrices
g
, with
g^{\mathrm{t}}\hskip 0.56917pt\Omega\hskip 0.56917ptg=\Omega
and
g^{\dagger}\hskip 0.56917pt\Gamma\hskip 0.56917ptg=\Gamma
, where
t
denotes the transpose, and where
\Omega
and
\Gamma
are the matrices

\Omega=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}&\mathrm{I}_{N}\\[2.84544pt]
-\mathrm{I}_{N}&\end{array}\!\right)\hskip 7.11317pt;\hskip 7.11317pt\Gamma=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{I}_{N}&\\[2.84544pt]
&-\mathrm{I}_{N}\end{array}\!\right)

In addition,
K
is the group of all block-diagonal matrices
k=\mathrm{diag}(U,U^{*})
where
U\in U(N)
, and
∗
denotes the conjugate. The action of
G
on
M
is given by the matrix analogue of Möbius transformations,

g\cdot z=(A\hskip 0.56917ptz+B)(C\hskip 0.56917ptz+D)^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\hskip 14.22636ptg=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\[2.84544pt]
C&D\end{array}\,\right)

(3.10)

This action preserves the Siegel metric, which is defined by

\langle v,\!v\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle z}=\|(\mathrm{I}_{N}-z\hskip 0.56917ptz^{\dagger})^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\hskip 0.85355ptv\hskip 0.85355pt\|^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}\hskip 28.45274pt\|v\|^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{tr}(v\hskip 0.56917ptv^{\dagger})

(3.11)

where each tangent vector
v
is identified with a symmetric complex matrix (with this metric, it is easy to see that geodesic symmetry at
0\in\mathrm{D}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
is given by
s_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(z)=-z
for
z\in\mathrm{D}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
). Now
[
42
]
,

\mathfrak{a}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}&a\\[2.84544pt]
a&\end{array}\right)\,;\hskip 1.42271pta=\mathrm{diag}(a_{\scriptscriptstyle 11},\ldots,a_{\scriptscriptstyle NN})\right\}

(3.12)

The positive roots are
\lambda(a)=a_{ii}-a_{jj}
for
i<j
, and
\lambda(a)=a_{ii}+a_{jj}
for
i\leq j
, all with
m_{\lambda}=1
. The order of the Weyl group is
|W|=2^{N}N!
, and
\omega(S)=\mathrm{vol}(U(N))/2^{N}
. Replacing into (
3.7
),

Z(\sigma)\,=\,\frac{\mathrm{vol}(U(N))}{2^{\scriptscriptstyle 2N}N!}\,\int_{\mathfrak{a}}\,\prod^{N}_{i=1}\,\exp\left[-\frac{\hskip 0.56917pta^{{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}_{\scriptscriptstyle ii}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]\prod_{i<j}\sinh|a_{ii}-a_{jj}|\prod_{i\leq j}\sinh|a_{ii}+a_{jj}|\,da

(3.13)

In
[
36
]
, a special Monte Carlo method, for computing (
3.13
) was indicated (this method is owed to Paolo Zanini). The idea is the following : if
\mathbf{a}
is a random variable with normal distribution, of mean zero and covariance
\sigma^{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{I}_{N}
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then
Z(\sigma)
is given by the expectation,

Z(\sigma)\,=\,\frac{\mathrm{vol}(U(N))}{2^{\scriptscriptstyle 2N}N!}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i<j}\sinh|\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{a}_{j}|\prod_{i\leq j}\sinh|\mathbf{a}_{i}+\mathbf{a}_{j}|\right]

(3.14)

For a given value of
\sigma
, this is easily approximated by an empirical average. However, it then remains to guarantee that
Z(\sigma)
is a well-behaved function of
\sigma
. Precisely (see Proposition
3.4
in
3.4
), if

\eta=(-2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, then
\psi(\eta)=\log\hskip 0.56917ptZ(\sigma)
is a strictly convex function, from the half-line
(-\infty,0)
onto
\mathbb{R}
. By approximating
Z(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle n})
at certain nodes
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle n\,}
, and then performing a suitable spline interpolation, it becomes possible to guarantee this behavior of
\psi(\eta)
.

This Monte Carlo method applies, with very little modification, not only to the computation of (
3.13
), but to the computation of the general formula (
3.7
). It has been used to produce tables of the function
Z(\sigma)
, for various Riemannian symmetric spaces
M
, of rank
N
up to
30
, which have been successfully used, in numerical computation (recall the rank of
M
is the dimension of
\mathfrak{a}
). Unfortunately, this method breaks down, when
N
is larger (roughly
\approx 50
). Either an analytic expression of
Z(\sigma)
(see
3.6
, below), or an asymptotic formula, for large
N
(see
3.7
, below), are then needed.

3.4

MLE and maximum entropy

Let
M
be a Hadamard manifold, which is also a homogeneous space. Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
then imply that, for any
\bar{x}\in M
and
\sigma>0
, there exists a well-defined probability distribution
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
on
M
, given by (
3.6
). The family of distributions
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
fits the definition of Gaussian distributions, stated at the beginning of
3.2
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
be given by (
3.6
), for
\bar{x}\in M
and
\sigma>0
. The maximum-likelihood estimate of the parameter
\bar{x}
, based on independent samples
(x_{n}\,;n=1,\ldots,N)
from
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
, is unique and equal to the empirical barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
of the samples
(x_{n})
.

The proof of this proposition is immediate. From (
3.6
), one has the log-likelihood function

\ell(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,=\,-N\log Z(\sigma)-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\sum^{N}_{n=1}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x_{n}\hskip 0.56917pt,\bar{x})

(3.15)

Since the first term does not depend on
\bar{x}
, one may maximise
\ell(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
, first over
\bar{x}
and then over
\sigma
. Clearly, maximising over
\bar{x}
is equivalent to minimising the sum of squared distances
d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x_{n}\hskip 0.56917pt,\bar{x})
. This is just the least-squares problem (
2.2
), whose solution is the empirical barycentre
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}\,
. Moreover,
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
is unique, since
M
is a Hadamard manifold (as discussed in
2.2.2
)

Consider now maximum-likelihood estimation of
\sigma
. This is better carried out in terms of the natural parameter
\eta=(-2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, or in terms of the moment parameter
\delta=\psi^{\prime}(\eta)
, where
\psi(\eta)=\log\hskip 0.56917ptZ(\sigma)
and the prime denotes the derivative.

Proposition 3.4
.

The function
\psi(\eta)
, just defined, is a strictly convex function, which maps the half-line
(-\infty,0)
onto
\mathbb{R}
. The maximum-likelihood estimates of the parameters
\eta
and
\delta
are

\hat{\eta}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}=(\psi^{\prime})^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(\hat{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle N})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\hat{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x_{n}\hskip 0.56917pt,\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle N})

(3.16)

where
(\psi^{\prime})^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
denotes the reciprocal function.

The proof of this proposition is given below. For now, note the maximum-entropy property of Gaussian distributions, stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.5
.

The Gaussian distribution
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
, given by (
3.6
), is the unique distribution on
M
, having maximum Shannon entropy, among all distributions with given barycentre
\bar{x}
and dispersion
\delta=\mathbb{E}_{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle x\sim P}[d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,\bar{x})]
. Its entropy is equal to
\psi^{*}(\delta)
where
\psi^{*}
is the Legendre transform of
\psi
.

Proof of Proposition
3.4
:
denote
\mu
the image of the distribution
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
under the mapping
x\mapsto d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,\bar{x})
. Then,
\psi(\eta)
is the cumulant generating function of
\mu
,

\psi(\eta)=\log\,\int^{\infty}_{0}\hskip 0.28436pte^{\eta\hskip 0.56917pts}\mu(ds)

(3.17)

and is therefore strictly convex. Note from (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) that
Z(\sigma)=0
when
\sigma=0
and
Z(\sigma)
increases to
+\infty
when
\sigma
increases to
+\infty
. Recalling
\eta=(-2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
and
\psi(\eta)=\log\hskip 0.56917ptZ(\sigma)
, it becomes clear that
\psi
is (in fact, strictly increasing, and) maps the half-line
(-\infty,0)
onto
\mathbb{R}
.

After maximisation with respect to
\bar{x}
, the log-likelihood function (
3.15
) becomes,

\ell(\eta)\,=\,N\left\{\eta\hskip 0.85355pt\hat{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}-\psi(\eta)\right\}

(3.18)

which is a strictly concave function. Differentiating, and setting the derivative equal to
0
, directly yields the maximum-likelihood estimates (
3.16
).

Remark :

\hat{\eta}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
in (
3.16
) is well-defined, since the range of
\psi^{\prime}
is equal to
(0,\infty)
. Indeed, it is possible to use (
1.94
), as in the proof of (
3.4
), to show that

\psi^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\eta)\,\leq\psi^{\prime}(\eta)\leq\,\psi^{\prime}_{c}(\eta)

(3.19)

where
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\eta)=\log Z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\sigma)
, and
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle c}(\eta)=\log Z_{\scriptscriptstyle c}(\sigma)
, with
\kappa=-c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}
a lower bound on the sectional curvatures of
M
. Precisely, (
3.19
) can be obtained by replacing
f(y)=d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,\bar{x})\hskip 0.85355ptp(y|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
into (
1.94
), where
p(y|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
is the probability density function in (
3.6
). Now,
\psi^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\eta)=n\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, which increases to
+\infty
when
\sigma
increases to
+\infty
. On the other hand, by a straightforward application of the chain rule, it is seen that

\psi^{\prime}_{c}(\eta)\,=\,\sigma^{3}\hskip 0.85355pt\frac{d}{d\sigma}\!\left(\log Z_{\scriptscriptstyle c}(\sigma)\right)

(3.20)

which, from (
3.5
), is
=0
when
\sigma=0
. Now, it follows from (
3.19
),
\psi^{\prime}
maps the half-line
(-\infty,0)
onto the half-line
(0,+\infty)
.

Proof of Proposition
3.5
:
let
Q(dx)
be a probability distribution on
M
with barycentre
\bar{x}
and dispersion
\delta=\mathbb{E}_{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle x\sim Q}[d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,\bar{x})]
. Assume
Q(dx)
has probablity density function
q(x)
, with respect to Riemannian volume. The Shannon entropy of
Q
is given by

S(q)\,=\,\int_{M}\,\log(q(x))\hskip 0.56917ptq(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(3.21)

Since
M
is a homogeneous space,
S(q)
does not depend on
\bar{x}
. Fixing some point
o\in M
, it is possible to assume, without loss of generality, that
\bar{x}=o
. Then, it is enough to maximise
S(q)
, subject to the constraints,

\int_{M}\,q(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)\,=\,1\hskip 11.38092pt\text{ and }\hskip 8.5359pt\int_{M}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,o)\hskip 0.85355ptq(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)\,=\,\delta

Using the method of Lagrange multipliers, this leads to a stationary point

q(x)\,=\,\exp\left(\hskip 0.56917pt\eta\hskip 0.85355ptd^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,o)-\psi(\eta)\right)

(3.22)

where the Lagrange multiplier
\eta
is finally given by
\eta=(\psi^{\prime})^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(\delta)
, in terms of the cumulant generating function,

\psi(\eta)=\log\,\int_{M}\exp\left(\eta\hskip 0.85355ptd^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,o)\right)\mathrm{vol}(dx)

Of course,
q(x)
in (
3.22
) is just
p(x|o\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
, once the parameter
\sigma>0
is defined by
\eta=(-2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
. Since the Shannon entropy is strictly concave, this stationary point
q(x)
is a unique maximum, over the (convex) set of probability density functions on
M
, which satisfy the above constraints. Its entropy is equal to

S(q)\,=\,\int_{M}\left(\hskip 0.56917pt\eta\hskip 0.85355ptd^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,o)-\psi(\eta)\right)q(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)=\eta\hskip 0.56917pt\delta-\psi(\eta)

(3.23)

To show that this is
\psi^{*}(\delta)
, as stated in the proposition, it is enough to show

S(q)\,=\,\sup_{\eta}\hskip 0.85355pt\{\eta\hskip 0.56917pt\delta-\psi(\eta)\}

(3.24)

However, since
\psi
is a strictly convex function, it is seen by differentiation that the
\sup
is achieved when
\psi^{\prime}(\eta)=\delta
, exactly as in (
3.22
). Accordingly, the right-hand side of (
3.24
) is equal to
\eta\hskip 0.56917pt\delta-\psi(\eta)
, as in (
3.23
).

3.5

Barycentre and covariance

3.5.1

The Riemannian barycentre

Let
M
be a Hadamard manifold, which is also a homogeneous space. Here, it is shown that the barycentre of the Gaussian distribution
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
on
M
, given by (
3.6
), is equal to
\bar{x}
.

First, it should be noted
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
does indeed have a well-defined Riemannian barycentre, since it has finite second-order moments. To see that this is true, it is enough to note that

\int_{M}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,x)\hskip 0.85355ptp(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)\,<\,\infty

Ineded, this integral is just
\psi^{\prime}(\eta)
in (
3.19
). This means
\pi=P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
satisfies (
2.4
) for
y_{o}=\bar{x}
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
be given by (
3.6
), for
\bar{x}\in M
and
\sigma>0
. The Riemannian barycentre of

P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
is equal to
\bar{x}
.

First proof :
the proof of this proposition relies on the fact that the variance function,

\mathcal{E}(y)\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x)\hskip 0.85355ptp(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

is
1/2
-strongly convex. In particular, it has a unique stationary point,
\hat{x}
with
\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\hat{x})=0
, which is also its unique global minimum, and (by definition) the Riemannian barycentre of
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
. Now, let
f(\bar{x})
be the function given by

f(\bar{x})\,=\,\int_{M}\,p(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

Clearly, this is a constant function, equal to
1
for all
\bar{x}
. On the other hand, its gradient may be written down, by differentiating under the integral, with respect to
\bar{x}
, using (
1.75
) and (
3.6
),

\mathrm{grad}\,f(\bar{x})\,=\,\sigma^{-2}\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x)\hskip 1.13791ptp(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

Now,
\mathrm{grad}\,f(\bar{x})
is identically zero. But, the right-hand side of the above expression is equal to
-\sigma^{-2}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})
, by (
2.5
). This shows that
\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})=0
, and therefore
\bar{x}
is the Riemannian barycentre of
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
.

Second proof :
this proof works if
M
is a Riemannnian symmetric space which belongs to the non-compact case. From (
2.5
),

\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})\,=\,-\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x)\hskip 1.13791ptp(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

Let
s_{\bar{x}}
be the geodesic symmetry at
\bar{x}
. From the definition of
s_{\bar{x}\,}
,
s_{\bar{x}}\cdot\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})=-\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})
. On the other hand,

s_{\bar{x}}\cdot\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})\,=\,-\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M}\left(s_{\bar{x}}\cdot\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x)\right)p(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

Since
s_{\bar{x}}
is an isometry and fixes
\bar{x}
, it follows that

s_{\bar{x}}\cdot\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x)=\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(s_{\bar{x}}\cdot x)\text{ and }p(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)=p(s_{\bar{x}}\cdot x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)

Therefore,

s_{\bar{x}}\cdot\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})\,=\,-\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(s_{\bar{x}}\cdot x)\hskip 0.85355ptp(s_{\bar{x}}\cdot x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

and, introducing the variable of integration
z=s_{\bar{x}}\cdot x
, it follows that
s_{\bar{x}}\cdot\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})=\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})
.
Now, it has been shown that
s_{\bar{x}}\cdot\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})=-\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})
and that
s_{\bar{x}}\cdot\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})=\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})
. Thus,
\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}(\bar{x})=0
and one may conclude as in the first proof.

3.5.2

The covariance tensor

The covariance form of the distribution
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
is the symmetric bilinear form
C_{\bar{x}}
on
T_{\bar{x}}M
,

C_{\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt}(u\hskip 0.56917pt,v)\,=\,\int_{M}\,\langle u\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x)\rangle\hskip 0.85355pt\langle\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x),v\rangle\,p(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)\hskip 28.45274ptu\,,v\in T_{\bar{x}}M

(3.25)

With
\sigma>0
fixed, the map which assigns to
\bar{x}\in M
the covariance form
C_{\bar{x}}
is a (0,2)-tensor field on
M
, here called the covariance tensor of
P(\bar{x}\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
. In order to compute this tensor field, consider the following situation.

Assume
M=G/K
is a Riemannian symmetric space which belongs to the non-compact case. Here,
K=K_{o}\hskip 1.13791pt
, the stabiliser in
G
of
o\in M
. For
k\in K
and
u\in T_{o}M
, it is clear
k\cdot u\in T_{o}M
. This defines a representation of
K
in the tangent space
T_{o}M
, called the isotropy representation. One says that
M
is an irreducible symmetric space, if this isotropy representation is irreducible.

If
M
is not irreducible, then it is a product of irreducible Riemannian symmetric spaces
M=M_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\times\ldots\times M_{s}

[
10
]
(Proposition 5.5, Chapter VIII. This is the de Rham decomposition of
M
).
Accordingly, for
x\in M
and
u\in T_{x}M
, one may write
x=(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,x_{s})
and
u=(u_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,u_{s})
, where
x_{r}\in M_{r}
and
u_{r}\in T_{x_{r}}M_{r}\hskip 1.13791pt
. Now, looking back at (
3.6
), it may be seen that

p(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,=\,\prod^{s}_{r=1}p(x_{r}|\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 28.45274ptp(x_{r}|\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,=\,\left(Z_{r}(\sigma)\right)^{-1}\hskip 0.85355pt\exp\left[-\frac{d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\bar{x}_{r})}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]

(3.26)

For the following proposition, let
\eta=(-2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
and
\psi_{r}(\eta)=\log Z_{r}(\sigma)
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Assume that
M
is a product of irreducible Riemannian symmetric spaces,
M=M_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\times\ldots\times M_{s\hskip 1.13791pt}
. The covariance tensor
C
in (
3.25
) is given by

C_{\bar{x}}(u\hskip 0.56917pt,u)\,=\,\sum^{s}_{r=1}\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{r}(\eta)}{\dim\hskip 0.85355ptM_{r}}\hskip 1.13791pt\|u_{r}\|^{2}_{\bar{x}_{r}}

(3.27)

for
u\in T_{\bar{x}}M
where
\bar{x}=(\bar{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,\bar{x}_{s})
and
u=(u_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,u_{s})
, with
\bar{x}_{r}\in M_{r}
and
u_{r}\in T_{\bar{x}_{r}}M_{r}\hskip 1.13791pt
.

Example :
let
M=\mathrm{H}(N)
, so
M=\mathrm{GL}(N,\mathbb{C})/U(N)
, with
U(N)
the stabiliser of
o=\mathrm{I}_{N}\,
. The de Rham decomposition of
M
is
M=M_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\times M_{\scriptscriptstyle 2\hskip 1.13791pt}
, where
M_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=\mathbb{R}
and
M_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is the submanifold whose elements are those
x\in M
such that
\det(x)=1
. Accordingly, each
\bar{x}\in M
is identified with the couple
(\bar{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\hskip 0.56917pt,\bar{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
,

\bar{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\,=\,\frac{1}{N}\log\det(\bar{x})\hskip 14.22636pt\bar{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\,=\,(\det(\bar{x}))^{-{\scriptscriptstyle 1/N}}\hskip 0.85355pt\bar{x}

and each
u\in T_{\bar{x}}M
is written
u=u_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\hskip 0.56917pt\bar{x}+u_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}

u_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\,=\,\frac{1}{N}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{tr}(\bar{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\hskip 0.56917ptu)\hskip 14.22636ptu_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\,=\,u-\frac{1}{N}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{tr}(\bar{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\hskip 0.56917ptu)\hskip 1.13791pt\bar{x}

These may be replaced into expression (
3.27
),

C_{\bar{x}}(u\hskip 0.56917pt,u)\,=\,\psi^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\eta)\hskip 0.85355ptu^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\,+\,\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\eta)}{N^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}-1}\hskip 0.56917pt\|u_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\|^{2}_{\bar{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}

(3.28)

where
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\eta)=\log\left(2\pi\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}
, and
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\eta)=\log Z(\sigma)-\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\eta)
(
Z(\sigma)
is given by (
3.35
) in
3.6
, below). After a direct calculation, this can be brought under the form

C_{\bar{x}}(u\hskip 0.56917pt,u)\,=\,g_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{tr}^{2}(\bar{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\hskip 0.56917ptu)+g_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{tr}(\bar{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\hskip 0.56917ptu)^{2}

(3.29)

where
g_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\sigma)
and
g_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\sigma)
are certain functions of
\sigma
.

Remark :
as a corollary of Proposition
3.7
, the covariance tensor
C
is a
G
-invariant Riemannian metric on
M
. This is clear, for example, in the special case of (
3.29
), which coincides with the general expression of a
\mathrm{GL}(N,\mathbb{C})
-invariant metric.

Proof of Proposition
3.7
:
since
C_{\bar{x}}
is bilinear

C_{\bar{x}}(u\hskip 0.56917pt,u)\,=\,\sum^{s}_{r=1}\sum^{s}_{q=1}\,C_{\bar{x}}(u_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,u_{q})

(3.30)

It will be shown that

C_{\bar{x}}(u_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,u_{q})=0\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for }r\neq q

(3.31)

and, on the other hand, that

C_{\bar{x}}(u_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,u_{r})=\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{r}(\eta)}{\dim\hskip 0.85355ptM_{r}}\hskip 1.13791pt\|u_{r}\|^{2}_{\bar{x}_{r}}

(3.32)

Then, (
3.27
) will follow immediately, by replacing (
3.31
) and (
3.32
) into (
3.30
).

Proof of (
3.31
) :
from (
3.25
),

C_{\bar{x}}(u_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,u_{q})\,=\,\int_{M}\,\langle u_{r}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x)\rangle\hskip 0.85355pt\langle\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x),u_{q}\rangle\,p(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(3.33)

However, since
M
is given as a product Riemannian manifold,

\langle u_{r}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x)\rangle\,=\,\langle u_{r}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}_{r}}(x_{r})\rangle\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046pt\langle u_{q}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}}(x)\rangle\,=\,\langle u_{q}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}_{q}}(x_{q})\rangle

(3.34)

Using (
3.26
) and (
3.34
), it follows from (
3.33
) that

\begin{array}[]{rl}C_{\bar{x}}(u_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,u_{q})=&\int_{\scriptscriptstyle M_{r}}\langle u_{r}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}_{r}}(x_{r})\rangle\hskip 0.85355ptp(x_{r}|\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx_{r})\int_{\scriptscriptstyle M_{q}}\langle u_{q}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}_{q}}(x_{q})\rangle\hskip 0.85355ptp(x_{q}|\bar{x}_{q}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx_{q})\\[5.69046pt]
=&\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}_{r}(\bar{x}_{r})\,\mathrm{grad}\,\mathcal{E}_{q}(\bar{x}_{q})\\[5.69046pt]
=&0\end{array}

where the second equality follows from (
2.5
), applied to the variance functions

\mathcal{E}_{r}(y)\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M_{r}}d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x_{r})\hskip 0.85355ptp(x_{r}|\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx_{r})\hskip 2.84544pt\text{and}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{E}_{q}(y)\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M_{q}}d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x_{q})\hskip 0.85355ptp(x_{q}|\bar{x}_{q}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx_{q})

which, by Proposition
3.6
, respectively have their global minima at
\bar{x}_{r}
and
\bar{x}_{q}\hskip 1.13791pt
.

Proof of (
3.32
) :
let
K_{\bar{x}}
denote the stabiliser of
\bar{x}
in
G
. For
k\in K_{\bar{x}}
and
u_{r}\in T_{\bar{x}_{r}}M_{r}\hskip 1.13791pt
, note that
k\cdot u_{r}\in T_{\bar{x}_{r}}M_{r}\hskip 1.13791pt
. This defines an
irreducible
representation of
K_{\bar{x}}
in
T_{\bar{x}_{r}}M_{r}\hskip 1.13791pt
. The symmetric bilinear form
C_{\bar{x}}
is invariant under this representation. Precisely, since any
k\in K_{\bar{x}}
is an isometry which fixes
\bar{x}
, it follows from (
3.25
),

\begin{array}[]{rll}C_{\bar{x}}(k\cdot u_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,k\cdot u_{r})=&\int_{\scriptscriptstyle M_{r}}\,\langle k\cdot u_{r}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}_{r}}(x_{r})\rangle^{2}\,p(x_{r}|\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx_{r})&\\[5.69046pt]
=&\int_{\scriptscriptstyle M_{r}}\,\langle u_{r}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}_{r}}(k^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\cdot x_{r})\rangle^{2}\,p(x_{r}|\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx_{r})&\\[5.69046pt]
=&\int_{\scriptscriptstyle M_{r}}\,\langle u_{r}\hskip 0.85355pt,\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}_{r}}(k^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\cdot x_{r})\rangle^{2}\,p(k^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\cdot x_{r}|\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx_{r})=&\!\!C_{\bar{x}}(u_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,u_{r})\end{array}

where the last equality follows by introducing the new variable of integration
z=k^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\cdot x_{r}\hskip 1.13791pt
. Finally, from Schur’s lemma
[
40
]
,
C_{\bar{x}}
is a multiple of the metric,

C_{\bar{x}}(u_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,u_{r})\,=\,f(\eta)\,\hskip 1.13791pt\|u_{r}\|^{2}_{\bar{x}_{r}}

where
f(\eta)
may be found from
\mathrm{tr}(C_{\bar{x}})\,=\,(\dim\hskip 0.56917ptM_{r})\hskip 0.56917ptf(\eta)
. To conclude, it is enough to note that
the trace may be evaluated by introducing an orthonormal basis of
T_{\bar{x}_{r}}M_{r}\hskip 1.13791pt
. It then follows that,

\mathrm{tr}(C_{\bar{x}})\,=\,\int_{M_{r}}\,\|\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{\bar{x}_{r}}(x_{r})\|^{2}\hskip 0.85355ptp(x_{r}|\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx_{r})\,=\,\int_{M_{r}}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\hskip 0.85355ptx_{r})\hskip 0.85355ptp(x_{r}|\bar{x}_{r}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx_{r})

which is equal to
\psi^{\prime}_{r}(\eta)
, by the same argument as in the discussion before Proposition
3.6
.

3.6

An analytic formula for
Z(\sigma)

Consider the special case where
M=\mathrm{H}(N)
, which corresponds to
\beta=2
in Example 2 of
3.3
. In this case, using the tools of random matrix theory (see
[
17
]
, Chapter 5), it is possible to provide an analytic formula for the normalising factor
Z(\sigma)
.

Proposition 3.8
.

When
M=\mathrm{H}(N)
, the normalising factor
Z(\sigma)
, given by (
3.9
) with
\beta=2
, admits of the following analytic formula

Z(\sigma)=\frac{\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)}{\mathstrut 2^{\scriptscriptstyle N^{2}}}\left(2\pi\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!\frac{N}{2}}\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left[{\small\left(\frac{N^{3}-N}{6}\right)}\sigma^{2}\right]\prod^{N-1}_{n=1}\left(1-e^{-n\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\!N-n}

(3.35)

Remark :
when
N=2
, (
3.35
) reduces to

Z(\sigma)=\left(\frac{\pi\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma}{2}\right)^{\!2}\left(e^{\sigma^{2}}-1\right)

(3.36)

which can be checked, by directly calculating the integral (
3.9
).

Proof of Proposition
3.8
:
putting
\beta=2
in (
3.9
), and noting that
N_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=N
, it follows that

Z(\sigma)\,=\,\frac{\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)}{\mathstrut 2^{\scriptscriptstyle N^{2}}N!}\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left[-\frac{N^{3}}{2}\hskip 1.13791pt\sigma^{2}\right]\times I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}

(3.37)

where
I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is the integral

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}_{+}}\,\prod^{N}_{i=1}\rho(u_{i}\hskip 0.56917pt,2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\,|V(u)|^{2}\,\prod^{N}_{i=1}\hskip 0.56917ptdu_{i}

(3.38)

This can be expressed using a well-known formula from random matrix theory
[
17
]
(Chapter 5, Page 79). Precisely, if
(p_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}\,;n=0,1,\ldots)
are orthonormal polynomials, with respect to the weight function
\rho(u\hskip 0.56917pt,2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\hskip 1.13791pt
, then
I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is given by

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\,=\,N!\hskip 0.85355pt\prod^{N-1}_{n=0}p^{-2}_{\hskip 0.56917ptnn}

(3.39)

where
p_{\hskip 0.56917ptnn}
is the leading coefficient in
p_{\hskip 0.56917ptn\hskip 1.13791pt}
. The required orthonormal polynomials
p_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}
are given by
p_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}=(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}\hskip 0.85355pts_{\hskip 0.56917ptn\hskip 1.13791pt}
, where
s_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}
are the Stieltjes-Wigert polynomials
[
43
]
(Page 33). Accordingly,

p^{-2}_{\hskip 0.56917ptnn}\,=\,\left(2\pi\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left[\frac{(2n+1)^{2}}{2}\hskip 1.13791pt\sigma^{2}\right]\prod^{n}_{m=1}\left(1-e^{-m\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}}\right)

Then, working out the product (
3.39
), it easily follows

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\,=\,N!\hskip 0.85355pt\left(2\pi\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!\frac{N}{2}}\hskip 0.56917pt\exp\left[{\small\left(\frac{4N^{3}-N}{6}\right)}\hskip 1.13791pt\sigma^{2}\right]\prod^{N-1}_{n=1}\left(1-e^{-n\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\!N-n}

(3.40)

and (
3.35
) may be obtained by replacing this into (
3.37
).

Remark :
the product appearing in (
3.40
) can be written as a product of
q
-Gamma functions. Letting
q=e^{-\sigma^{2}}
, and recalling the definition of the
q
-Gamma function
[
44
]
, it may be seen that

\prod^{N-1}_{n=1}\left(1-e^{-n\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\!N-n}\,=\,(1-q)^{\scriptscriptstyle(N^{2}-N)/2}\hskip 0.56917pt\prod^{N}_{n=2}\Gamma_{q\hskip 0.56917pt}(n)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{($\Gamma_{q}$ the $q$-Gamma function)}

(3.41)

In other words, the product of
q
-Gamma functions plays, for the present problem, the same role that the product of classical Gamma functions (known as the Barnes function) plays, for the Gaussian unitary ensemble.

3.7

Large
N
asymptotics

Pursuing the development started in
3.6
, it is possible to derive an asymptotic expression of
Z(\sigma)
, valid in the limit where
N
goes to infinity, while the product
t=N\sigma^{2}
remains constant.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
Z(\sigma)
be given by (
3.35
). If
N\rightarrow\infty
, while
t=N\sigma^{2}
remains constant, then the following equivalence holds,

\frac{1}{N^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\log Z(\sigma)\sim-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{2N}{\pi}\right)+\frac{3}{4}+\frac{t}{6}-\frac{\mathrm{Li}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(e^{-t})-\zeta(3)}{t^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}

(3.42)

where
\mathrm{Li}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(x)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}x^{k}/k^{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
for
|x|<1
(the trilogarithm), and
\zeta
is the Riemann Zeta function.

The proposition follows by a direct calculation, once the following lemmas have been shown.

Lemma 3.1
.

In the notation of (
3.35
), if
N\rightarrow\infty
,

\frac{1}{N^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\log\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)\sim-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{N}{2\pi}\right)+\frac{3}{4}

(3.43)

Lemma 3.2
.

If
N\rightarrow\infty
, while
t=N\sigma^{2}
remains constant, then

\lim\hskip 1.13791pt\frac{1}{N^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\log\prod^{N-1}_{n=1}\left(1-e^{-n\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\!N-n}\,=\,\int^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(1-x)\log\left(1-e^{-tx}\right)\hskip 0.28436ptdx

(3.44)

and this improper integral is equal to
-(\mathrm{Li}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(e^{-t})-\zeta(3))/t^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
:
recall, from the footnote in
3.3
, that

\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)\,=\,(2\pi)^{(N^{2}-N)/2}/G(N)\hskip 11.38092pt\text{where }G(N)=1!\times 2!\times\ldots\times(N-1)!

Then, from the asymptotic formula of the Barnes function
[
33
]
(see Chapter XII, Exercice 49)

\log\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)\,=\,\frac{N^{2}}{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\log(2\pi)-N^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\log(N)-\frac{3}{4}\right]+o(N^{2})

which directly implies (
3.43
).

Proof of Lemma
3.2
:
taking the logarithm of the product, the left-hand side of (
3.44
) reads

\frac{1}{N^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\log\prod^{N-1}_{n=1}\left(1-e^{-n\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\!N-n}\,=\,\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N-1}_{n=1}\left(1-\frac{n}{N}\right)\log\left(1-e^{-t\hskip 0.56917pt\frac{n}{N}}\right)

which is a Riemann sum for the improper integral in the right-hand side. To evaluate this integral, one may resort to a symbolic computation software, or introduce the power series of the logarithm, under the integral,

\int^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(1-x)\log\left(1-e^{-tx}\right)\hskip 0.28436ptdx\,=\,-\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{1}{k}\hskip 0.85355pt\int^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(1-x)\hskip 0.85355pte^{-ktx}\hskip 0.28436ptdx

and note that

\int^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(1-x)\hskip 0.85355pte^{-ktx}\hskip 0.28436ptdx=\frac{1-e^{-ktx}}{(kt)^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}

in order to obtain
-(\mathrm{Li}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(e^{-t})-\zeta(3))/t^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
.

Remark :
from (
3.42
), it follows that
Z(\sigma)\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, while
t=N\sigma^{2}
remains constant. However, this is merely because
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, one should keep in mind,

\lim\hskip 1.13791pt\frac{1}{N^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\log\left[\frac{Z(\sigma)}{\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)}\right]\,=\,-\frac{1}{2}\log(2)+\frac{3}{4}+\frac{t}{6}-\frac{\mathrm{Li}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(e^{-t})-\zeta(3)}{t^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}

(3.45)

which may be thought of as the “asymptotic cumulant generating function”.

3.8

The asymptotic distribution

From the point of view of random matrix theory, a Gaussian distribution
P(\mathrm{I}_{N}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
on
M=\mathrm{H}(N)
defines a unitary matrix ensemble. If
x
is a random matrix, drawn from this ensemble, and
(x_{i}\,;i=1,\ldots,N)
are its eigenvalues, which all belong to
(0,\infty)
, then the empirical distribution
\nu_{\scriptscriptstyle N\hskip 0.85355pt}
, which is given by (as usual,
\delta_{x_{i}}
is the Dirac distribution at
x_{i}
)

\nu_{\scriptscriptstyle N}(B)=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum^{\scriptscriptstyle N}_{i=1}\delta_{x_{i}}(B)\right]

(3.46)

for measurable
B\subset(0,\infty)
, converges to an absolutely continuous distribution
\nu_{\scriptscriptstyle t\hskip 0.56917pt}
, when
N
goes to infinity, while the product
t=N\sigma^{2}
remains constant.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
c=e^{-t}
and
a(t)=c(1+\sqrt{1-c})^{\scriptscriptstyle-2}
while
b(t)=c(1-\sqrt{1-c})^{\scriptscriptstyle-2}
. When
N
goes to infinity, while the product
t=N\sigma^{2}
remains constant, the empirical distribution
\nu_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
converges weakly to the distribution
\nu_{\scriptscriptstyle t}
with probability density function

\frac{d\nu_{\scriptscriptstyle t}}{dx}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi\hskip 0.56917pttx}\arctan\left(\frac{4\hskip 0.56917pte^{t}x-(x+1)^{2}}{x+1}\right)\mathbf{1}_{[a(t),b(t)]}(x)

(3.47)

where
\mathbf{1}_{[a(t),b(t)]}
denotes the indicator function of the interval
[a(t),b(t)]
.

Remark :
as one should expect, when
t=0
(so
\sigma^{2}=0
),
a(t)=b(t)=1
.

The proof of Proposition
3.10
is a relatively direct application of a result in
[
45
]
(Page 191). Recall the variables
u_{i}=e^{t}x_{i}
which appear in (
3.9
). Let
\tilde{\nu}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
be the empirical distribution of the
u_{i}
(this is the same as (
3.46
), but with
u_{i}
instead of
x_{i}
). By applying
[
17
]
(Chapter 5, Page 81),

\tilde{\nu}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}(B)=\frac{1}{N}\hskip 0.56917pt\int_{B}R^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pt(1)}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}(u)\hskip 0.85355pt(du)

(3.48)

for measurable
B\subset(0,\infty)
, where the one-point correlation function
R^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pt(1)}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}(u)
is given by

R^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pt(1)}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}(u)=\rho(u\hskip 0.56917pt,2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\sum^{N-1}_{n=0}p^{2}_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}(u)

(3.49)

in the notation of
3.6
(
p_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}
are orthonormal polynomials, with respect to the weight
\rho(u\hskip 0.56917pt,2\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
). According to
[
46
]
(Page 133),
\tilde{\nu}_{\scriptscriptstyle N}
given by (
3.48
) converges weakly to the so-called equilibrium distribution
\tilde{\nu}_{\scriptscriptstyle t\hskip 0.56917pt}
, which minimises the electrostatic energy functional

E(\nu)=\frac{1}{t}\int^{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(u)\nu(du)-\int^{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\int^{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\log|u-v|\nu(du)\nu(dv)

(3.50)

over probability distributions
\nu
on
(0,\infty)
. Also according to
[
46
]
(Page 133), this equilibrium distribution is the asymptotic distribution of the zeros of the polynomial
p_{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle N}
(in the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
while
N\sigma^{2}=t
). Fortunately,
p_{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle N}
is just a constant multiple of the Stieltjes-Wigert polynomial
s_{\hskip 0.56917ptN}

[
43
]
(Page 33). Therefore, the required asymptotic distribution of zeros can be read from
[
45
]
(Page 191). Finally, (
3.47
) follows by introducing the change of variables
x=e^{-t}u
.

Remark :
in
[
47
]
, the equilibrium distribution
\tilde{\nu}_{\scriptscriptstyle t}
is derived directly, by searching for stationary distributions of the energy functional (
3.50
). This leads to a singular integral equation, whose solution reduces to a Riemann-Hilbert problem. Astoundingly, the Gaussian distributions on
\mathrm{H}(N)
, as introduced in the present chapter, provide a matrix model for Chern-Simons quantum field theory (a detailed account is given in
[
47
]
).

3.9

Duality : the
\Theta
distributions

Recall the Riemannian symmetric space
M=\mathrm{H}(N)
of
3.6
. Its dual space is the unitary group
M^{*}=U(N)
. Consider now a family of distributions on
M^{*}
, which will be called
\Theta
distributions, and which display an interesting connection with Gaussian distributions on
M
, studied in
3.6
.
Recall Jacobi’s
\vartheta
function
3
3

3

To follow the original notation of Jacobi
[
33
]
, this should be written
\vartheta(e^{i\phi}|q)
where
q=e^{-\sigma^{2}}
. In other popular notations, this function is called
\vartheta_{\scriptscriptstyle 00}
or
\vartheta_{\scriptscriptstyle 3\,}
.
,

\vartheta(e^{\scriptscriptstyle i\phi}|\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\,=\,\sum^{+\infty}_{m=-\infty}\exp(-m^{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}+2m\hskip 0.85355pti\phi)

As a function of
\phi
, up to some minor modifications, this is just a wrapped normal distribution (in other words, the heat kernel of the unit circle),

\frac{1}{2\pi}\hskip 0.85355pt\vartheta\!\left(e^{\scriptscriptstyle i\phi}|{\scriptstyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}}\right)\,=\,\hskip 0.85355pt\sum^{\infty}_{m=-\infty}\exp\left[-\frac{(2\phi-2m\pi)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]

Each
x\in M^{*}
can be written
x=k\cdot e^{i\theta}
for some
k\in U(N)
and
e^{i\theta}=\mathrm{diag}(e^{i\theta_{i}}\,;i=1,\ldots,N)
, where
k\cdot y=k\hskip 0.56917pty\hskip 0.56917ptk^{\dagger}
, for
y\in M^{*}
. With this notation, define the following matrix
\vartheta
function,

\Theta\left(x\middle|\sigma^{2}\right)\,=\,k\cdot\vartheta\!\left(e^{\scriptscriptstyle i\theta}|{\scriptstyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}}\right)

(3.51)

which is obtained from
x
by applying Jacobi’s
\vartheta
function to each eigenvalue of
x
. Further, consider the positive function,

f_{*}(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,=\,\det\left[\left(2\pi\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\Theta\!\left(x\bar{x}^{\dagger}\middle|\sigma^{2}\right)\right]

(3.52)

which is also equal to

\det\left[\left(2\pi\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.85355pt\Theta\!\left(\bar{x}^{\dagger}x\middle|\sigma^{2}\right)\right]

since the matrices
x\bar{x}^{\dagger}
and
\bar{x}^{\dagger}x
are similar. Then, let
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M^{*}}(\sigma)
denote the normalising constant

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M^{*}}(\sigma)=\int_{M^{*}}f_{*}(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(3.53)

which does not depend on
\bar{x}
, as can be seen, by introducing the new variable of integration
z=x\bar{x}^{\dagger}
, and using the invariance of
\mathrm{vol}(dx)
. (compare to the proof of Proposition
3.2
).

Now, define a
\Theta
distribution
\Theta(\bar{x},\sigma)
as the probability distribution on
M^{*}
, whose probability density function, with respect to
\mathrm{vol}(dx)
, is given by

p_{*}(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,=\,\left(Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M^{*}}(\sigma)\right)^{-1}\hskip 0.85355ptf_{*}(x|\bar{x}\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)

(3.54)

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M}(\sigma)=Z(\sigma)
, be given by (
3.35
), and
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M^{*}}(\sigma)
be given by (
3.53
). Then, the following equality holds

\frac{Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M}(\sigma)}{Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M^{*}}(\sigma)}=\exp\left[{\small\left(\frac{N^{3}-N}{6}\right)}\sigma^{2}\right]

(3.55)

Remark :
the Gaussian density (
3.6
) on
M
, and the
\Theta
distribution density (
3.54
) on
M^{*}
are apparently unrelated. Therefore, it is interesting to note their normalising constants
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M}(\sigma)
and
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M^{*}}(\sigma)
scale together according to the simple relation (
3.55
). The connection between the two distributions is due to the duality between the two spaces (
M
and
M^{*}
).

Proof of Proposition
3.11
:
since
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M^{*}}(\sigma)
does not depend on
\bar{x}
, one may set
\bar{x}=o
in (
3.53
), where
o=\mathrm{I}_{N}\hskip 0.85355pt
. Then,
f_{*}(x|o\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)
is a class function, so (
3.53
) can be computed using (
1.118
). Note that
\omega(S_{\scriptscriptstyle N})
, which appears in (
1.118
), is equal to
\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)
, in the current notation. Therefore,

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M^{*}}(\sigma)=\frac{\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)}{\mathstrut 2^{\scriptscriptstyle N^{2}}N!}\left(2\pi\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!\frac{N}{2}}\times I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}

(3.56)

where
I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is the integral

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\,=\,\int_{[0\hskip 0.56917pt,2\pi]^{N}}\,\prod^{N}_{i=1}\vartheta\!\left(e^{\scriptscriptstyle i\theta_{i}}|{\scriptstyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}}\right)|V(e^{i\theta})|^{2}\hskip 1.13791ptd\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\ldots\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle N}

(3.57)

which follows from the identity

\det\Theta\!\left(x\middle|\sigma^{2}\right)=\prod^{N}_{i=1}\vartheta\!\left(e^{\scriptscriptstyle i\theta_{i}}|{\scriptstyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}}\right)

Now,
I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
can be expressed using
[
17
]
(Chapter 5, Page 79), as in the proof of Proposition
3.8
. Precisely, if
(p_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}\,;n=0,1,\ldots)
are orthonormal trigonometric polynomials, with respect to the weight function
\vartheta\!\left(e^{\scriptscriptstyle i\theta}|{\scriptstyle\sigma^{2}\!/2}\right)
, on the unit circle, then
I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is given by (
3.39
),

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\,=\,N!\hskip 0.85355pt\prod^{N-1}_{n=0}p^{-2}_{\hskip 0.56917ptnn}

in terms of the leading coefficients
p_{\hskip 0.56917ptnn}
of the polynomials
p_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}
(these leading coefficients may always be chosen to be real). At present, the required orthonormal polynomials
p_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}
are given by

p_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}(z)\,=\,\left[q^{n}\!\prod^{n}_{m=1}(1-q^{m})^{-1}\right]^{\!\frac{1}{2}}r_{\hskip 0.56917ptn}(-q^{-\frac{1}{2}}z)

(3.58)

where
q=e^{-\sigma^{2}}
and
r_{n}(z)
is the
n
-th Rogers-Szegö polynomial, which is monic
[
48
]
. Therefore,

p^{-2}_{\hskip 0.56917ptnn}\,=\,\prod^{n}_{m=1}\left(1-e^{-m\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}}\right)

(3.59)

and, from (
3.57
),
I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is given by

I_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\,=\,N!\prod^{N-1}_{n=1}\left(1-e^{-n\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\!N-n}

(3.60)

which may be replaced into (
3.56
) to obtain

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle M^{*}}(\sigma)=\frac{\omega_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(N)}{\mathstrut 2^{\scriptscriptstyle N^{2}}}\left(2\pi\hskip 0.85355pt\sigma^{2}\right)^{\!\frac{N}{2}}\hskip 0.56917pt\prod^{N-1}_{n=1}\left(1-e^{-n\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\!N-n}

(3.61)

Finally, (
3.55
) follows easily, by comparing (
3.61
) to (
3.35
).

Remark :
the construction of the
\Theta
distributions seems to indicate a general construction of “dual distributions” on pairs of dual Riemannian symmetric spaces. Recalling the general notation of
1.9.2
, it seems that Gaussian distributions arise from a classical Gaussian density profile on the maximal Abelian subspace
\mathfrak{a}
, while
\Theta
distributions (“their duals”) arise from wrapping this Gaussian density profile around the torus
\mathrm{Exp}_{o}(i\hskip 0.56917pt\mathfrak{a})
.

Chapter 4

Bayesian inference and MCMC

The present chapter is entirely made up of previously unpublished material. It continues the study of Gaussian distributions, from the previous chapter, in a new direction : Bayesian inference, and the Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) techniques, useful in Bayesian inference.

•

4.1
introduces two Bayesian estimators, the MAP and the MMS, for Gaussian distributions on a Riemannian symmetric space
M
. Proposition
4.1
states these two estimators are equal, if the likelihood and prior densities are identical.

•

4.2
discusses a surprising experimental result : when
M
is a space of constant negative curvature, numerical computation shows the MAP and the MMS are so close to each other that they appear to be equal, even if the likelihood and prior densities are different.

•

4.3
states the original Proposition
4.2
, which provides easy-to-verify sufficient conditions, for the geometric ergodicity of an isotropic Metropolis-Hastings Markov chain, in a Riemannian symmetric space which belongs to the non-compact case. This is then applied to the computation of the MMS, via the subsequent Proposition
4.3
.

•

4.4
discusses the Riemanian gradient descent method. Proposition
4.4
states this method has an exponential rate of convergence, when used to find the global mnimum of a strongly convex function, defined on a Hadamard manifold. Propositions
4.2
,
4.3
, and
4.4
are the three essential ingredients of the recipe, used here for the computation of the MMS.

•

4.5
gives Lemma
4.4
, to be used in the proof of Proposition
4.2
. This lemma states that the logarithmic rate of growth of the volume density function is bounded at infinity, for a Riemannian symmetric space which belongs to the non-compact case.

•

4.6
is devoted to the proof of Proposition
4.2
. The proof is a generalisation of the proof in
[
49
]
, carried out in the special case of Metropolis algorithms in a Euclidean space.

4.1

MAP versus MMS

Let
M
be a Riemannian symmetric space, which belongs to the non-compact case (see
1.9.2
). Recall the Gaussian distribution
P(x\hskip 0.85355pt,\sigma)
on
M
is given by its probability density function (
3.6
)

p(y|x\hskip 0.56917pt,\sigma)\,=\,\left(Z(\sigma)\right)^{-1}\hskip 0.85355pt\exp\left[-\frac{d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(y,x)}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]

(4.1)

In
3.4
, it was seen that maximum-likelihood estimation of the parameter
x
, based on independent samples
(y_{n}\,;n=1,\ldots,N)
, amounts to computing the Riemannian barycentre of these samples. The one-sample maximum-likelihood estimate, given a single observation
y
, is therefore
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle ML}=y
.

Instead of maximum-likelihood estimation, consider a Bayesian approach to estimating
x
, based on the observation
y
. To do so, assign to
x
a prior density, which is also Gaussian,

p(x|z\hskip 0.56917pt,\tau)\,=\,\left(Z(\tau)\right)^{-1}\hskip 0.85355pt\exp\left[-\frac{d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,z)}{2\tau^{2}}\right]

(4.2)

Upon observation of
y
, Bayesian inference concerning
x
is carried out, using the posterior density

\pi(x)\propto\exp\left[-\frac{d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(y,x)}{2\sigma^{2}}-\frac{d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,z)}{2\tau^{2}}\right]

(4.3)

where
\propto
indicates a missing (unknown) normalising factor.

In particular, the maximum
a posteriori
estimator
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle MAP}
of
x
is equal to the mode of the posterior density
\pi(x)
. In other words,
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle MAP}
minimises the weighted sum of squared distances
d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(y,x)/\sigma^{2}+d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x,z)/\tau^{2}
. This is expressed in the following notation
1
1

1

If
p\hskip 0.85355pt,q\in M
and
c:[0,1]\rightarrow M
is a geodesic curve with
c(0)=p
and
c(1)=q
, then
p\,\#_{\scriptscriptstyle t}\,q=c(t)
, for
t\in[0,1]
. Therefore,
p\,\#_{\scriptscriptstyle t}\,q
is a geodesic convex combination of
p
and
q
, with respective weights
(1-t)
and
t
.
,

\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle MAP}\,=\,z\,\#_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho}\,y\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }\rho=\frac{\tau^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+\tau^{2}}

(4.4)

Thus,
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle MAP}
is a geodesic convex combination of the prior barycentre
z
and the observation
y
, with respective weights
\sigma^{2}/(\sigma^{2}+\tau^{2})
and
\tau^{2}/(\sigma^{2}+\tau^{2})
.

On the other hand, the minimum mean square error estimator
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle MMS}
is the barycentre of the posterior density
\pi(x)
. That is,
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle MMS}
is the global minimiser of

\mathcal{E}_{\pi}(y)\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt\scriptscriptstyle 2}(y\hskip 0.56917pt,x)\hskip 0.85355pt\pi(x)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{vol}(dx)

(4.5)

whose existence and uniqueness are established in the remark below. While it is easy to compute
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle MAP}
from (
4.4
), it is much harder to find
\hat{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle MMS\,}
, as this requires minimising the integral (
4.5
), where the density
\pi(x)
is known only up to normalisation.

Still, there is one special case where these two estimators are equal.

Proposition 4.1
.

In the above notation, if
\sigma^{2}=\tau^{2}
(that is
\rho=1/2
), then
