\mathrm{Ret}
is a regular retraction, which satisfies (
5.16
). Assume that (
5.17
) holds, so it is possible replace
\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=0
in (
5.3
). Assume also that there exists a positive function, with bounded and
\ell
-Lipschitz gradient, which verifies (
5.5
). If
\mu_{t}\leq(c/2\ell)
for all
t
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\|X(x_{\tau_{t}})\|^{2}_{x_{\tau_{t}}}\right]\leq(2/\!\hskip 0.56917ptc)\!\,\left[\left(V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\middle/t\right)\!\hskip 0.56917pt\{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\}_{t}+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\{\mu\}_{t}+(\delta\tau_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\hskip 0.56917pt\|V\|_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\infty})\hskip 0.85355pt\{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\}_{t}\hskip 0.56917pt\right]

(5.18)

where
\|V\|_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\infty}=\sup_{x\in M}\|\mathrm{grad}\,V\|_{x}\hskip 0.56917pt
.

The assumptions of Proposition
5.2
(namely, that
X_{y}(x)
has bounded third order moments, and that
\mathrm{grad}\,V(x)
is uniformly bounded), can seem a bit too strong. In fact, these assumptions are quite natural, in several applications, where the underlying Riemannian manifold
M
is compact. One such application, to the PCA problem, is presented in
5.5
.

Remark :
the first two terms on the right-hand side of (
5.18
) are the same as on the right-hand side of (
5.11
). Thus, replacing the Riemannian exponential
\mathrm{Exp}
by a regular retraction
\mathrm{Ret}
has the effect of introducing a second-order term (
i.e.
a constant multiple of
\{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\}_{t}
) into (
5.18
). This additional term vanishes, in the limit where
\delta
goes to zero.

Proof of Proposition
5.2
:
for
s=0,1,\ldots,
it follows by applying Lemma
5.1
to (
5.15
) that

V(x_{s+1})-V(x_{s})\leq\left\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,\Phi_{x_{s}}\!\left(\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\right)\right\rangle_{x_{s}}+(\ell/2)\left\|\Phi_{x_{s}}\!\left(\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{x_{s}}

(5.19)

Here, the right-hand side may also be written

\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\left\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,X_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\right\rangle_{x_{s}}+(\ell/2)\left\|\Phi_{x_{s}}\!\left(\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{x_{s}}+\left\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,\Phi_{x_{s}}\!\left(\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\right)-\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\right\rangle_{x_{s}}

However, by (
5.16
),

\left\|\Phi_{x_{s}}\!\left(\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{x_{s}}\leq\mu^{2}_{s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\|X_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\|^{2}_{x_{s}}

(5.20)

and, in addition,

\left\|\Phi_{x_{s}}\!\left(\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\right)-\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\right\|_{x_{s}}\leq\delta\hskip 0.56917pt\mu^{3}_{s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\|X_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\|^{3}_{x_{s}}

(5.21)

Replacing (
5.20
) and (
5.21
) into (
5.19
), and using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

V(x_{s+1})-V(x_{s})\leq\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,X_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\rangle_{x_{s}}+\mu^{2}_{s+1}(\ell/2)\hskip 0.56917pt\|X_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\|^{2}_{x_{s}}+(\delta\|V\|_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\infty})\hskip 0.56917pt\mu^{3}_{s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\|X_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\|^{3}_{x_{s}}

Now, it is possible to proceed as in the proof of Proposition
5.1
. Taking conditional expectations with respect to
\mathcal{Y}_{s}\hskip 0.85355pt
, and using (
5.2
) and (
5.3
),

-\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,X\rangle_{x_{s}}-\mu^{2}_{s+1}\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\|X_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\|^{2}_{x_{s}}\leq-\Delta V_{s}+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}_{s+1}+(\delta\|V\|_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\infty})\hskip 0.56917pt\mu^{3}_{s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\mathbb{E}\left[\|X_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\|^{3}_{x_{s}}\middle|\mathcal{Y}_{s}\right]

where
\Delta V_{s}=\mathbb{E}\left[V(x_{s+1})-V(x_{s})\middle|\mathcal{Y}_{s}\right]
.
Then, using (
5.5
), it follows that

(c-\ell\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\|X\|^{2}_{x_{s}}\leq-\Delta V_{s}+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}_{s+1}+(\delta\|V\|_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\infty})\hskip 0.56917pt\mu^{3}_{s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\mathbb{E}\left[\|X_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917pts+1}}\|^{3}_{x_{s}}\middle|\mathcal{Y}_{s}\right]

so, inserting (
5.17
), one obtains the inequality

(c-\ell\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\|X\|^{2}_{x_{s}}\leq-\Delta V_{s}+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}_{s+1}+(\delta\tau_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\hskip 0.56917pt\|V\|_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\infty})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{3}_{s+1}

Here, if
\mu_{t}\leq(c/2\ell)
for all
t
, then

(c/\!\hskip 1.13791pt2)\hskip 0.85355pt\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle s+1}\hskip 0.56917pt\|X\|^{2}_{x_{s}}\leq-\Delta V_{s}+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}_{s+1}+(\delta\tau_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\hskip 0.56917pt\|V\|_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\infty})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{3}_{s+1}

Finally, (
5.18
) follows by summing over
s=1,\ldots,t
and dividing by
\sum^{t}_{s=1}\mu_{s+1}=t/\!\hskip 0.56917pt\{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\}_{t}\,
.

5.4

Example : mixture estimation

Let
M
be a Riemannian symmetric space, which belongs to the non-compact case, (see
1.9.2
). Consider a probability density
m
on
M
, which is a mixture of Gaussian densities (of the kind defined in
3.2
),

m(y|x)=\frac{1}{K}\sum^{K}_{\kappa=1}p(y|x_{\kappa})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }\,p(y|x_{\kappa})=(Z(1))^{-1}\exp\left[-\frac{d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(y,x_{\kappa})}{2}\right]

(5.22)

where
K
is the number of mixture components, and the normalising factor
Z(1)
is given by (
3.7
). The parameters
x=(x_{\kappa}\,;\kappa=1,\ldots,K)
are to be estimated, by fitting the mixture density (
5.22
) to data
y_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,y_{\scriptscriptstyle N}\hskip 0.85355pt
. Then, maximum-likelihood estimation amounts to minimising the negative log-likelihood function

f(x)=-\log\hskip 0.56917ptZ(1)-\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\log\hskip 0.56917ptm(y_{n}|x)

(5.23)

where the first term,
-\log\hskip 0.56917ptZ(1)
, has been added to ensure that
f(x)
is positive. The function
f
is defined on the product Riemannian manifold,
M^{\scriptscriptstyle K}=M\times\ldots\times M
. Its gradient is then
\mathrm{grad}\,f=(\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\,f\,;\kappa=1,\ldots,K)
, where
\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\,f
denotes the gradient with respect to
x_{\kappa}\hskip 0.85355pt
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For the negative log-likelihood function (
5.23
),

\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\,f(x)=-\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\omega_{\kappa}(y_{n})\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x_{\kappa}}(y_{n})

(5.24)

where
\omega_{\kappa}(y)\propto p(y|x_{\kappa})
are positive weights, which add up to
1
.

Let
(y_{t}\,;t=1,2,\ldots)
be chosen at random among the data
y_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,y_{\scriptscriptstyle N}\hskip 0.85355pt
. By Lemma
5.2
,

x^{t+1}_{\kappa}=\mathrm{Exp}_{x^{t}_{\kappa}}\!\left(\mu\hskip 0.85355ptX_{\kappa}(y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1},x^{t}_{\kappa})\right)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }\,X_{\kappa}(y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1},x^{t}_{\kappa})=\omega_{\kappa}(y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1})\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x^{t}_{\kappa}}(y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1})

(5.25)

is a constant-step-size stochastic gradient scheme, for the cost function
f
. Here, the step-size
\mu
is assumed to be less than
1
(in comparison to (
5.9
),
t
and
t+1
have been written as superscripts, rather than subscripts, in order to accommodate the appearance of
\kappa
).

Now, let
C
be a compact and convex subset of
M
, which contains all of the data points
y_{n}\hskip 0.85355pt
, as well as all of the initial values
x^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}_{\kappa}
(since
M
is a Hadamard manifold, one may take
C
to be any sufficiently large closed geodesic ball). The diameter of
C
will be denoted
\mathrm{D}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\hskip 0.85355pt
. From (
5.25
),

x^{t+1}_{\kappa}=x^{t}_{\kappa}\;\#_{\scriptscriptstyle\rho_{\hskip 0.28436ptt+1}}\,y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }\rho_{\hskip 0.56917ptt+1}=\mu\hskip 0.85355pt\omega_{\kappa}(y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1})

in the notation of (
4.4
), from
4.1
. Accordingly, the iterates
x^{t}_{\kappa}
remain within
C
, for all
t
and
\kappa
. Because
C
is compact and convex, it then becomes possible to derive the following result, by repeating, with very minor changes, the arguments leading to (
5.12
).

Proposition 5.3
.

For the stochastic gradient scheme (
5.25
), let
C
be a compact and convex subset of
M
, which contains all of the data points
y_{n}\hskip 0.85355pt
, as well as all of the initial values
x^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}_{\kappa}
. If
\mu\leq(1/2\ell_{\scriptscriptstyle C})
, where
\ell_{\scriptscriptstyle C}
denotes the supremum of the operator norm of
\mathrm{Hess}\,f(x)
, taken over
x=(x_{\kappa}\,;\kappa=1,\ldots,K)\in C^{\scriptscriptstyle K}
, then for all
t=1,2,\ldots
,

\frac{1}{2t}\sum^{t}_{s=1}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathrm{grad}\,f(x^{s})\|^{2}_{x^{s}}\right]\leq\left(f_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\middle/t\hskip 0.56917pt\mu\right)+(\ell_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu

(5.26)

Here,
f_{\scriptscriptstyle C}=\sup_{x\in C^{\scriptscriptstyle K}}f(x)
and
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}=\sup_{x\in C^{\scriptscriptstyle K}}\|\mathrm{grad}\,f\|_{x}
(explicit bounds on
f_{\scriptscriptstyle C\hskip 0.56917pt}
,
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
and
\ell_{\scriptscriptstyle C}
are given in the remark below).

Remark :
tedious, but straightforward, calculations provide the upper bounds

f_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}}{2}\hskip 7.68236pt;\hskip 6.544pt\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\leq K\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{D}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}

(5.27)

\ell_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\leq(1+c\hskip 0.56917pt\mathrm{D}_{\scriptscriptstyle C})+(1+Z(1)\exp(\mathrm{D}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}/2))\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}

(5.28)

where
c
is such that the sectional curvatures of
M
lie within
[-c^{\hskip 0.56917pt\scriptscriptstyle 2},0]
.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
:
taking the gradient of (
5.23
), it is clear that

\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\,f(x)=-\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{n=1}\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\log m(y_{n}|x)

(5.29)

Now,
\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\log m(y|x)
can be computed as follows. If
\lambda
is a random variable, independent from
y
, with
\mathbb{P}(\lambda=\kappa)=K^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, for
\kappa=1,\ldots,K
, then
\mathbb{P}(\lambda=\kappa|y)=\omega_{\kappa}(y)
, with
\omega_{\kappa}(y)
as in (
5.24
). Therefore, using Bayes rule,

\frac{p(\lambda\hskip 0.56917pt,y)}{m(y|x)}=\sum^{K}_{\nu=1}\mathbf{1}\{\lambda=\nu\}\hskip 0.85355pt\omega_{\nu}(y)

where
p(\lambda\hskip 0.56917pt,y)
is the joint distribution of the couple
(\lambda\hskip 0.56917pt,y)
. Taking logarithms,

\log p(\lambda\hskip 0.56917pt,y)-\log m(y|x)=\sum^{K}_{\nu=1}\mathbf{1}\{\lambda=\nu\}\hskip 0.85355pt\log\omega_{\nu}(y)

(5.30)

If
\mathbb{E}_{y}
denotes conditional expectation with respect to
y
,

\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\sum^{K}_{\nu=1}\mathbf{1}\{\lambda=\nu\}\hskip 0.85355pt\log\omega_{\nu}(y)\right]=\sum^{K}_{\nu=1}\omega_{\nu}(y)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\log\omega_{\nu}(y)=0

where the second equality follows since the conditional probabilities
\omega_{\nu}(y)
always add up to
1
. By replacing this into (
5.30
),

\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\log m(y|x)=\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\log p(\lambda\hskip 0.56917pt,y)\right]

(5.31)

But, since
\lambda
and
y
are independent, the joint distribution
p(\lambda\hskip 0.56917pt,y)
reads

p(\lambda\hskip 0.56917pt,y)=\frac{1}{K}\sum^{K}_{\nu=1}\mathbf{1}\{\lambda=\nu\}\hskip 0.85355ptp(y|x_{\nu})

Therefore, taking logarithms,

\log p(\lambda\hskip 0.56917pt,y)=-\log(K)+\sum^{K}_{\nu=1}\mathbf{1}\{\lambda=\nu\}\hskip 0.85355pt\log p(y|x_{\nu})

This immediately yields,

\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\log p(\lambda\hskip 0.56917pt,y)\right]=\omega_{\kappa}(y)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\log p(y|x_{\kappa})=\omega_{\kappa}(y)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x_{\kappa}}(y)

(5.32)

where the second equality follows from (
1.75
), and from the definition of
p(y|x_{\kappa})
in (
5.22
). Finally, replacing (
5.32
) into (
5.31
),

\mathrm{grad}_{\kappa}\log m(y|x)=\omega_{\kappa}(y)\hskip 0.85355pt\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x_{\kappa}}(y)

(5.33)

so that (
5.24
) follows by plugging (
5.33
) into (
5.29
).

5.5

Example : the PCA problem

Here, the notation will be the same as in
1.4
and
1.6
. The aim is to apply Proposition
5.2
, to a constant-step-size stochastic gradient scheme, for the objective function (
1.22
),

f(x)=\mathrm{tr}\left(x\Delta\right)\hskip 28.45274ptx\in\mathrm{Gr}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}}(p\,,q)

(5.34)

where
\Delta
is the covariance matrix of a zero-mean random vector
y
, with values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d=p+q)
. It is assumed that
y
has finite moments of order
6
.

The gradient of the objective function
f
was given by (
1.26
) and (
1.29
). These can be written

\mathrm{grad}\,f(x)=g\cdot\tilde{\omega}(x)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }\tilde{\omega}(x)=\mathrm{P}_{o}(g^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta)

(5.35)

Now, let
b\in\mathrm{St}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}}(p\,,q)
be such that
x=[b]
. By the discussion before (
1.40
), choosing
g=(b,b^{\scriptscriptstyle\perp})
, it follows that
\mathrm{grad}\,f(x)=[X(b)]
, where
X(b)=b^{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}\omega(b)\hskip 0.56917pt
. From (
1.27
) and (
1.29
), it is clear that
\omega(b)=(b^{\scriptscriptstyle\perp})^{\dagger}\Delta\hskip 0.85355ptb
. Therefore, using the fact that
x=bb^{\dagger}
(this is the definition of
[b]
),

X(b)=(\mathrm{I}_{d}-x)\Delta\hskip 0.85355ptb

(5.36)

In terms of the random vector
y
,
X(b)
is the expectation of
X_{y}(b)
, where

X_{y}(b)=(\mathrm{I}_{d}-x)(yy^{\dagger})\hskip 0.85355ptb

(5.37)

Let
X_{y}(x)=[X_{y}(b)]
, and note that the expectation of
X_{y}(x)
is equal to
\mathrm{grad}\,f(x)
(by linearity).
Then, consider the constant-step-size stochastic gradient scheme

x_{t+1}=\mathrm{Ret}_{x_{t}}\!\left(\mu\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1}}(x_{t})\right)

(5.38)

where
(y_{t}\,;t=1,2,\ldots)
are independent copies of
y
. If the retraction
\mathrm{Ret}
is given as in (
1.41
), this becomes

x_{t+1}=\mathrm{Span}(b_{t+1})\hskip 7.11317pt;\hskip 5.97527ptb_{t+1}=b_{t}+\mu\hskip 0.85355pt(\mathrm{I}_{d}-b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt}b^{\dagger}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt})(y^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1}y^{\dagger}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1})\hskip 0.85355ptb_{t}

(5.39)

Proposition
5.2
, applied to this scheme, yields the following bound.

Proposition 5.4
.

Consider the constant-step-size scheme (
5.38
)-(
5.39
). For all
t=1,2,\ldots,

\frac{1}{2t}\sum^{t}_{s=1}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathrm{grad}\,f(x_{s})\|^{2}_{x_{s}}\right]\leq\left(p\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}})\middle/t\hskip 0.56917pt\mu\right)\!\hskip 0.56917pt+(4\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}}\hskip 0.56917ptm^{4}_{y})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu+(\sqrt{8}\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}\hskip 0.56917ptm^{6}_{y})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}

(5.40)

where
\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}}
and
\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}
denote the operator norm and Frobenius norm of the matrix
\Delta
, while
m^{4}_{y}
and
m^{6}_{y}
denote the fourth-order and sixth-order moments of the random vector
y
.

Proposition
5.4
follows directly from Proposition
5.2
, by introducing
V(x)=p\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}}-f(x)
, which satisfies
0\leq V(x)\leq p\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}\hskip 0.56917pt}
. Since
-\mathrm{grad}\,V(x)=\mathrm{grad}\,f(x)
, (
5.5
) now holds with
c=1
.

The function
V
has
2\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}}
-Lipschitz gradient, as will be shown in the remark below, and the norm of its gradient can be computed from (
5.35
),

\|\mathrm{grad}\,V\|_{x}=\|\mathrm{grad}\,f\|_{x}=\|\tilde{\omega}_{x}\|_{o}\leq\|g^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}

(5.41)

where the inequality follows from (
1.25
), since
\mathrm{P}_{o}
is an orthogonal projection. But, since
g
is orthogonal, (
5.41
) implies that
\|\mathrm{grad}\,V\|_{x}
is bounded by
\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F\hskip 0.56917pt}
, uniformly in
x
.

Thus, to obtain (
5.40
), it is possible to replace into (
5.18
),
V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\leq p\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}\hskip 0.56917pt}
,
\ell=2\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}\hskip 0.56917pt}
, and
\|V\|_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\infty}=\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F\hskip 0.56917pt}
.

For
\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
and
\tau_{\scriptscriptstyle 3\hskip 0.85355pt}
, recall that
X_{y}(x)=[X_{y}(b)]
, where
X_{y}(b)=b^{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}\omega_{y}(b)
, with
\omega_{y}(b)=(b^{\scriptscriptstyle\perp})^{\dagger}(yy^{\dagger})\hskip 0.85355ptb
. However, this implies
X_{y}(x)=g\cdot\tilde{\omega}_{y}(b)
, (
\tilde{\omega}_{y}(b)
is obtained from
\omega_{y}(b)
, according to (
1.27
)). Thus,
\|X_{y}\|_{x}=\|\tilde{\omega}_{y}(b)\|_{o}=\sqrt{2}\|\omega_{y}(b)\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F\hskip 0.56917pt}
. By evaluating the Frobenius norm,

\|\omega_{y}(b)\|^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}=\mathrm{tr}\left((\mathrm{I}_{d}-x)(yy^{\dagger})x(yy^{\dagger})\right)\leq\|(\mathrm{I}_{d}-x)(yy^{\dagger})\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}\hskip 0.56917pt\|(yy^{\dagger})x\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}\leq\|yy^{\dagger}\|^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle F}

where the first inequality follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and the second inequality follows because
x
and
\mathrm{I}_{d}-x
are orthogonal projectors. Since
\|yy^{\dagger}\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}=\|y\|^{2}
(the squared Euclidean norm of
y
), this implies
\|X_{y}\|_{x}\leq\sqrt{2}\|y\|^{2}
. Therefore, it is possible to set
\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}=2m^{4}_{y}
and
\tau_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}=\sqrt{8}m^{6}_{y}\hskip 0.85355pt
.

Finally, the constant
\delta
in (
5.16
) can be taken equal to
1
. Indeed, if
\mathrm{Ret}_{x}
is given by (
1.41
) and
\Phi_{x}
is given by (
1.42
), then for
v\in T_{x}\mathrm{Gr}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}}(p\,,q)
, where
v=g\cdot\tilde{\omega}
and
\omega
has s.v.d.
\omega=ras^{\dagger}
,
\Phi_{x}(v)=g\cdot\tilde{\varphi}
where
\varphi
has s.v.d.
\varphi=r\arctan(a)s^{\dagger}
. Therefore,

\|\Phi_{x}(v)-v\|_{x}=\|g\cdot\tilde{\varphi}-g\cdot\tilde{\omega}\|_{x}=\|\tilde{\varphi}-\tilde{\omega}\|_{o}

(5.42)

If
k
is given by (
1.47
), then

\|\tilde{\varphi}-\tilde{\omega}\|_{o}=\|k\cdot\arctan(\tilde{a})-k\cdot\tilde{a}\|_{o}=\|\arctan(\tilde{a})-\tilde{a}\|_{o}

By an elementary property of the
\arctan
function,
\|\arctan(\tilde{a})-\tilde{a}\|_{o}\leq\|\tilde{a}\|^{3}_{o}\hskip 0.85355pt
. Therefore,

\|\tilde{\varphi}-\tilde{\omega}\|_{o}\leq\|\tilde{a}\|^{3}_{o}

(5.43)

Replacing (
5.43
) into (
5.42
), and noting that
\|\tilde{a}\|_{o}=\|v\|_{x}\hskip 0.85355pt
, it follows that

\|\Phi_{x}(v)-v\|_{x}\leq\|v\|^{3}_{x}\hskip 0.85355pt
. This is the second inequality in (
5.16
), with
\delta=1
. The first inequality in (
5.16
) is obtained by an analogous reasoning, using once more the properties of the
\arctan
function.

Remark :
it was claimed that the function
V
has
2\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}}
-Lipschitz gradient (this means
\mathrm{grad}\,V
satisfies (
1.86
), with
\ell=2\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{op}})
. To prove this claim, let

c(t)
be a geodesic, with
c(0)=x
and
\dot{c}(0)=v
. In the notation of (
1.32
),
c(t)=\exp(t\,\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})\cdot x
. From
[
10
]
(Theorem 3.3, Chapter IV),

\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(\mathrm{grad}\,V(x)\right)=\exp(\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})\cdot\mathrm{grad}\,V(x)

(5.44)

But,
\mathrm{grad}\,V(x)=-\mathrm{grad}\,f(x)
, which is given by (
5.35
). Therefore,

\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(\mathrm{grad}\,V(x)\right)=(\exp(\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})g)\cdot\mathrm{P}_{o}(g^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta)

(5.45)

On the other hand, letting
y=c(1)
, one has
y=(\exp(\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})g)\cdot o
. Thus, from (
5.35
),

\mathrm{grad}\,V(y)=(\exp(\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})g)\cdot\mathrm{P}_{o}((\exp(\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})g)^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta)

(5.46)

From (
5.45
) and (
5.46
),

\|\mathrm{grad}\,V(y)-\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(\mathrm{grad}\,V(x)\right)\|_{y}=\|\mathrm{P}_{o}((\exp(\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})g)^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta)-\mathrm{P}_{o}(g^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta)\|_{o}\leq\|(\exp(\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})g)^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta-g^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}

where the inequality holds since
\mathrm{P}_{o}
is an orthogonal projection. Using the fact that

(\exp(\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})g)^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta-g^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta=\int^{1}_{0}\left(\Delta(t)\hskip 0.56917pt\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v}-\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v}\hskip 0.56917pt\Delta(t)\right)dt

where
\Delta(t)=(\exp(t\,\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v})g)^{\dagger}\cdot\Delta
, so
\|\Delta(t)\|_{\scriptscriptstyle op}=\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle op}\hskip 0.56917pt
, it follows that

\|\mathrm{grad}\,V(y)-\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(\mathrm{grad}\,V(x)\right)\|_{y}\leq\int^{1}_{0}\|\Delta(t)\hskip 0.56917pt\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v}-\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v}\hskip 0.56917pt\Delta(t)\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}\hskip 1.13791ptdt\leq 2\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle op}\hskip 0.56917pt\|\hat{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle v}\|_{\scriptscriptstyle F}

By the remark at the end of
1.4
, the right-hand side is
2\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle op}\hskip 0.56917pt\|v\|_{x\hskip 0.85355pt}
. In other words,

\|\mathrm{grad}\,V(y)-\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(\mathrm{grad}\,V(x)\right)\|_{y}\leq 2\|\Delta\|_{\scriptscriptstyle op}\hskip 0.56917ptL(c)

This is equivalent to the required form of (
1.86
), as can be seen by applying
\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
under the norm.

5.6

A central limit theorem (CLT)

Here, the aim will be to derive a central limit theorem, describing the asymptotic behavior of certain constant-step-size exponential schemes, defined on Hadamard manifolds. This is a generalisation of the central limit theorem, which holds in Euclidean space, found in
[
54
]
.

5.6.1

Geometric ergodicity

Consider the constant-step-size exponential scheme, defined on a Hadamard manifold
M
,

x_{t+1}=\mathrm{Exp}_{x_{t}}\!\left(\mu\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1}}(x_{t})\right)

(5.47)

Since the observations
(y_{t}\,;t=1,2,\ldots)
are independent and identically distributed, it follows that
(x_{t}\,;t=0,1,\ldots)
is a time-homogeneous Markov chain with values in
M
. The following assumptions ensure that
(x_{t})
is geometrically ergodic, and therefore has a unique invariant distribution
\pi_{\mu}
.

e1.
the noise vector
e_{y}(x)
satisfies (
5.3
).

e2.

e_{y}(x)
is
P
-almost-surely a continuous function of
x
, and the distribution of
e_{y}(x)
has strictly positive density, with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
T_{x}M
.

v1.
there exists a positive function
V:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, with compact sublevel sets, and
\ell
-Lipschitz gradient, which satisfies (
5.5
).

v2.
there exist
x^{*}\in M
and
\lambda>0
, such that

\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,X\rangle_{x}\leq-\lambda\hskip 0.56917ptV(x)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for }x\neq x^{*}

(5.48)

v3.

V(x)=0
if and only if
x=x^{*}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Consider the constant-step-size scheme (
5.47
), on a Hadamard manifold
M
. Assume that
e1
,
e2
,
v1
,
v2
hold. If
\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, then the Markov chain
(x_{t})
is geometrically ergodic, with a unique invariant distribution
\pi_{\mu}
.

As the step-size
\mu
goes to zero, the invariant distribution
\pi_{\mu}
concentrates on the point
x^{*}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Under the same conditions as in Proposition
5.5
, if
v3
holds, then
\pi_{\mu}\,\Rightarrow\,\delta_{x^{*}}
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
(here,
\Rightarrow
denotes weak convergence of probability measures).

5.6.2

A diffusion limit

Consider now the re-scaled sequence
(u_{t}\,;t=0,1,\ldots)
, with values in
T_{x^{*}}M
,

u_{t}=\psi_{\mu}(x_{t})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }\psi_{\mu}(x)=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x^{*}}(x)

(5.49)

This is the image of
(x_{t}\,;t=0,1,\ldots)
, under the diffeomorphism
\psi_{\mu}:M\rightarrow T_{x^{*}}M
. It is therefore a time-homogeneous Markov chain with values in
T_{x^{*}}M
. The trasition kernels of
(x_{t})
and
(u_{t})
will be denoted
Q_{\mu}
and
\tilde{Q}_{\mu}\hskip 0.85355pt
, respectively. Note that

\tilde{Q}_{\mu}\phi(u)=Q_{\mu}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))

(5.50)

for any measurable function
\phi:T_{x}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

The following assumptions ensure that, as
\mu
goes to zero,
(u_{t}\,;t=0,1,\ldots)
behave like samples, taken at evenly spaced times
\tau_{t}=t\mu
, from a linear diffusion process
(U_{\tau}\,;\tau\geq 0)
.

d1.
the (2,0)-tensor field
\Sigma
, defined by

\Sigma(x)=\int_{Y}\,e_{y}(x)\otimes e_{y}(x)\hskip 0.85355ptP(dy)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for }x\in M

(5.51)

is continuous on
M
.

d2.
there exists a linear map
A:T_{x^{*}}M\rightarrow T_{x^{*}}M
, such that for
x\in M
,

X(x)=\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(A\left(\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x^{*}}(x)\right)+R(x)\right)

(5.52)

where
\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
denotes parallel transport along the unique geodesic
c:[0,1]\rightarrow M
, connecting
x^{*}
to
x
, and
\|R(x)\|_{x^{*}}=o(d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*}))
.

Now, let
(U_{\tau}\,:\tau\geq 0)
, be the linear diffusion process, with generator,

\mathcal{L}\phi(u)=A^{i}_{j}u^{j}\hskip 0.56917pt\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u^{i}}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{ij}_{*}\hskip 0.56917pt\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial u^{i}\partial u^{j}}(u)

(5.53)

where
(A^{i}_{j})
and
(\Sigma^{ij}_{*})
are the matrices which represent the linear map
A
and the tensor
\Sigma(x^{*})
, in a basis of normal coordinates centred at
x^{*}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Consider the constant-step-size scheme (
5.47
), on a Hadamard manifold
M
. Let
(u_{t}\,:t=0,1,\ldots)
be given by (
5.49
), and assume that
e1
,
d1
,
d2
hold. For any compactly-supported, smooth function
\phi:T_{x}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\mu^{-1}\left[\tilde{Q}_{\mu}\phi(u)-\phi(u)\right]=\mathcal{L}\phi(u)\,+\,\varepsilon_{\mu}(u)

(5.54)

where
\varepsilon_{\mu}(u)\rightarrow 0
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
, uniformly on compact subsets of
T_{x^{*}}M
.

Remark :
this proposition implies a
functional central limit theorem
, by application of
[
55
]
(Theorem 19.28). This says that the process
(U^{\mu}_{\tau}\,;\tau\geq 0)
, equal to
u_{t}
for
t\mu\leq\tau<(t+1)\mu
, converges in distribution to the linear diffusion
U
, with generator (
5.53
), in Skorokhod space. This functional central limit theorem can be used to study the asymptotic behavior of
(x_{t})
, near a general critical point, which satisfies
d2
. Sadly, I have not yet had time to develop this idea.

5.6.3

The stable case

A central limit theorem can be derived, in the case where
x^{*}
is a stable critical point of the mean field
X
, in the following sense.

t1.
the linear map
A
in (
5.52
) has its spectrum contained in the open left half-plane.

In this case, the generator
\mathcal{L}
in (
5.53
) admits of a unique invariant distribution, which is multivariate normal with mean zero and covariance matrix
V
, the solution of the Lyapunov equation
AV+V\!\hskip 0.28436ptA^{\dagger}=\Sigma_{*}

[
56
]
(
A=(A^{i}_{j})
and
\Sigma_{*}=(\Sigma^{ij}_{*})
). This will be denoted
\mathrm{N}(0,V)
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
5.5
, the Markov chain
(x_{t})
has a unique invariant distribution
\pi_{\mu}
. Then, the same holds for the Markov chain
(u_{t})
, which will have a unique invariant distribution
\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}
. This is
\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}(A)=\pi_{\mu}(\mathrm{Exp}_{x^{*}}(\mu^{1/2}A))
, for any measurable
A\subset T_{x^{*}}M
.

The following assumptions will be essential for the central limit theorem, which is stated in Proposition
5.8
. Assumption
t2
ensures tightness of the family
(\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}\,;\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})
.

t2.
for each
r>0
there exists
v(r)>0
such that
V(x)\geq v(r)
if
d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})>r
. Moreover,
v(r)\rightarrow\infty
as
r\rightarrow\infty
and
\left.a\middle/v(a^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}r)\right.
is a non-descreasing function of
a>0
, for any
r
.

t3.
there exists
\alpha>0
such that

\int_{Y}\,\|e_{y}(x)\|^{2+\alpha}_{x}\hskip 1.13791ptP(dy)\leq\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\hskip 0.56917ptV(x)

(5.55)

for some constants
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\hskip 0.56917pt,\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Under the conditions of Propositions
5.6
and
5.7
, if
t1
,
t2
,
t3
hold, then
\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}\Rightarrow\mathrm{N}(0,V)
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
.

5.7

Proof of the CLT

5.7.1

Proof of Proposition
5.5

The proof relies on the following two lemmas, which will be proved below.

Lemma 5.3
.

Assume that
e2
holds. Then, the Markov chain
(x_{t})
is Feller, and
|\mathrm{vol}|
-irreducible and aperiodic (where
|\mathrm{vol}|
denotes the Riemannian volume measure on
M
).

Lemma 5.4
.

Assume that
e1
,
v1
,
v2
hold. If
\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, then

Q_{\mu}V(x)\leq(1-\lambda\mu/2)V(x)+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}

(5.56)

for all
x\in M
.

Admitting these lemmas, the fact that the chain
(x_{t})
is geometrically ergodic follows from
[
51
]
(Theorem 16.0.1). Specifically, let
\tilde{V}(x)=V(x)+1
. Then, by (
5.56
),

Q_{\mu}\tilde{V}(x)\leq(1-\lambda\mu/2)\tilde{V}(x)+b\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }b=(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}+\lambda\mu/2

Let
C=\{x:V(x)\leq 4b/(\lambda\mu)\}
. Clearly,

Q_{\mu}\tilde{V}(x)\leq(1-\lambda\mu/4)\tilde{V}(x)+b\hskip 0.56917pt\mathbf{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}(x)

(5.57)

By
v1
,
\tilde{V}
has compact sublevel sets, so
C
is a compact subset of
M
. Therefore, since
(x_{t})
is Feller,
C
is a small set for
Q_{\mu}

[
51
]
(Theorem 6.0.1). Then, (
5.57
) is a geometric drift condition towards the small set
C
. This is equivalent to
(x_{t})
being geometrically ergodic.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
:
let
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a bounded continuous function. By a slight abuse of notation, let
y
denote a random variable with distribution
P
. From (
5.47
),

Q_{\mu}f(x)=\mathbb{E}\left[f\!\left(\mathrm{Exp}_{x}\!\left(\mu\hskip 0.56917ptX(x)+\mu\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x)\right)\right)\right]

(5.58)

By
e2
,
e_{y}(x)
is
P
-almost-surely a continuous function of
x
. By the dominated convergence theorem,
Q_{\mu}f(x)
is a bounded continuous function of
x
. In other words, the transition kernel
Q_{\mu}
is a Feller kernel, so the chain
(x_{t})
is Feller.

To show that
(x_{t})
is
|\mathrm{vol}|
-irreducible and aperiodic, it is enough to show that
Q_{\mu}(x,B)>0
whenever
\mathrm{vol}(B)>0
, where
Q_{\mu}(x,B)=Q_{\mu}\mathrm{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}(x)
. By
e2
, if
w=e_{y}(x)
then the distribution of
w
is of the form
\gamma(w)\hskip 0.85355ptdw
, where
\gamma(w)>0
, and
dw
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
T_{x}M
. Therefore, from (
5.47
),

Q_{\mu}(x,B)=\int_{T_{x}M}\mathbf{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}\!\left(\mathrm{Exp}_{x}\!\left(\mu\hskip 0.56917ptX(x)+\mu\hskip 0.56917ptw\right)\right)\,\gamma(w)\hskip 0.85355ptdw

Since
M
is a Hadamard manifold,
\mathrm{Exp}_{x}
is a diffeomorphism of
T_{x}M
onto
M
. Accordingly,

Q_{\mu}(x,B)=(1/\mu)^{n}\hskip 1.42271pt\int_{B}\gamma\!\left((1/\mu)\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x}(z)-X(x)\right)\!\left|J_{x}(z)\right|^{-1}\mathrm{vol}(dz)

where
n
is the dimension of
M
, and
\mathrm{Exp}^{*}_{x}(\mathrm{vol})(dw)=(\left|J_{x}\right|\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{x})(w)dw
, so that
|J_{x}(z)|>0
.
Now, if
\mathrm{vol}(B)>0
, it is clear that
Q_{\mu}(x,B)>0
.

Proof of Lemma
5.4
:
for any
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\in M
, it follows from (
5.47
) that

Q_{\mu}V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})=\mathbb{E}\left[V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})\right]\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=\mathrm{Exp}_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}\!\left(\mu\hskip 0.56917ptX(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})+\mu\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\right)

(5.59)

where
y
denotes a random variable with distribution
P
(this is the same abuse of notation made in (
5.58
)). However, using Lemma
5.1
, it is possible to write, as in (
5.13
),

V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})\leq V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,X_{y}\rangle_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}+\mu^{2}\ell\left(\|X\|^{2}_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}+\|e_{y}\|^{2}_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}\right)

By
e1
and (
5.59
), it follows after taking expectations,

Q_{\mu}V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\leq V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,X\rangle_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}+\mu^{2}\ell(1+\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})\|X\|^{2}_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}

(5.60)

By
v1
,
V
satisfies (
5.5
), so that (
5.60
) implies

Q_{\mu}V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\leq V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt(1-\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\ell(1+\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})/c)\hskip 0.56917pt\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,X\rangle_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}++(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}

(5.61)

Since
\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,X\rangle
is negative, if
\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, then (
5.61
) implies

Q_{\mu}V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\leq V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})+(\mu/2)\hskip 0.56917pt\langle\mathrm{grad}\,V,X\rangle_{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}

Finally, by
v2
, this yields

Q_{\mu}V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\leq(1-\lambda\mu/2)V(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}

(5.62)

which is the same as (
5.56
), since
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
is arbitrary.

5.7.2

Proof of Proposition
5.6

Proposition
4.2
implies that the chain
(x_{t})
has a unique invariant distribution, here denoted
\pi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, for any
\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
. Integrating both sides of (
5.56
) with respect to
\pi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, it follows that

\int_{M}Q_{\mu}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)\leq(1-\lambda\mu/2)\int_{M}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}

Since
\pi_{\mu}
is an invariant distribution of the transition kernel
Q_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, this means

\int_{M}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)\leq(1-\lambda\mu/2)\int_{M}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)+(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})\hskip 0.85355pt\mu^{2}

In other words,

\int_{M}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)\leq 2(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}/\lambda)\hskip 0.56917pt\mu

(5.63)

so, by Markov’s inequality,

\pi_{\mu}(V>v)\leq 2(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}/\lambda)\hskip 0.56917pt\frac{\mu}{v}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for all $v>0$}

(5.64)

By
v1
,
V
has compact sublevel sets, so (
5.64
) implies the family
(\pi_{\mu}\,;\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})
is tight. If
\pi_{*}
is a limit point of this family at
\mu=0
, then by the Portmaneau theorem,
\pi_{*}(V>v)=0
for all
v>0
. In other words,
\pi_{*}(V=0)=1
. By
v3
, this is equivalent to
\pi_{*}(\{x^{*}\})=1
, or to
\pi_{*}=\delta_{x^{*}}\hskip 0.56917pt
.

5.7.3

Proof of Proposition
5.7

The proof exploits the relation (
5.50
), between the transition kernels
Q_{\mu}
and
\tilde{Q}_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, using the following Lemmas
5.5
,
5.6
, and
5.7
.

In Lemma
5.5
,
[H\!:\!T]
will denote the contraction of a (0,2)-tensor
H
with a (2,0)-tensor
T
. This is
[H\!:\!T]=H_{ij}T^{ij}
, in any local coordinates. Moreover, if
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is compactly-supported and smooth,
A_{f}\hskip 0.56917pt,B_{f}
denote positive numbers such that

|\mathrm{Hess}\,f_{x}(w,w)|\leq A_{f}\|w\|^{2}_{x}\hskip 7.11317pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046pt|\nabla\mathrm{Hess}\,f_{x}(w,w,w)|\leq B_{f}\|w\|^{3}_{x}

(5.65)

for any
x\in M
and
w\in T_{x}M
, where
\nabla\mathrm{Hess}\,f
is the covariant derivative of the Hessian of
f
(respectively,
\mathrm{Hess}\,f
and
\nabla\mathrm{Hess}\,f
are (0,2)- and (0,3)-tensor fields).

Lemma 5.5
.

For any compactly-supported, smooth
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

Q_{\mu}f(x)=f(x)+\mu\hskip 0.56917ptXf(x)+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}[\mathrm{Hess}\,f\!:\!\Sigma+X\otimes X]_{x}+\mu^{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathcal{R}_{x}(f,\mu)

(5.66)

where the remainder term
\mathcal{R}_{x}(f,\mu)
satisfies

|\mathcal{R}_{x}(f,\mu)|\leq 2A_{f}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}>K\}\|e_{y}\|^{2}_{x}\right]+\hskip 227.62204pt

\phantom{abcd}6B_{f}\hskip 0.85355pt\mu\hskip 0.56917pt(2\|X\|^{2}_{x}+2K^{2})\hskip 0.85355pt\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}>K\}\|e_{y}\|_{x}\right]+2B_{f}\hskip 0.85355pt\mu\hskip 0.56917pt(4\|X\|^{3}_{x}+4K^{3})

(5.67)

for any (arbitrarily chosen)
K>0
.

Given normal coordinates
(x^{i}\,;i=1,\ldots,n)
on
M
, with origin at
x^{*}
, recall the coordinate vector fields
\partial_{i}=\left.\partial\middle/\partial x^{i}\right.
. Any function
\phi:T_{x^{*}}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
may be identified with a function of
n
variables,
\phi(u)=\phi(u^{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,u^{n})
, for
u\in T_{x^{*}}M
where
u=u^{i}\partial_{i}(x^{*})
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
(x^{i}\,;i=1,\ldots,n)
be normal coordinates on
M
with origin at
x^{*}
. For any smooth function
\phi:T_{x^{*}}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, if
\psi_{\mu}
is given by (
5.49
), then

\partial_{i}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u^{i}}(u)\hskip 10.243pt;\hskip 8.5359pt\partial_{ij}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))=\mu^{-1}\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial u^{i}\partial u^{j}}(u)

(5.68)

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
X^{i}(x)
denote the components of the mean field
X
, with respect to the normal coordinates
(x^{i}\,;i=1,\ldots,n)
. If
d2
holds, then

X^{i}(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.56917ptA^{i}_{j}u^{j}+R^{i}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}u)

(5.69)

where
|R^{i}(u)|=o(\|u\|_{x^{*}})
.

Lemmas
5.5
,
5.6
, and
5.7
will be proved below. Accepting them to be true, recall (
5.50
)

\tilde{Q}_{\mu}\phi(u)=Q_{\mu}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))

Replacing (
5.66
) into the right-hand side gives

\mu^{-1}\left[\tilde{Q}_{\mu}\phi(u)-\phi(u)\right]=\hskip 216.2411pt

X(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))+\frac{\mu}{2}\hskip 0.56917pt[\mathrm{Hess}\,(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})\!:\!T]_{\psi^{-1}(u)}+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{R}_{\psi^{-1}(u)}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)

(5.70)

where
T=\Sigma+X\otimes X
. However, working in normal coordinates,

X(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))=X^{i}(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))\partial_{i}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))

so that, by (
5.68
) and (
5.69
),

X(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))=\left\{A^{i}_{j}u^{j}+\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}R^{i}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}u)\right\}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u^{i}}(u)

Since
\phi
is compactly-supported, this can be written

X(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))=A^{i}_{j}u^{j}\hskip 0.56917pt\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u^{i}}(u)+\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\mu}(u)

(5.71)

where
\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\mu}(u)\rightarrow 0
, uniformly on
T_{x^{*}}M
, as
\mu\rightarrow 0
. For the second term in (
5.70
), using (
1.21
),

[\mathrm{Hess}\,(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})\!:\!T]_{\psi^{-1}(u)}=T^{ij}(\psi^{-1}(u))\left[\partial_{ij}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))-\Gamma^{k}_{ij}(\psi^{-1}(u))\hskip 0.56917pt\partial_{k}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))\right]

so that, by (
5.68
),

[\mathrm{Hess}\,(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})\!:\!T]_{\psi^{-1}(u)}=\mu^{-1}\hskip 0.85355ptT^{ij}(\psi^{-1}(u))\left[\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial u^{i}\partial u^{j}}(u)-\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma^{k}_{ij}(\psi^{-1}(u))\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u^{k}}(u)\right]

where
(\Gamma^{i}_{jk})
denote the Christoffel symbols. Since
\phi
is compactly-supported, this can be written

[\mathrm{Hess}\,(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})\!:\!T]_{\psi^{-1}(u)}=\mu^{-1}\hskip 0.85355ptT^{ij}_{*}\hskip 0.56917pt\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial u^{i}\partial u^{j}}(u)+\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\mu}(u)

where
(T^{ij}_{*})
is the matrix which represents the tensor
T(x^{*})
in normal coordinates, and where
\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\mu}(u)\rightarrow 0
, uniformly on
T_{x^{*}}M
, as
\mu\rightarrow 0
. Since (clearly, from (
5.52
)),
T(x^{*})=\Sigma(x^{*})
, it follows

[\mathrm{Hess}\,(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})\!:\!T]_{\psi^{-1}(u)}=\mu^{-1}\hskip 0.85355pt\Sigma^{ij}_{*}\hskip 0.56917pt\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial u^{i}\partial u^{j}}(u)+\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\mu}(u)

(5.72)

Then, replacing (
5.71
) and (
5.72
) into (
5.70
), and recalling the definition of
\mathcal{L}
from (
5.53
),

\mu^{-1}\left[\tilde{Q}_{\mu}\phi(u)-\phi(u)\right]=\mathcal{L}\phi(u)\,+\,\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\mu}(u)+\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\mu}(u)+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{R}_{\psi^{-1}(u)}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)

(5.73)

To conclude, let
\varepsilon_{\mu}(u)=\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\mu}(u)+\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\mu}(u)+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{R}_{\psi^{-1}(u)}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)
, and recall that
\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\mu}(u)
and

\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\mu}(u)
converge to zero, uniformly on
T_{x^{*}}M
. Moreover, using (
5.3
) and (
5.67
), it is straightforward that
\mathcal{R}_{\psi^{-1}(u)}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)
is bounded on compact subsets of
T_{x^{*}}M
, (by an upper bound which is independent of
\mu
). Therefore,
\varepsilon_{\mu}(u)\rightarrow 0
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
, uniformly on compact subsets of
T_{x^{*}}M
.

Proof of Lemma
5.5
:
the proof will rely on the following variant of Taylor expansion (compare to
[
54
]
, Section 2). Let
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a compactly-supported, smooth function. If
A_{f}\hskip 0.56917pt,B_{f}
are given by (
5.65
),

x\in M
and
\xi\hskip 0.56917pt,\eta\in T_{x}M
, then

f(\mathrm{Exp}_{x}(\xi+\eta))=f(x)+(\xi+\eta)f+\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.56917pt[\mathrm{Hess}\,f\!:\!(\xi+\eta)\otimes(\xi+\eta)]+\mathcal{R}_{f}(x)\hskip 17.64056pt

\text{where }\left|\mathcal{R}_{f}(x)\right|\leq 2A_{f}\hskip 0.56917pt\|\eta\|^{2}_{x}+6B_{f}\hskip 0.56917pt\|\xi\|^{2}_{x}\|\eta\|^{\phantom{2}}_{x}+2B_{f}\hskip 0.56917pt\|\xi\|^{3}_{x}\hskip 84.3623pt

(5.74)

To apply (
5.74
), recall (
5.58
),

Q_{\mu}f(x)=\mathbb{E}\left[f\!\left(\mathrm{Exp}_{x}\!\left(\mu\hskip 0.56917ptX(x)+\mu\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x)\right)\right)\right]

and let
\xi=\mu\hskip 0.56917ptX(x)+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}\leq K\}\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x)
,
\eta=\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}>K\}\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x)
. Taking the expectation of the Taylor expansion in (
5.74
) and using (
5.2
) and (
5.51
), it follows that, as in (
5.66
),

Q_{\mu}f(x)=f(x)+\mu\hskip 0.56917ptXf(x)+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}[\mathrm{Hess}\,f\!:\!\Sigma+X\otimes X]_{x}+\mu^{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathcal{R}_{x}(f,\mu)

where
|\mathcal{R}_{x}(f,\mu)|
is less than or equal to

\begin{array}[]{l}2A_{f}\hskip 0.85355pt\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}>K\}\|e_{y}\|^{2}_{x}\right]+\\[4.26773pt]
6B_{f}\hskip 0.85355pt\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hskip 0.56917ptX(x)+\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}\leq K\}\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x)\|^{2}_{x}\times\|\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}>K\}\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x)\|_{x}\right]+\\[4.26773pt]
2B_{f}\hskip 0.85355pt\mu\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hskip 0.56917ptX(x)+\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}\leq K\}\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x)\|^{3}_{x}\right]\end{array}

Then, to obtain (
5.67
), it is enough to note

\begin{array}[]{l}\|\hskip 0.56917ptX(x)+\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}\leq K\}\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x)\|^{3}_{x}\leq 4\|X\|^{3}_{x}+4K^{3}\\[5.69046pt]
\|\hskip 0.56917ptX(x)+\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}\leq K\}\hskip 0.56917pte_{y}(x)\|^{2}_{x}\leq 2\|X\|^{2}_{x}+2K^{2}\end{array}

which follow from the elementary inequalities
(a+b)^{3}\leq 4a^{3}+4b^{3}
and
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
.

Proof of (
5.74
) :
if
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is smooth and compactly-supported, for
x\in M
and
\zeta\in T_{x}M
,
one has from the second- and third-order Taylor expansions of
f
at
x
, that

f(\mathrm{Exp}_{x}(\zeta))=f(x)+\zeta f+\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.56917pt[\mathrm{Hess}\,f\!:\!\zeta\otimes\zeta]+\mathcal{R}_{f}(x)

where, simultaneously,
|\mathcal{R}_{f}(x)|\leq A_{f}\|\zeta\|^{2}_{x}
and
|\mathcal{R}_{f}(x)|\leq B_{f}\|\zeta\|^{3}_{x}\hskip 5.69046pt
. If
\zeta=\xi+\eta
, then

\begin{array}[]{rl}|\mathcal{R}_{f}(x)|\leq 2A_{f}\|\eta\|^{2}_{x}&\text{if $\|\eta\|_{x}\geq\|\xi\|_{x}$}\\[3.41418pt]
|\mathcal{R}_{f}(x)|\leq 2B_{f}\|\xi\|^{3}_{x}+6B_{f}\|\xi\|^{2}_{x}\|\eta\|_{x}&\text{if $\|\eta\|_{x}<\|\xi\|_{x}$}\end{array}

and (
5.74
) is obtained by adding up these two cases.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
:
let
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function. From the definition of coordinate vector fields
[
53
]
(Page 49),

\partial_{i}f(x)=(f\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{x^{*}})^{\prime}(\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x^{*}}(x))(\partial_{i}(x^{*}))

(5.75)

where the prime denotes the Fréchet derivative. To obtain (
5.68
), set
f=\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}
and
x=\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u)
, so that
f\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{x^{*}}(w)=\phi(\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}w)
(for
w\in T_{x^{*}}M
) and
\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x^{*}}(x)=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}u
. Then, in particular,
(f\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{x^{*}})^{\prime}=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\phi^{\prime}
. Replacing into (
5.75
), it follows that

\partial_{i}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu})(\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u))=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\phi^{\prime}(u)(\partial_{i}(x^{*}))

Now, if
\phi
is identified with a function of
n
variables,
\phi(u)=\phi(u^{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,u^{n})
where
u=u^{i}\partial_{i}(x^{*})
, then
\phi^{\prime}(u)(\partial_{i}(x^{*}))=\partial\phi(u)/\partial u^{i}
. This yields the first identity in (
5.68
). The second identity follows from the first by repeated application.

Proof of Lemma
5.7
:
assume that
d2
holds. Using the same notation as in (
5.52
), consider the Taylor expansion of the coordinate vector fields
\partial_{i}
(see
[
11
]
, Page 90),

\partial_{i}(x)=\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(\partial_{i}(x^{*})+\nabla\partial_{i}(x^{*})\left(\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x^{*}}(x)\right)+o(d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*}))\right)

where
\nabla\partial_{i}(x^{*}):T_{x^{*}}M\rightarrow T_{x^{*}}M
is the covariant derivative of
\partial_{i}
at
x^{*}
. From (
1.7
) and (
1.62
), it is clear that
\nabla\partial_{i}(x^{*})=0
, and therefore

\partial_{i}(x)=\Pi^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(\partial_{i}(x^{*})+o(d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*}))\right)

(5.76)

Take the scalar product of (
5.52
) and (
5.76
). Since parallel transport preserves scalar products,

\langle X\hskip 0.56917pt,\partial_{i}\rangle_{x}=\langle A\left(\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x^{*}}(x)\right),\partial_{i}(x^{*})\rangle_{x^{*}}+o(d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*}))

However,
\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x^{*}}(x)=x^{i}\partial_{i}(x^{*})
, and
\partial_{i}(x^{*})
form an orthonormal basis of
T_{x^{*}}M
. Therefore,

\langle X\hskip 0.56917pt,\partial_{i}\rangle_{x}=A^{i}_{j}x^{j}+o(d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*}))

(5.77)

where
A(\partial_{i}(x^{*}))=A^{k}_{i}\partial^{\phantom{k}}_{k}(x^{*})
. Finally, note that (in normal coordinates), the metric coefficients satisfy

g_{ij}(x)=\delta_{ij}+o(d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*}))

Using these to express the scalar product in (
5.77
), it can be seen that

X^{i}(x)=A^{i}_{j}x^{j}+o(d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*}))

(5.78)

Thus, (
5.69
) follows by putting
x=\psi^{-1}_{\mu}(u)
in (
5.78
). Then,
x^{j}=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{j}
and
d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{x^{*}\hskip 0.56917pt}
.

5.7.4

Proof of Proposition
5.8

To begin, it is helpful to establish tightness of the family
(\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}\,;\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Assume that
e1
,
e2
,
v1
,
v2
,
t2
hold. Then, the family of probability distributions
(\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}\,;\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})
is tight.

Accepting this lemma, let
\tilde{\pi}_{*}
be some limit point of the family
(\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}\,;\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})
at
\mu=0
. By integrating both sides of (
5.54
) with respect to

\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, and recalling that
\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}
is an invariant distribution of
\tilde{Q}_{\mu}
(so the integral of the left-hand side is zero), it follows that

\int_{T_{x^{*}}M}\mathcal{L}\phi(u)\hskip 0.85355pt\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}(du)=-\int_{T_{x^{*}}M}\varepsilon_{\mu}(u)\hskip 0.85355pt\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}(du)

(5.79)

where
\varepsilon_{\mu}(u)=\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\mu}(u)+\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\mu}(u)+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{R}_{\psi^{-1}(u)}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)
, in the notation of (
5.70
), (
5.71
) and (
5.72
), from the proof of Proposition
5.7
. Since both
\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 1}_{\mu}(u)
and

\varepsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\mu}(u)
converge to zero as
\mu\rightarrow 0
, uniformly on
T_{x^{*}}M
, it follows from (
5.79
) that,

\left|\int_{T_{x^{*}}M}\mathcal{L}\phi(u)\hskip 0.85355pt\tilde{\pi}_{*}(du)\right|\leq\limsup_{\mu\rightarrow 0}\int_{T_{x^{*}}M}\,\mu\left|\mathcal{R}_{\psi^{-1}(u)}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)\right|\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}(du)

Since
\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}
is the image of
\pi_{\mu}
under
\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, this is the same as

\left|\int_{T_{x^{*}}M}\mathcal{L}\phi(u)\hskip 0.85355pt\tilde{\pi}_{*}(du)\right|\leq\limsup_{\mu\rightarrow 0}\int_{M}\,\mu\left|\mathcal{R}_{x}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)\right|{\pi}_{\mu}(dx)

(5.80)

To bound the right-hand side, put
f=\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}
in (
5.67
). If
\bar{f}=\phi\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{x^{*}}
, then
\bar{f}
is compactly-supported and smooth. Moreover, applying the chain rule, it follows from (
5.65
) that
A_{f}=\mu^{-1}\!A_{\bar{f}}
and
B_{f}=\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}B_{\bar{f}\hskip 0.85355pt}
. Therefore, by (
5.67
),

\left|\mathcal{R}_{x}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)\right|\leq 2\mu^{-1}\!A_{\bar{f}}\hskip 1.13791pt\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}>K\}\|e_{y}\|^{2}_{x}\right]+\hskip 227.62204pt

\phantom{abcd}6\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}B_{\bar{f}}\hskip 0.85355pt(2\|X\|^{2}_{x}+2K^{2})\hskip 0.85355pt\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}>K\}\|e_{y}\|_{x}\right]+2\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}B_{\bar{f}}\hskip 0.85355pt(4\|X\|^{3}_{x}+4K^{3})

(5.81)

Now, since
t3
holds, it follows from (
5.55
) that

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}>K\}\|e_{y}\|^{2}_{x}\right]\leq K^{-\alpha}\hskip 0.56917pt(\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\hskip 0.56917ptV(x))

(5.82)

Moreover, by (
5.3
) (assuming that
K>1
),

(2\|X\|^{2}_{x}+2K^{2})\hskip 0.85355pt\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}\{\|e_{y}\|_{x}>K\}\|e_{y}\|_{x}\right]\leq(2\|X\|^{2}_{x}+2K^{2})(\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\hskip 0.56917pt\|X\|^{2}_{x})

(5.83)

Then, it follows from (
5.81
), (
5.82
) and (
5.83
) that

\begin{array}[]{l}\mu\left|\mathcal{R}_{x}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)\right|\leq\\[5.69046pt]
2A_{\bar{f}}\hskip 1.13791ptK^{-\alpha}\hskip 0.56917pt(\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\hskip 0.56917ptV(x))\,+6\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}B_{\bar{f}}(2\|X\|^{2}_{x}+2K^{2})(\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\hskip 0.56917pt\|X\|^{2}_{x})+2\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}B_{\bar{f}}\hskip 0.85355pt(4\|X\|^{3}_{x}+4K^{3})\end{array}

Integrate this inequality with respect to
\pi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, and recall from Proposition
5.6
that
\pi_{\mu}
converges weakly to
\delta_{x^{*}}
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
. It follows that,

\limsup_{\mu\rightarrow 0}\int_{M}\,\mu\left|\mathcal{R}_{x}(\phi\circ\psi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt,\mu)\right|{\pi}_{\mu}(dx)\leq 2A_{\bar{f}}\hskip 1.13791ptK^{-\alpha}\hskip 0.56917pt(\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\hskip 0.56917ptV(x^{*}))

However, since
K
can be chosen arbitrarily large, and
\alpha>0
, the limit superior is equal to zero, and (
5.80
) becomes

\int_{T_{x^{*}}M}\mathcal{L}\phi(u)\hskip 0.85355pt\tilde{\pi}_{*}(du)=0

This means that
\tilde{\pi}_{*}
is an invariant distribution of the generator
\mathcal{L}
, and therefore
\tilde{\pi}_{*}=\mathrm{N}(0,V)
, as required.

Proof of Lemma
5.8
:
by Proposition
5.5
,
e1
,
e2
,
v1
,
v2
ensure that the chain
(x_{t})
has a unique invariant distribution
\pi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, whenever
\mu\leq c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
. Then, the chain
(u_{t})
has a unique invariant distribution
\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
. According to (
5.49
), this is
\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}(A)=\pi_{\mu}(\mathrm{Exp}_{x^{*}}(\mu^{1/2}A))
, for any measurable
A\subset T_{x^{*}}M
. The same
e1
,
e2
,
v1
,
v2
also imply (
5.63
), in the proof of Proposition (
5.6
). Now, for
u\in T_{x^{*}}M
, let
x=\mathrm{Exp}_{x^{*}}(\mu^{1/2}u)
, and note that
\|u\|_{x^{*}}>r
if and only if
d(x\hskip 0.56917pt,x^{*})>\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}r
. It then follows from Assumption
t2
that

\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}(\|u\|_{x^{*}}>r)\leq\pi_{\mu}(V>v(\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}r))

so, using Markov’s inequality and (
5.63
),

\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}(\|u\|_{x^{*}}>r)\leq 2(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}/\lambda)\left(\mu\middle/v(\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}r)\right)

To conclude, let
\bar{\mu}=c\hskip 0.56917pt(2\ell(1+\sigma^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}))^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
. By
t2
,

\left.\mu\middle/v(\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}r)\right.\leq\left.\bar{\mu}\middle/v(\bar{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}r)\right.
. Therefore,

\tilde{\pi}_{\mu}(\|u\|_{x^{*}}>r)\leq 2(\ell\hskip 0.56917pt\sigma^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}/\lambda)\left(\bar{\mu}\middle/v(\bar{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}r)\right)

However (again by
t2
), the right-hand side of this inequality is independent of
\mu
, and goes to zero as
r\rightarrow\infty
. This is equivalent to the required tightness.

5.8

Riemannian AR(1)

Let
M
be a Hadamard manifold, and
P
a probability distribution on
M
, which has a strictly positive probability density, with respect to Riemannian volume. Then, let
(y_{t}\,;t=1,2,\ldots)
be independent samples from
P
, and
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
be a point in
M
. Consider the stochastic update rule, starting from
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
at
t=0
,

x_{t+1}=x_{t}\,\#_{\scriptscriptstyle\mu}\,y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where $\mu\in(0,1)$}

(5.84)

where the notation is that of (
4.4
) from
4.1
. This will be called a Riemannian AR(1) model, since each new
x_{t+1}
is a geodesic convex combination of the old
x_{t}
and of the new sample
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1\hskip 0.56917pt}
. If
M
is a Euclidean space,
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then (
5.84
) reads
x_{t+1}=(1-\mu)\hskip 0.56917ptx_{t}+\mu\hskip 0.56917pty_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1\hskip 0.56917pt}
, which is a first-order auto-regressive model (whence the name AR(1)).

The update rule (
5.84
) may be viewed as a constant-step-size exponential scheme, of the form (
5.47
). Specifically, (
5.84
) is equivalent to

x_{t+1}=\mathrm{Exp}_{x_{t}}\!\left(\mu\hskip 0.56917ptX_{y_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.56917ptt+1}}(x_{t})\right)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }X_{y}(x)=\mathrm{Exp}^{-1}_{x}(y)

(5.85)

which defines a time-homogeneous Markov chain
(x_{t})
with values in
M
.

One is tempted to apply the results of
5.6
(
e.g.
on geometric ergodicity), directly to the scheme (
5.85
). However, some of the assumptions in
5.6
(especially
e1
), turn out to be quite unnatural. Fortunately, it is possible to proceed along a different path, which only requires the existence of second-order moments. Specifically, it is merely required that

\mathcal{E}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.85355pt\int_{M}\,d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)\hskip 0.85355ptP(dy)<\,\infty

(5.86)

for some (and therefore all)
x\in M
. As discussed in
2.2.3
, (
5.86
) guarantees existence and uniqueness of the Riemannian barycentre
x^{*}
of
P
. This is enough for the following proposition.

Proposition 5.9
.

Consider the Riemannian AR(1) model (
5.84
) (or (
5.85
)), on a Hadamard manifold
M
. If (
5.86
) is verified, then
the Markov chain
(x_{t})
is geometrically ergodic, with a unique invariant distribution
\pi_{\mu}
. Moreover,
\pi_{\mu}\,\Rightarrow\,\delta_{x^{*}}
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
.

The proof of this proposition begins like that of Proposition
5.5
, by noting that the Markov chain
(x_{t})
is Feller and
|\mathrm{vol}|
-irreducible and aperiodic. Indeed, since
X_{y}(x)
is given by (
5.85
), and since
P
has a strictly positive probability density with respect to
|\mathrm{vol}|
, it follows that Assumption
e2
holds. Therefore, it is possible to argue exactly as in the proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Now, let
V(x)=d^{\hskip 0.56917pt2}(x^{*},x)/2
. To prove that the chain
(x_{t})
is geometrically ergodic, it is enough to obtain the inequality

Q_{\mu}V(x)\leq(1-\mu)^{2}\hskip 0.56917ptV(x)+\mu^{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{E}(x^{*})

(5.87)

which is similar to (
5.56
) of Lemma
5.4
. This can then be used, exactly as in the proof of Proposition
5.5
, based on
[
51
]
(Theorem 16.0.1).

Proof of (
5.87
) :
for any
x\in M
, note from (
5.84
) that

Q_{\mu}V(x)=\mathbb{E}\left[V(x\,\#_{\scriptscriptstyle\mu}\,y)\right]

(5.88)

where
y
denotes a random variable with distribution
P
. Recall from
1.7.4
that
V(x)
is
1/2
-strongly convex. Therefore, by (
1.79
),

V(x\,\#_{\scriptscriptstyle\mu}\,y)\leq(1-\mu)V(x)+\mu V(y)-\mu(1-\mu)d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)/2

taking expectations, this yields,

Q_{\mu}V(x)\leq(1-\mu)V(x)+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{E}(x^{*})-\mu(1-\mu)\mathcal{E}(x)

(5.89)

after using the fact that
\mathcal{E}(x)=\mathbb{E}[d^{\hskip 0.85355pt2}(x\hskip 0.56917pt,y)/2]
for any
x\in M
, which is clear from (
5.86
). Now, recall from
2.2.3
that
\mathcal{E}
is
1/2
-strongly convex. Therefore, by (
4.34
)

\mathcal{E}(x)\geq\mathcal{E}(x^{*})+d^{\hskip 0.56917pt2}(x^{*},x)/2

(5.90)

where the right-hand side is just
\mathcal{E}(x^{*})+V(x)
. Thus, replacing (
5.90
) into (
5.89
), one has

Q_{\mu}V(x)\leq(1-\mu)V(x)+\mu\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{E}(x^{*})-\mu(1-\mu)V(x)-\mu(1-\mu)\mathcal{E}(x^{*})

which immediately yields (
5.87
).

Geometric ergodicity ensures the chain
(x_{t})
has a unique invariant distribution
\pi_{\mu}
. To prove that
\pi_{\mu}\,\Rightarrow\,\delta_{x^{*}}
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
, it is possible to argue as in the proof of Proposition
5.6
. Precisely, integrating both sides of (
5.87
) with respect to
\pi_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, it follows that

\int_{M}Q_{\mu}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)\leq(1-\mu)^{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\int_{M}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)+\mu^{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{E}(x^{*})

Since
\pi_{\mu}
is an invariant distribution of the transition kernel
Q_{\mu}\hskip 0.56917pt
, this means

\int_{M}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)\leq(1-\mu)^{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\int_{M}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)+\mu^{2}\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcal{E}(x^{*})

In other words,

\int_{M}V(x)\hskip 0.56917pt\pi_{\mu}(dx)\leq\mathcal{E}(x^{*})\mu/(2-\mu)

(5.91)

Since
\mu/(2-\mu)\leq\mu
for
\mu\leq 1
, (
5.91
) can be used like (
5.63
) in the proof of Proposition
5.6
. In this way,
\pi_{\mu}\,\Rightarrow\,\delta_{x^{*}}
follows by noting that
V
, defined by
V(x)=d^{\hskip 0.56917pt2}(x^{*},x)/2
, has compact sublevel sets, by the Hopf-Rinow theorem and the fact that
M
is complete (see
[
11
]
), and that
V(x)=0
if and only if
x=x^{*}
.

Remark :
thanks to Proposition
5.9
, it is now possible to prove that a central limit theorem, identical to Proposition
5.8
, holds for the Riemannian AR(1) model (
5.84
). This only requires the additional condition (
5.55
).

Chapter 6

Open problems

While working on this thesis, there are several problems which I found very interesting, and important, but could not solve, or even attack in a meaningful way. I would therefore like to close the thesis with a list of these problems, in the hope that they will attract the attention of more people (not just myself).

In Chapter
2
:
the conclusion of Lemma
2.1
only holds for compact Riemannian symmetric spaces which are simply connected. Therefore, the subsequent Propositions
2.2
and
2.3
are restricted to this simply connected case. The problem is to describe, at least partially, what happens for compact Riemannian symmetric spaces which are not simply connected. It would be particularly interesting to give counterexamples to either one of Propositions
2.2
and
2.3
, in the non-simply-connected case.

In Chapter
3
:
formula (
3.7
) gives the normalising factor
Z(\sigma)
for a Gaussian distribution on a symmetric space
M
, which belongs to the non-compact case. When
M
is the space of
N\times N
Hermitian positive-definite matrices, (
3.35
) and (
3.42
) provide a closed form expression (valid for any
N
), and an asymptotic expression (valid for large
N
), of the multiple integral in (
3.7
).
The problem is to find similar expressions of this integral, for other symmetric spaces. It should be easiest to first deal with the spaces of
N\times N
real or quaternion positive-definite matrices, and then move on to other spaces, such as the Siegel domaine (Example 3, in
3.3
).
In Chapter
4
:
the problem is to prove or disprove the conjecture, mentioned in
4.2
. Namely, that the MAP and MMS Bayesian estimators are equal, for Gaussian distributions on a space of constant negative curvature.

In Chapter
4
:
as mentioned in
4.4
, I have never met with a function
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
(
M
a non-Euclidean Hadamard manifold), which is strongly convex, and also has a bounded Hessian. The problem is to construct a function
f
with these properties, or to show this is not possible. Another problem, which is quite important for convex optimisation, is to show that a function
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, which is convex and has a bounded Hessian, has co-coercive gradient, in the sense of
[
57
]
(Theorem 2.1.5, property (2.1.11)).

In Chapter
5
:
to state in clear and general terms the functional central limit theorem, which follows from Proposition
5.7
, and also to derive a similar functional central limit theorem for decreasing-step-size schemes. These can be used in studying the behavior of stochastic approximation schemes, in the presence of unstable critical points (only stable critical points were considered, in the above).

In Chapter
5
:
to generalise Proposition
5.8
to the case where
M
is not a Hadamard manifold. I believe that, in this case, the asymptotic form of the invariant distribution will no longer be multivariate normal (roughly, the scheme can always “jump across” the cut locus of a stable critical point).
